| BUILT-IN SELF-TEST (22 Oct 2026; step 3 of Alan's order) | `tools/hexn_tang_bist_v1.py` (+ `tools/hexn_bist_sets_v1.py`, `tools/hexn_tang_bist_run_v1.sh`): one command that loads nothing new onto the chip; it uses the read-back commands every bitstream already has (PEEK counters, DUMP weights and thresholds, DUMPSUMS, DIVTEST, RUN). Eight fixed training sets per run (16 samples per class), each checked stage by stage: COUNT -> counters, SETW -> weights, ACCUM -> sums, FINISH -> thresholds, RUN -> answers and all burst counts; then 24 divider tests. The sets are chosen so that the battery reaches counters exactly at the weight cutoff and one either side, counters at the +-31 limit, accumulator saturation, a threshold of 0 and threshold sums that land exactly on a rounding tie of both parities (7 detectors: at T 15, below T 8, at -T 6, above -T 21, 44 positive and 168 negative saturations, ties 2 even and 4 odd, threshold 0 once; 19 detectors: 33 / 32 / 30 / 23, ties 1 and 1, threshold 0 27 times). Not reached: a threshold clamped at the accumulator maximum. Checked against a Python stand-in (pass), and with an injected fault of the kind the board had (every +1 weight missing after SETW) it fails and names the stage (SETW -> weights, 53 of 126 weights wrong). Not yet run on the board. Known-value loading (writing a counter directly) was not added: every counter value is reachable with crafted COUNT samples, so no hardware change is needed. |
| NO-FEEDBACK 19 DETECTORS AND THE SELF-TEST ON THE BOARD (22 Oct 2026) | hex19, 18 wires, `-noalu`, placement seed 2: 15730 LUT4 of 20736 (75.9%), 2861 flip-flops, 39.4 MHz post-route (board clock 27); bitstream `hexn_nofb_w18_hex19_noalu.fs`. Built-in self-test (`tools/hexn_tang_bist_run_v1.sh ... --cells 19 --wires 18 --aw 7`) on the real board: BIST PASS, all 8 sets, every stage ok (342 counters, 342 weights, 19 sums, 19 thresholds, 76 test runs with all burst counts per set; 24 of 24 divider tests). Battery coverage at 19 detectors: counters at T 33, below T 32, at -T 30, above -T 23; positive saturation 57, negative 709; rounding ties 1 even and 1 odd; threshold 0 27 times; not reached: a threshold clamped at the accumulator maximum. First board run of the self-test. |
| DIRECTION, NOT A RESULT: DETECTOR BANK AS A CLEANING FRONT END FOR THE FULL CELL (Alan, 22 Oct 2026) | The full cell (feedback, leaky accumulator) had trouble taking in input (section 4a: frozen cluster 0.417 on clean data, 0.27 at noise 0.25, against about 0.68 for four plain detectors). Idea: the counted detector bank, which recognises noisy patterns and is proven on the board, sits in front as the cleaner and tokeniser, like early vision before a decision stage; its output (the K burst counts, 0 to 7 clocks each, so graded confidence is kept, not only the winner) goes to the cell network. The detector chip is the same hex cell with the feedback half dropped. The bank can also be taught invariances (flipped, rotated, dropout copies) simply by showing them in training. The cell network's own job would then be sequences and context of tokens. Scope: this is a design direction under test; nothing in it is a measured result yet. |
| FIRST TOKEN TEST ON THE CLUSTER (22 Oct 2026; `tools/hexn_cluster_v21.py`, clone of v20 with `--tokens`; 3 seeds, 600 tries, frozen model, test noise 0 and 0.05) | The cluster was fed clean tokens instead of 12-wire patterns: one wire per class 0.00; a block of 3 wires per class 0.25, 0.25, 0.00; 6 wires per class 0.25, 0.25, 0.00 (0.25 is chance: one class named for everything; 0.00: all cells silent). Same cluster on raw 12-wire patterns at the same budget: 0.25 and 0.25 on seeds 0 and 1 (seed 2 not finished when written). So at this budget the cluster learns neither input; tokens are neither better nor worse. The budget is too small to separate them (the design record's best cluster used about 2,000 tries and reached about 0.4). Not yet tested: graded burst counts as input, a task the cluster can learn, more tries. |
| ONE FULL CELL NEXT TO THE 19 DETECTORS (22 Oct 2026; sizing from synthesis only, not placed and routed) | `hexn_cell_v2` alone, AW 7, `synth_gowin -noalu`: 837 LUT4 (2686 cells in all; with the carry-chain cells it is 129 LUT4 + 274 ALU, which this design avoids). The hex19 no-feedback build leaves 20736 - 15730 = 5006 slots, so one cell fits with large margin and a few more probably would; to be measured on a real build. The cell has 24 receive lanes (6 faces x 4), the bank 19 detector outputs, so the 19 outputs map onto 19 of the cell's lanes. To work out first: the detectors send their burst in the second window of a run, the cell works in forward and feedback phases, so the timing between them has to be lined up in simulation before any build. The Kintex card (XC7K480T per the manual: 298600 LUT6) is the place for a real cluster behind the 19 detectors. |
| DETECTORS -> GLUE BUFFER -> ONE FULL CELL, INTERFACE TEST IN SIMULATION (22 Oct 2026; Alan's buffer idea: absorb the first burst, drip it, fire the second) | New files: `rtl/hexn_glue_v1.v` (per detector lane a 3-bit capture counter that counts the clocks the detector's tx is high only while the capture window is open, then replays that many clocks into the cell starting the clock after the play pulse, keeping the captured value), `rtl/hexn_front_v1.v` (simulation top: 19 `hexn_det_v3` with weights and thresholds given directly, glue, one `hexn_cell_v2`; five windows of 10 clocks: detectors accumulate, detectors burst and the glue captures, the cell's forward phase with replay, the cell's feedback phase, the cell's forward phase with its output burst counted), `rtl/tb_hexn_front_v1.v`, `tools/hexn_front_check_v1.py`. The check generates random trials (random weights, thresholds, cell lane settings, cell threshold), runs them back to back WITHOUT a reset, and compares three things with models written from the rules / the cell's equations: captured burst lengths, the cell's forward total (a lane's burst of n clocks adds +-1 on clocks 1..n, total clamped at 0 and 127 each clock), and the cell's output burst count (min(total - threshold, 6) + 1 once the total reaches the cell threshold). Result: 3000 trials (cell fired in 1986): 0 wrong in all three; 300 trials with another seed: 0 wrong. Negative control: opening the capture window to the first window as well makes all 300 trials wrong (captured lengths 300, cell totals 255, cell outputs 175), which confirms that the detector's stale burst in the first window of a run (it replays its previous result until the next ph_start) is real and that the glue's gating absorbs it. Findings that shape the cell side: a single cell has no feedback source, so its default output rule (needs feedback agreement) cannot fire; the output lane uses code 1 (fires when the forward total reaches the threshold). Not yet done: the trained bank in front (lattice, trainer) instead of directly loaded weights, a serial command to load the cell's lane settings, a board build; the cell's settings and threshold are inputs here, not trained. |
| CELL MODE (detectors -> glue buffer -> one full cell) | Bank v12, lattice generator v11, top v9 (commands LOADCELL 0x0E and RUNCELL 0x0F), build script v13. The detector bank feeds hexn_glue_v1 (captures the first window's burst lengths, replays them) and one hexn_cell_v2 with random lane settings and one threshold. The cell has no feedback source, so it fires on a plain forward-total threshold (output code 1). Measured in simulation: bank level hex7 600 trials and hex19 400 trials, 0 wrong in both modes; top level through the serial commands hex7 120 trials and hex19 60 trials, 0 wrong (answer, counts, captured lengths, cell total and cell output all compared with the rules and a clock-by-clock cell model; the cell fired in about 80 percent of the hex7 cell trials and about half of the hex19 ones). Tang Nano 20K build (noalu, seed 1) of hex7 with the cell: 10285 of 20736 LUT slots (49.6 percent), 1321 flip-flops, 55.4 MHz against the 27 MHz board clock. Hex19 with the cell (v14 build, noalu, seed 2): 16681 of 20736 LUT slots (80.4 percent), 3070 flip-flops, 97.9 MHz against the 27 MHz board clock; fits the Tang with about 4000 slots free. BOARD RESULT of the first (v13) hex7 cell build, run by Alan 22 Oct: self-test PASS (all 8 sets, DIVTEST ok); cell test: trained settings 0 of 7 differ, RUN 30 of 30 right, captured burst lengths all right, cell output burst all right, but the cell forward total readback was wrong in 6 of 30 trials. Cause: bank v12 read the cell register by hierarchical name (fcell.fwd_acc); iverilog accepts that, yosys leaves it undriven (its log said 'used but has no driver') so the board read 0. My miss: I had not read that warning. Fix (clones, originals untouched): cell v3 with port fwd_o, bank v13, lattice generator v12 (modules _v12), build v14 (stops if the synth log has 'has no driver'), check v2. Simulation after the fix: hex7 150 bank-level and 60 top-level trials, 0 wrong including the forward total. The v14 hex7 build (10264 slots, 49.5 percent, 55.5 MHz) is sent to Alan; BOARD RESULT of the v14 hex7 build (Alan, 22 Oct, 30 cell trials plus 30 RUN trials on the real Tang): trained settings 0 of 7 differ, RUN wrong 0, captured burst lengths wrong 0, cell forward total wrong 0, cell output burst wrong 0 (cell fired in 14): CELL TEST PASS. This is one cell with random lane settings and a plain threshold (no feedback, no leaky accumulator), 30 trials; the self-test (BIST) was run on the v13 build only, not re-run on v14. BOARD RESULT of the v14 hex19 build (Alan, 22 Oct): self-test PASS (all 8 sets, 342 counters and weights per set, DIVTEST ok); cell test with the 19-class script docs/hex-n/frontend/example_random19_script_v1.txt (generated by a tool, not the Explorer page): trained settings 0 of 19 differ, RUN wrong 0, captured lengths wrong 0, cell forward total wrong 0, cell output burst wrong 0 (cell fired in 21 of 30): CELL TEST PASS. Same scope limits as hex7: one cell, plain threshold, 30 trials. Host test tools/hexn_tang_cell_v1.py and wrapper tools/hexn_tang_cell_run_v1.sh pass against a Python stand-in only (that checks the serial framing, not the hardware). NOT yet run on the board. Not built: feedback and leaky accumulator in the cell, a cluster of cells. | Hex7 and hex19 cell mode pass on the board (30 cell trials each, one cell, plain threshold). |
| KINTEX CARD STATUS (corrected 22 Oct; read from the main branch ledger, read-only, nothing merged): the Kintex card (YPCB-00338, xc7k480tffg1156-2) works for loading and JTAG reads, though its PCIe interface died in June (fpga/archive/kintex7_xc7k480t/README.md). Card-measured on the UniCell side: the unchanged Tang tree design ran on it (#1237); data read out over JTAG through a BSCANE2 user register, OpenOCD, cable ft232 (#1241); freeze, save and load of cell state matched simulation word for word (#1245 to #1253); the Flex-Sub assembler's own Xilinx output built with openXC7 and ran at 50 MHz clock with 282 MHz timing margin (#1254); carrier and flex tests are logged up to #1304. Build flow: openXC7 (nextpnr-xilinx) and `make program JTAG_LINK="-c ft232"` into SRAM. For Hex-N this means: the Tang serial link has no direct equivalent, so a Kintex Hex-N top needs a JTAG command and readback path (a BSCANE2 register carrying the same command bytes and answers); nothing of that is built yet. The Hex-N RTL has only been synthesized for the Gowin target and, for sizing, with yosys for Xilinx 7-series; it has not been through openXC7. | Noted, not built. |
| SEVERAL CELLS BEHIND ONE GLUE BUFFER (22 Oct): rtl/hexn_bank_v14.v (parameter NC cells, each with its own lane settings and threshold, all hearing the same replayed tokens), lattice generator v13 (modules _v13), rtl/tb_hexn_cellbank_v2.v, tools/hexn_cellbank_check_v3.py. Simulation against the rules and the cell model: hex7 with 1 cell (100 trials), hex7 with 3 cells (200 trials), hex19 with 4 cells (120 trials): 0 wrong in the detector part, the captured lengths and every cell's forward total and output burst (random lane settings and thresholds). Size, synthesis only (yosys synth_gowin -noalu on the hex7 lattice module alone, no UART): 3523 LUT4 with 1 cell, 4120 with 3 cells, 3999 with 6 cells. The numbers are NOT monotonic, so the mapper's noise at this size is at least about 400 LUT4 and the per-cell cost (about 300 from 1 to 3 cells) cannot be read from them; cell counts do rise (11378, 12998, 13767). A placed-and-routed build is the real test. Not tested: whether different input classes give different, useful cell outputs (settings here are random); not run on the board; no serial commands for more than one cell (board top v9 drives one). | Simulation only. |
| DO THE CELL TOTALS KEEP THE CLASS INFORMATION? (22 Oct; model only, no RTL: tools/hexn_cells_separability_v1.py) 7-class example script, input noise 15 percent on every sample, NC cells with random lane settings behind the detectors, a nearest-centroid reader on the cells' forward totals (centroids learned from noisy copies of the training samples, so the reader has its own training that the hardware does not have), 4 random cell sets per row. Detector answer alone (unique strict maximum, a tie counts as wrong): 0.47 to 0.48. Cell totals: 1 cell 0.237, 2 cells 0.296, 3 cells 0.378, 4 cells 0.442, 6 cells 0.498, 8 cells 0.521, 12 cells 0.536. Reading: a handful of random cells keeps the class information (it is about equal to the detector answer at 6 cells); a single cell does not. NOT a like-for-like comparison (the reader is trained, the detector answer ignores near-ties), and random settings were not tuned, so this says the information survives, not that the cells improve on the detectors. Accuracy at this noise is low in both columns. Not run in RTL or on the board. | Model only. |
| LEAKY ACCUMULATOR, MODEL ONLY (22 Oct; tools/hexn_leak_model_v1.py static scene, hexn_leak_model_v2.py scene that switches; answers open question 2 of section 5 in part). The cell as built (hexn_cell_v2/v3) CLEARS its forward total at the start of every window. Model: the trained 7-class bank gives each window's burst lengths; one accumulator per class counts that class's detector burst (+1 per clock, clamp 0..127); at each window start the old total is changed by a leak rule; the answer is the unique highest total (a tie is wrong). Each window is a fresh noisy copy of the same scene. STATIC SCENE, noise 15 percent, correct after 1/2/4/8 windows: clear 0.47/0.48/0.50/0.48 (never improves); no leak 0.47/0.66/0.81/0.88; subtract 1 0.47/0.66/0.80/0.87; halve 0.47/0.66/0.74/0.77. At noise 25 percent: clear 0.17 to 0.25; no leak 0.17/0.35/0.54/0.69. SCENE SWITCHES from class A (8 windows) to class B (8 windows), noise 15 percent, correct for B at 1/2/4/8 windows after the switch (and for A at the last window before it): clear 0.48/0.50/0.46/0.43 (A 0.51); no leak 0.05/0.10/0.23/0.46 (A 0.89, stuck on the old class); subtract 1 0.11/0.19/0.35/0.60 (A 0.88); halve 0.44/0.61/0.75/0.76 (A 0.78); a-(a>>2) 0.17/0.37/0.68/0.82 (A 0.88); a-(a>>3) 0.05/0.16/0.46/0.73 (A 0.89). At noise 25 percent the order is the same with lower values (a-(a>>2): A 0.58, B after 8 windows about 0.57). Reading: carrying the total between windows is what lets the cell use several looks (clear never gains from them); how fast it leaks trades steadiness on a held scene against how quickly it follows a changed one. A leak of a minus a shifted copy of a (shift 1 = halve, shift 2 = three quarters, shift 3 = seven eighths) is cheap in hardware and gave the best balance at shift 2 in these runs; shift 1 recovers fastest. Limits: one lane per class cell; the same detector bank and 7 classes; frames are independent noisy copies of one stored scene; window length, the two-phase window and the feedback total of the real cell are not modelled; values below 2^shift never decay with this rule. NOT in RTL, NOT on a board. | Model only. |
| LEAKY CELL IN RTL, SIMULATION (22 Oct): rtl/hexn_cell_v4.v (clone of v3; input leak_sh: at the start of a forward window the total a becomes a - (a >> leak_sh); 0 = cleared = v3, 7 = kept), rtl/hexn_bank_v15.v (leak applied once per run at the first cell window, kept at the second forward window; one setting for all cells), lattice generator v14 (modules _v14), rtl/tb_hexn_cellbank_v3.v, tools/hexn_cellbank_check_v4.py (random cell settings, per-trial leak setting, totals carried from run to run, compared with a clock-by-clock Python model that starts from the carried total): hex7 with 3 cells, leak always 0, 120 trials: 0 wrong (same as the old cell); random leak 0..7, 300 trials: 0 wrong in the detector part, in every cell total (450) and every cell output, with 291 totals carried under a nonzero setting. SCENE EXPERIMENT THROUGH THE RTL (tools/hexn_leak_rtl_v1.py): hex7 bank trained on the 7-class example script, 7 cells one-hot on the 7 detectors (class totals), 84 episodes per setting, noise 15 percent, scene A 8 windows then scene B 8 windows. The RTL class totals equal the Python chain in all 6720 frames. Correct for the true scene, last window of A, then 1/2/4/8 windows after the switch: clear 0.51, then 0.50/0.52/0.52/0.52; halve 0.79, 0.46/0.69/0.74/0.75; a-(a>>2) 0.80, 0.19/0.46/0.69/0.80; a-(a>>3) 0.82, 0.05/0.18/0.50/0.75; keep 0.82, 0.04/0.12/0.27/0.54. This agrees with the model of the previous row (84 episodes is a small sample: a few points of noise either way). Not done: the cost of the leak in logic (the shift and subtract) is not measured; the leak setting is not in the board top (no serial command); not built for the Tang or run on the board; the real cell's feedback total is still unused; one leak setting is shared by all cells. | Simulation only. |
| FEEDBACK ROLES, CONCEPT-LEVEL MODEL (22 Oct; tools/hexn_feedback_model_v1.py; not gate-accurate to the cell's feedback phase, not RTL). Setting: the leaky class accumulators (leak a-(a>>2)) on the 7-class bank, static scene and the scene that switches, as in the leak model. Rules tried on top of the plain 'add the burst' base: competition (each class also loses a share of the strongest rival's burst: wta1 = half, wta2 = quarter) and belief reinforcement (the class that led after the previous window gets +1 or +2). Noise 15 percent, correct after 8 static windows: base 0.862, wta1 0.848, wta2 0.857, belief1 0.857, belief2 0.857, both 0.867. After a switch, correct 1/2/4 windows later: base 0.13/0.35/0.66; wta1 0.22/0.43/0.73; wta2 0.16/0.39/0.69; belief1 0.05/0.20/0.54; belief2 0.04/0.11/0.36. Noise 25 percent: static after 8 windows base 0.638, belief2 0.669, wta1 0.612; after a switch wta1 is again a little faster than base (0.17/0.28/0.43 against 0.14/0.23/0.40), belief again slower. Reading: with the leak in place, neither feedback role changes the result much. Competition follows a change a few points faster and gives up a little on a held scene; belief reinforcement holds a scene a little better at high noise and follows a change much slower. Neither is a clear win; this task is already handled by the leak. Feedback in the real cell is an AGREEMENT GATE for routing (a lower cell passes its token only when the cell above reached its threshold); that role is not tested here and needs a two-layer layout, which is the next question if feedback is pursued. Decision for now: do not add feedback hardware for the class-accumulator job; the cell's existing feedback phase and gate stay as built. | Model only. |
| LEAK SETTING ON THE BOARD, DESIGN ONLY (22 Oct; nothing built). Board top v10 (clone of v9) would add: 0x10 SETLEAK [s] (s 0..7, one byte, default 0 = clear = the behaviour tested on the board on 22 Oct) feeding hexn_bank_v15 leak_sh; NC cells in the bank (parameter NC, hex7 first with NC = 7, one cell per class) with 0x11 LOADCELLN [n, thr, 5 lane bytes] for cell n (0x0E keeps loading cell 0); RUNCELL answering the answer/burst data as now plus, per cell, its forward total and output burst. Cell totals persist between RUNCELL commands, so the host sends the frames of a scene one after the other with s set; the first frame of a scene is sent with s = 0 (clear), as in the simulation. Host tool: a clone of tools/hexn_tang_cell_v1.py that plays episodes (scene A then scene B) and scores the answer from the cell totals, checking each total against the Python chain used in tools/hexn_leak_rtl_v1.py. Open before building: logic cost of 7 cells plus the leak on hex7 (the synthesis counts were too noisy; needs a placed build), whether the leak shift and subtract fit the clock, and serial answer length (about 2 + 2 x cells + lanes bytes). | Design only. |
| FEEDBACK AGREEMENT GATE, MECHANISM CHECK WITH TWO CELL MODELS (22 Oct; tools/hexn_gate_model_v1.py, Python twin of the cell, no RTL). Cell A takes a token and passes its output only if its total reached the threshold AND the cell above it (B) sent feedback (output code 0, 'ALL THREE'). Findings: (1) DEADLOCK: if A's only output lane is gated by ALL THREE and the feedback comes only from the cell that output feeds, nothing is ever sent (feedback exists only after the output was sent, the output only after feedback). Fix: A needs two output routes, an UNGATED one (code 1) that feeds B and carries B's feedback back, and the GATED one (code 0) that is the real output. (2) With that wiring the gate behaves as an agreement gate: with B's threshold low (2) the gated output starts in the 4th window of a held token (window 1 A forward, 2 B receives, 3 B's feedback arrives, 4 A's gated output); with B's threshold high (40) A reaches its own threshold in every window but the gated output never opens. (3) The window-start pulse (ph_start) must be given at the start of the FEEDBACK phase as well: the burst counter restarts only on that pulse and saturates at 7, so without it a cell sends nothing in the feedback phase. Bank v15 (and v12 to v14) pulse it only at the start of the two forward windows (cph_start), so FEEDBACK IS NOT EXERCISED IN THE CURRENT BANK; the cell-mode tests so far (simulation and board) never used the feedback phase's transmit side. The cell model and the RTL cell were not compared in this feedback-sending case. Open before RTL: the layout of a two-layer system (which cells are A and B, how many gated outputs), the lag of three windows per gated layer, and what the gate should do for the classification job (the concept-level model of the previous rows found no clear gain from feedback roles on the class accumulators). | Mechanism shown in the Python model only. |
| PATTERN RECALL FROM A PARTIAL CUE, CONCEPT MODEL (22 Oct; Alan's question: leaky accumulators hold a pattern and then lose it, how can a stored copy be reloaded when a partial cue arrives; tools/hexn_recall_model_v1.py, no RTL). Patterns are sets of lanes (the class cells) active together; a cue is part of one stored pattern with drops and false lanes; success = the completed set equals the stored pattern exactly. LOOKUP STORE (the M stored patterns kept in a small memory, recall = largest overlap with the cue, a tie counts as wrong): 19 lanes, 5 active, cue 3 of them, no drops, 2 percent false lanes: 1/2/4/8/12/16/24 stored patterns give 1.00/1.00/0.99/0.87/0.90/0.66/0.67. With 20 percent drops and 5 percent false lanes: 1.00/0.93/0.80/0.55/0.52/0.34/0.33. 7 lanes, 3 active, cue 2, 10 percent drops: 1.00/0.90/0.64/0.23/0.18/0.06/0.00 (35 possible patterns, so 7 lanes saturate quickly). CELLS ALONE (no store: lane settings set once by a clipped Hebbian rule into the cell's three settings off/+1/-1, one step, best single threshold): 19 lanes, no drops: 1.00 for one stored pattern, 0.51 for two, 0.00 for four or more. Reading: that particular rule fails beyond two patterns; it does NOT show the cells cannot do recall (better weights, graded weights from the detector counters, several steps, or competition between cells were not tried). Reading for the design: the short-term trace (leaky accumulator) and the long-term pattern (stored vector) are two different things; a small content-addressable store of burst-length vectors, written when an accumulator pattern has stayed high for some windows and read by best overlap with the current partial cue, then replayed into the cells like the glue buffer replays tokens, is the simple version that works in the model for up to about 8 patterns on 19 lanes at low noise. It matches the UniCell freeze, save and load mechanism, which has run on the Kintex card (main ledger #1250 to #1253), as a way to write and reload cell state. NOT tested: the writing rule (when to store, what to do when the store is full), graded burst lengths instead of on/off lanes, interaction with the leak, any RTL. | Model only. |
| FAMILIAR, NOVEL, FIT: WHAT THE COUNTED DETECTOR BANK ALREADY GIVES (22 Oct; Alan: the system should say 'have I seen this before, even partially', note the difference, recall the stored pattern and see how well it fits or deviates; tools/hexn_familiar_model_v1.py, model only). 7-class example bank, centre of each class as the stored template. FAMILIAR: a sample with wire-flip noise q is 'familiar' (some detector's burst >= 1) in 1.00 / 0.99 / 0.91 / 0.77 / 0.54 / 0.28 / 0.12 of tries at q = 0 / 0.05 / 0.10 / 0.15 / 0.20 / 0.30 / 0.40, and the winner is the right class in 1.00 / 0.91 / 0.89 / 0.84 / 0.82 / 0.66 / 0.41 of the familiar ones. NOVEL: random 18-wire patterns never trained are called familiar in 7.4 percent of tries at burst >= 1 and 5.8 percent at burst >= 2 or 3 (so a burst floor of 2 removes a little false familiarity at almost no cost). FIT: largest burst against the number of wires that differ from the stored centre: 0 or 1 wires 7.00, 2 wires 6.76, 3 wires 4.48, 4 wires 1.46, 5 wires 1.25, 6 wires 0.80, 8 wires 0.40: the burst length is a graded fit measure over about 2 to 5 differing wires and saturates at 7 near a perfect match (0 to 1 differing wires look the same). MAPPING (design, not built): recognition and 'how well' = the detector's burst; 'seen before' = largest burst above a floor; novelty = largest burst 0 (a candidate to learn, but training is serial and frozen afterwards, so online learning is not available); recall of the stored pattern = read the winner's stored template (its positive-wire mask) out of the settings the bank already holds; deviation = which wires differ = (input AND negative mask) OR (NOT input AND positive mask) for the winner, one mask per run; both would be a mux over the K x NB settings bits plus a few gates. Not built: the readout, a command for it, any board test. Limits: one template per class (the centre), 7 classes, flip noise only (no missing-data cue), not compared with the pattern-recall store of the previous row. | Model only. |
| LEAKY CELLS BUILD FOR THE TANG (22 Oct; board top v10, bank v15, cell v4, lattice v14, build script v15, hex7, NCELL = 7, noalu, seed 1): 12112 of 20736 LUT4 slots (58.4 percent), 1621 flip-flops, 51.4 MHz post-route (board clock 27), no 'has no driver' in the synthesis log. For comparison the one-cell build (v14) was 10264 slots: the six extra leaky cells and the leak cost 1848 slots, about 308 per cell, measured by placed builds (the earlier synthesis-only counts were too noisy to give this). Simulation through the serial commands: hex7 with 7 cells, random leak per trial, 100 trials, 0 wrong (cell totals 350, outputs, detector part). Bitstream hexn_leak15_w18_hex7_nc7_noalu.fs, host test tools/hexn_tang_leak_v1.py with wrapper tools/hexn_tang_leak_run_v1.sh (passes against a Python stand-in). BOARD RESULT (Alan, 22 Oct, hex7 with 7 leaky cells, tools/hexn_tang_leak_run_v1.sh): trained settings 0 of 7 differ from the rules; 84 episodes per leak setting at noise 15 percent, 5 settings, 6720 frames: the board's class totals differ from the Python chain in 0 of 6720 frames: LEAK TEST PASS. The accuracy table is identical to the simulation table of the leaky-cell row, every entry (clear 0.512 at the end of scene A and 0.524 eight windows after the switch; half 0.786 and 0.750; a-(a>>2) 0.798 and 0.798; a-(a>>3) 0.821 and 0.750; keep 0.821 and 0.536), as expected from identical totals and identical episodes. Scope: one bank, 7 classes, one noise level, one leak setting per bitstream run shared by all cells; the board self-test (BIST) was not run on this bitstream. | Passes on the board. |
| SAVE AND RECALL WITH PARTIAL MATCHING, WIRE-LEVEL CONCEPT MODEL (22 Oct; Alan: apply the leak, familiarity and recall ideas to a save and recall system; tools/hexn_saverecall_model_v1.py, no RTL). 18 wires, 8 random object patterns (instances, not trained classes) stored in slots. SAVE: the object is watched for WS windows with flip noise qs and every wire has a small saturating up/down counter (+1 high, -1 low, clamp +-7), the stored bit is the counter's sign (the leaky/integrating cleaning step, per wire). RECALL: the cue is watched for NC windows over the KNOWN wires only (a share 'hide' of wires unknown), integrated the same way; score of a slot = matching known wires / known wires; the best slot is accepted if its score >= 0.75 and it is the unique best (second run: and it beats the runner-up by 12 percent of the known wires); deviation = known wires that differ between the integrated cue and the recalled slot. 400 tries per row. RESULTS (no margin rule; ID = the right slot found): full view, 1 cue window, noise 15 percent: saved from 1 window ID 0.40 (saved copy never exact), from 3 windows 0.71 (28 percent exact), from 8 windows 0.86 (every saved copy exact). With 8 save windows and 3 cue windows ID 0.99; half the wires hidden: ID 0.69 (1 cue window) and 0.81 (3 cue windows); saved from 1 window and half hidden 0.59. Noise 25 percent on both save and cue with 30 percent hidden and 2 changed wires: ID about 0.29 to 0.30. DEVIATION (modified object, 3 cue windows, noise 15 percent, nothing hidden): 2 wires changed: ID 0.88, reported deviation 2.77 against 2 true; 4 wires changed: ID 0.45, reported 3.77 against 4; but even with NOTHING changed the reported deviation is 1.3 to 2.4 wires (cue noise), so a modification is visible only above that floor, about 3 or 4 wires here. FALSE FAMILIARITY (never-saved random objects accepted): 12 to 14 percent at full view, 26 to 37 percent when 30 to 50 percent of the wires are hidden (few known wires match by chance: with 8 stored random patterns the best chance match is about 70 percent). The margin rule (12 percent) cuts it to 5 to 10 percent at full view and 16 to 32 percent hidden, at a cost in ID (full view 8 save windows, 1 cue window: 0.86 to 0.62; 3 cue windows 0.99 to 0.96). Reading: what you save matters most (watch the object several windows and store the integrated, cleaned pattern), the cue gains a lot from a few windows, partial cues work but accept more false matches, and a modification shows only once it exceeds the noise floor. All stored patterns here are random and nearly orthogonal; real patterns that resemble each other will be harder. NOT built, NOT in RTL, no board test. Rough scale of a hardware version, an estimate not a measurement: 18 four-bit counters, 8 slots of 18 bits, and 8 compare-and-count trees with a best-of-8 pick. | Model only. |
| SAVE AND RECALL MEMORY BLOCK (22 Oct, simulated and built; NOT yet run on the board) | rtl/hexn_mem_v1.v with board top rtl/hexn_tang_top_v11.v (commands 0x20-0x25). Per-wire saturating counters (4 bit, clamp +-7) integrate watched cues; a saved slot stores the bit (counter > 0); 8 slots of 18 bits. Recall compares over KNOWN wires only, one slot at a time, and returns best slot, best and second-best match counts, known-wire count, integrated cue, recalled pattern and deviation mask (cue XOR recalled, AND known). Host policy (not in silicon): familiar if best is strictly ahead of second and matches >= 0.75 x known. Simulation through the serial commands (tools/hexn_mem_check_v1.py): 0 of 480 answers differ from the Python rules at 20 tries and 0 of 2080 at 100 tries. Build v16 (hex7, NCELL=7, mem v1): 12616 of 20736 slots (60.8%), 1845 flip-flops, no undriven-signal warnings, 51.6 MHz against the 27 MHz board clock. Board test tool: tools/hexn_tang_mem_v1.py via tools/hexn_tang_mem_run_v1.sh (Python stand-in passes 880 of 880). BOARD RESULT (22 Oct, Alan's OptiPlex, hex7 NC=7 bitstream hexn_mem16): MEMORY TEST PASS, 0 of 3080 board answers differ from the Python rules (saved slots, best/second match, known count, integrated cue, recalled pattern, deviation mask). The accuracy table is the Python rules' behaviour, now confirmed on silicon: right slot found 0.58 (1 window, 1 cue window) to 0.98 (8 save windows, 3 cue windows); with half the wires hidden 0.59 (1 cue window) and 0.79 (3); never-saved object wrongly called familiar 0.10-0.15 with no margin, 0.05-0.09 with the 12 percent margin at low hiding, rising to 0.39/0.29 at half hidden; deviation 2.75 reported vs 2 true changed wires and 3.73 vs 4; recall falls to 0.41 at 25 percent noise with 30 percent hidden and 2 changed. Not tested: BIST on this bitstream, similar-pattern recall, write rule with leak. | Board PASS (memory block). |
| WRITE RULE FOR THE MEMORY BLOCK (22 Oct, Python and simulation; board run pending) | Host policy on the existing memory commands, no new hardware (tools/hexn_mem_write_v1.py). Online stream of 400 encounters (12 objects, 3 cue windows at 15% noise, 1/3 of views 30% hidden, 10% of views changed by 2 wires, popularity switches at the half way point); 8 slots. Parts: GUARD (store a novel object only from a view with >= 16 of 18 wires visible, after 4 extra look windows, because a store writes 0 on unseen wires), EVICT weakest, FADE (strength -1 every 10 events, +3 per hit, cap 9). Mean of 10 seeds, right slot found for objects hot in the second phase: naive store-any FIFO 0.53; guard but stop when full 0.50; guard + weakest out 0.82; guard + weakest out + fade 0.88. Cost of fading: old objects recognised late 0.83 (stop when full) / 0.52 (no fade) / 0.28 (fade); they are forgotten on purpose. Wires wrong per stored slot 3.50 (naive) vs 1.0-1.1 (guard). Flaws seen: duplicate slots (an object judged novel from a noisy view is stored twice) 0.3-0.8 per run; never-stored object called familiar 4-6%; wrong slot named 1-3%. Simulation through top v11: 0 of 1968 answers differ. Board tool tools/hexn_tang_write_v1.py, wrapper tools/hexn_tang_write_run_v1.sh (BIST first, then the write test). BOARD RESULT (22 Oct, OptiPlex, bitstream hexn_mem16): BIST PASS (all stages incl. RUN 28 checked, DIVTEST 24 checked) and WRITE RULE TEST PASS: 0 of 5891 board answers differ from the Python rules and the result table equals the Python-only table for the same 3 seeds. Right slot found for objects hot in phase 2 (3 seeds): 0.56 naive, 0.47 guard-stop, 0.79 guard+weakest, 0.83 guard+weakest+fade; old objects late 0.07 / 0.58 / 0.58 / 0.38; duplicates 2.00 / 0.33 / 0.67 / 0.67 per run. Same ordering as the 10-seed Python means. | Board PASS (write rule). |
| EXPLORER PAGE: MEMORY AND LEAK SECTIONS (22 Oct) | docs/hex-n/frontend/hexn_explorer_v1.html gained section 7 (memory: pick the write rule parts - how complete a view must be, look windows, what to do when full, fade, gain - and the world: cue windows, noise, hidden share, changed views, events, runs; shows your rule against the four tested rules and the final slots) and section 8 (leak setting s 0-7 with the board's exact level - (level >> s) arithmetic); export is now section 9. The page's memory code reproduces the Python MemRef exactly on a scripted recall and gives the four presets 0.53/0.51/0.84/0.90 right-slot rate at 10 seeds against Python 0.53/0.50/0.82/0.88 (different random numbers, so averages only; tools/hexn_frontend_mem_check_v1.js). Fixed a page bug found on the way: the dataset-card click handler was bound to every .card. | Done. |
| MERGE ON STORE (22 Oct; Python and simulation; board run pending) | tools/hexn_mem_write_v2.py (clone of v1 plus merge, same memory commands, no new hardware). When a new object is about to be stored, every slot is DUMPed and slots within md wires of the acquired view are found; the nearest is blended (its stored bits watched twice, then the new view's windows, SAVE back to the same slot) instead of taking a new slot; other near slots are retired (complement saved, strength 0; the block has no clear-one-slot command). 30 seeds, guard + weakest out + fade as base: duplicates 0.40 (no merge) -> 0.17 (md 3, keep copies) / 0.10 (md 2, retire) / 0.23 (md 3, retire); right-slot rate for popular objects 0.90 -> 0.87-0.89; never-stored called familiar 0.037 -> 0.05; merges of two DIFFERENT objects 0.23 of 4.4 merges at md 2, 1.0 of 7.9 at md 3, and md 5 is harmful (17 of 29 merges wrong, right-slot 0.70). Finding: merging removes most duplicate slots but costs about as much recall as it gains; no net improvement at these settings, md 1 is harmless but changes little. Simulation through top v11: 0 of 4121 answers differ. Board tool tools/hexn_tang_write_v2.py, wrapper tools/hexn_tang_write_run_v2.sh. | Board run pending. |
| KINTEX-7 SIZE FROM SYNTHESIS (22 Oct; yosys synth_xilinx on the Tang top, NO place and route, NO timing) | tools/hexn_kintex_size_v1.sh. LUT/FF as mapped by yosys (XC7K480T has 298,600 LUT6 and 597,200 FF per the data sheet as I recall it, to be checked against DS180): hex7 NCELL=1 3470 LUT / 1578 FF; hex7 NCELL=7 4364 / 1874; hex19 NCELL=1 7903 / 3290; hex19 NCELL=7 9698 / 3718; no BRAM except 2 RAMB18 at hex19; 12 DSP48 at hex7, 24 at hex19 (the real flow is run with -nodsp). Slopes: about 370 LUT per extra detector and 150 (hex7) to 300 (hex19) LUT per extra cell. Raw LUT headroom is therefore large (a few percent of the chip), but placed size, routing and clock rate are NOT known; they need the openXC7 run (fpga/build_hexn_kintex_v1.sh, fpga/hexn_kintex_sweep_v1.sh, top rtl/hexn_kintex_top_v1.v with an LFSR-driven command input because the card has no UART and no JTAG command path yet). | Run on the OptiPlex. |
| KINTEX-7 PLACED RESULTS, FIRST SWEEP (23 Oct; OptiPlex, openXC7 nextpnr-xilinx, xc7k480tffg1156-1, 50 MHz target, top rtl/hexn_kintex_top_v1.v, bank v15) | All five configurations routed. Max clock: hex7 1 cell 71.92 MHz and hex7 7 cells 70.45 MHz (pass); hex19 1 cell 27.56, 7 cells 25.12, 19 cells 27.01 MHz (fail at 50). Adding cells costs little speed. The hex19 critical path (36.3 ns) was NOT the host link and not wiring: it goes through the bank's answer logic (rtl/hexn_bank_v15.v lines 103-109: maximum of 19 counts by a serial compare chain, then a serial count of ties, then the first class, all in one clock) and the lattice instantiation. The yosys LUT/FF columns of these runs printed 0 because the reader did not match the OptiPlex yosys stat layout (fixed, tools/hexn_kintex_stat_v1.py). | Measured. |

Lattice generator tools/hexn_lattice_gen_v15.py -> rtl/gen/hexn_lattice_{hex7,hex19}_w18_v15.v. | KINTEX-7 SIZE AND SPEED, FINAL SWEEP WITH FIXES (23 Oct; OptiPlex, openXC7 nextpnr-xilinx, xc7k480tffg1156-1, 50 MHz target, lattice v16 + bank v17 + cell v5, top rtl/hexn_kintex_top_v1.v, fpga/hexn_kintex_sweep_v4.sh) | ALL FIVE PASS at 50 MHz and route. hex7 1 cell: 4736 LUT, 1762 FF, 74.91 MHz. hex7 7 cells: 5681 LUT, 2044 FF, 76.98 MHz. hex19 1 cell: 10100 LUT, 3502 FF, 68.36 MHz. hex19 7 cells: 11996 LUT, 3928 FF, 69.79 MHz. hex19 19 cells: 15292 LUT, 4780 FF, 70.47 MHz (about 5.1% of 298,600 LUT and 0.8% of 597,200 FF; chip totals as I recall them from the data sheet, to be checked). 2 RAMB18 in every build, no DSP (the flow uses -nodsp). History of hex19 1 cell: 27.56 MHz (bank v15, critical path in the answer logic) -> 40.05 (bank v16, pipelined answer; next path in the cell's forward sum) -> 68.36 (cell v5). Slopes from these runs: about 450 LUT per extra detector (hex7 to hex19 at 1 cell), about 160 (hex7) and 290 (hex19) LUT per extra cell; clock does not fall as cells are added over 1-19. A straight-line extrapolation (NOT measured: routing, congestion and the clock at larger sizes are unknown) puts a hex19 bank with several hundred cells inside the LUT budget; the next measurement is a larger cell count and several banks side by side. nextpnr-xilinx is reported by the ypcb-00338-1p1 repo to be pessimistic (235 MHz reported against 400 MHz on silicon); no silicon clock measurement of this design yet. Function on the card is untested: it needs the JTAG command path (BSCANE2 USER1 byte bridge, planned) and a build through the ypcb-00338-1p1 flow (board.fasm holds AC24, the card reset pin, high). | Measured (placed, not run). |
| WHAT THE FIX DOES NOT YET COVER | Hex7 (7 detectors) and hex19 (19 detectors) have been run on the board, both with the one-clock ACCUM trainer (trainbank_v8) in the noalu build. Not yet run on the board: rhombus4, par16, and any lattice wider than 18 wires (18 wires has since been built and run, see the 18-wire row; 20 wires and beyond are not done). The carry-chain flow is the one used for the early size measurements in this document; those slot counts are for that flow and are lower than the noalu counts. Idea (Alan, 22 Oct): build stage-by-stage read-back and known-value loading into the training bank permanently, which would also serve as a built-in self-test in silicon. | Not done. |
| 16 CLASSES, 7-bit accumulator, 12 / 18 / 20 wires (hexn_tally_v5.py --aw 7; 16 samples/class, noise 0.15, 25 seeds; template rule 0.727 / 0.870 / 0.907 at pattern gap 4, 0.709 / 0.870 / 0.904 at gap 2) | gap 4: 0.554 / 0.638 / 0.676 (worst 0.46 / 0.50 / 0.60; seeds at 0.50 or more 21 / 25 / 25; at 0.70 or more 0 / 1 / 7); gap 2: 0.480 / 0.622 / 0.667 (worst 0.33 / 0.48 / 0.58; at 0.50 or more 11 / 24 / 25). Paired: 18 vs 12 wires +0.084 (ratio +5.72, 21 wins 2 losses) at gap 4 and +0.142 (ratio +9.35, 25 wins 0 losses) at gap 2; 20 vs 18 wires +0.038 (ratio +2.94) and +0.045 (ratio +3.21); 20 vs 12 wires +0.122 (ratio +8.99) and +0.187 (ratio +10.67, 25 wins 0 losses). 7 bits gives the same result as 8 bits at 18 wires (identical at gap 4, 24 ties of 25 at gap 2). Share of the template rule kept: 0.76 / 0.73 / 0.75 (gap 4), 0.68 / 0.71 / 0.74 (gap 2) | REAL (accept): with the accumulator widened, every extra step in input width helps with 16 classes (12 to 18 to 20 wires: 0.55 to 0.64 to 0.68 at gap 4; 0.48 to 0.62 to 0.67 at gap 2) and the worst seed rises from 0.33 to 0.58. The detector keeps a steady 70 to 76% of the data's own ceiling at every width, so the gain from more wires follows the ceiling itself (0.73 to 0.91); the remaining distance to the ceiling is the same fraction at every width |
| Decision table started in the BROADCAST state (any input is passed to all six faces at level 2; Alan, 21 Oct) instead of straight-through | 0.324 vs 0.414 (25 seeds; 4 wins 18 losses; paired difference -0.090, 3.4x its error); sd 0.091, worst 0.21, best 0.46, none at 0.50 or more | **clearly worse** (the compare tool's wording "no difference shown" only means "not accepted"). Tested with the search doing the pruning, not the cell. |
| Per-lane weights, a 2-bit weight on each of the 24 lanes, trained and fixed at run time (Alan, 21 Oct) | 0.340 vs 0.376 (25 seeds; 7 wins 15 losses; sd 0.121 vs 0.155, only 1 seed at 0.50 or more vs 6) | no difference shown; leans worse |

**Why the layout change mattered.** A diagnostic (`tools/hexn_diag_v1.py`) on models trained on clean data showed each one learned only 1 to 3 of the 4 classes. In the original layout output groups 1 and 2 were driven by the same cell and groups 2 and 3 by another; a v2 cell makes one decision, so those groups could not differ.

**Frozen model (train, freeze, then add noise), `tools/hexn_cluster_v20.py`, 12 seeds, run on Alan's machine and here.** Trained on clean data the frozen model scores 0.417 / 0.382 / 0.323 / 0.273 at test noise 0 / 0.05 / 0.15 / 0.25 (ceiling 1.00 / 0.98 / 0.89 / 0.70). Trained at noise 0.15: 0.375 / 0.352 / 0.292 / 0.274, no better. Letting the threshold offset adapt during the test stream made it **worse** at every level (by 0.139 to 0.072), losing on 8 to 11 of 12 seeds. This tests one form of run-time adaptation; it does not show adaptation in general cannot help.

**Where it stands.** Best cluster so far 0.385 (0.418 on 15 seeds with 2,000 tries, not accepted), against 0.68 for four separate detectors and about 0.90 for the ceiling. The gap is not closed. Two mistakes of mine are on record: the first feedback test was void (see above), and the earlier network tests (section 4) were withdrawn.

## 4b. Published relatives (21 Oct 2026; a first pass from abstracts and summaries, NOT a novelty review and not a claim either way)

Each idea in this design has a published relative. Read from the linked papers only as far as stated; the notes are what the fetched text said.

| Our idea | Relative | What the source says |
|---|---|---|
| Decision-table cell: a table addressed by which faces had input | Weightless networks, WiSARD and [ULEEN](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2304.10618) | A neuron is a lookup table addressed by input bits with learned 1-bit entries; each class has its own bank and the strongest summed response wins. Training can be one-shot counting followed by a threshold ("bleaching"), with no search. |
| Table cells frozen into hardware; small fan-in | [LogicNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.03021) | A trained neuron with few quantised inputs becomes a truth table in an FPGA circuit; the table cost grows exponentially with fan-in, so fan-in must stay small. |
| Tally with a commit threshold per decision | Tsetlin machine, hardware tool [MATADOR](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2403.10538) | Learning elements are small state machines whose state crossing a boundary flips include/exclude; trained models are sparse and map to few AND/NOT gates. Update rules not read. A search result title suggests on-chip training accelerators exist ("Convolutional Tsetlin Machine-based Training and Inference Accelerator", not read). |
| Remember the path used, multiply by feedback when it arrives | Eligibility traces, [e-prop](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.24506v1) | Each connection keeps a running trace of its recent effect; a later learning signal is multiplied by the trace; no history is stored. The maths assumes smooth functions, ours are discrete. |

What may still be particular to this design is the combination under its constraints: small cells with six faces and burst signalling, local rules only, and retraining in place in silicon. That is a guess about scope, not a finding.

## 5. Open questions

1. A1 to A7 (how strongly each lane counts, which way the bias acts, what "graded" means, and so on).
2. Should the accumulator leak slowly or clear at the end of each window? Not decided; the biggest gap against a real neuron.
3. How many bits of memory, and should it be a plain shift register or something richer (a counter, context tags)?
4. Credit assignment in a network: not yet measured (the earlier test was void, see section 4). Needs a layout in which input can actually reach the output, which depends on the answer to section 2a.
5. Where the goal and teacher live in hardware (an external teacher such as the ESP32, a reference held in the cell, or neighbours scoring each other).
6. A real routing question for clusters: several routes, and different results depending on the input, so a route has to remember the input. Wire weights alone cannot do this; the memory above is one answer, a ring of cells holding a value is another (untested).
7. Real hardware: stacked-die chips could carry a third dimension in future (a note on the 3D thread in UniCell; nothing here depends on it).
8. DECIDED (Alan, 21 Oct 2026): the fan-out relays on a hex grid are repeaters / bridges on the cell edges (three per cell, one per edge direction), NOT full cells. They are required joins and part of the repeating pattern, so their cost belongs to the design (about 4 flip-flops each, about 1.6% on top of the detectors by the count in section 4a), and the 'compose from existing cells' rule is not applied to them. Observation by Claude, NOT checked by a layout: in a hex lattice every cell sits on exactly one straight line in each of the three edge directions, and 3 directions x 4 lanes per face = 12 wires, so a 12-wire detector bank may fit this pattern with one 4-wire group per direction; 18 or 20 wires would need more lanes per face (the original specification had 16) or more groups per direction. Open: the output path, and feeding each parallel line from the boundary.

9. DECIDED (Alan, 22 Oct 2026): two lines. The DETECTOR CHIP (the counted detector bank: minimal detector `hexn_det_v2`, training bank, lattice, Tang Nano 20K build) has no feedback and may lose the feedback phase of its timing as well; the full cell (`hexn_cell_v2`, the 'v2' design with feedback lanes) is the NEURAL design and keeps its feedback. Until that is done the detector bank still runs the forward-plus-feedback window timing it inherited from the cell (the feedback half does nothing and doubles a sample's run time to W x 2 x PL clocks, 40 clocks at W = 2, PL = 10); removing it would need a feedback-free detector and run controller as new files, with the burst counts checked against the Python model again. Not done yet.

## 6. Targets

Tang Nano 20K (Gowin) and a Kintex card, both on hand. Status (22 Oct 2026): the counted-detector bank with the 12-wire hex lattice (hex7, 7 detectors) has been placed and routed for the Tang Nano 20K and loaded onto the real board, which answers over the serial link. Its results match the simulation exactly (5,250 of 5,250 test samples, see the board rows in section 4a); this holds for the build without carry-chain arithmetic cells. Pins are taken from `docs/man/tang-nano-20k.cst`, checked against the chip database but NOT against the board schematic.

| HOST LINK BUG FOUND ON THE CARD AND FIXED (23 Oct) | tools/hexn_jtag_host_v1.py (_sync), tools/hexn_jtag_sessions_check_v1.py | Symptom: a second test run on the card WITHOUT reloading the design got no answer to its first command ('the card answered 0 of 1 bytes'), twice (leaky-cell test after the cell test; all-cells test after the BIST); the first run after a load always worked. Cause (read from rtl/hexn_jtag_bridge_v1.v, reproduced in simulation): a new host session starts counting received bytes at 0, but the card keeps its byte counter (pop_total) and last seq from the earlier session and only drops bytes when the ack is ahead of its own counter, so the host mapped every byte the card sent to the past and discarded it. The design was not at fault. Fix: at its first frame a link reads the card's counters, lines up with them (abs_got = pop_total, seq = last seq) and discards stale bytes. Simulation: four sessions in a row, with unread bytes left behind, PASS (the old host hangs on the second session); loopback with injected errors still PASS. Needs a rerun of the failed card tests. Also on the card: BIST PASS on the 128-cell build (build_hex19_nc128), 19 detectors. |

| CORE BUG FOUND ON THE CARD AT 128 CELLS, FIXED IN hexn_core_v2 (23 Oct) | rtl/hexn_core_v2.v (clone of v1, left untouched), rtl/tb_hexn_jtag_v2.v, tools/hexn_core_ncell_check_v1.py, tools/hexn_card_probe_v1.py | Symptom on the card: with the 128-cell build, the all-cells test stopped with 'unexpected reply byte 0x00 (wanted 0xA5)' right after the cells were loaded, on a fresh load too; the probe showed LOADCELLN itself works for all 128 cells. Cause (read from the RTL, reproduced in simulation): the byte counter ci of the reply state machines is 8 bits, and RUNCELLN ends when ci == 2*NC + K; with NC = 128 and K = 19 that is 275, unreachable for an 8-bit counter, so the reply never ended and the stream kept going. Simulation (hex7, NC 128, K 7): core v1 FAIL (next acknowledgement 0x00, 296 stray bytes), core v2 PASS. Fix: ci is 12 bits in v2; the card design (fpga/ypcb/hexn/hexn.v, prebuild.py) now uses hexn_core_v2. Builds with NCELL up to 118 (19 detectors) were not affected. CONSEQUENCES: (1) the 128-cell and 255-cell builds made with core v1 are not valid for the cell run command; the 128-cell build is also the one the BIST passed on (the BIST does not use RUNCELLN). (2) The earlier 255-cell synthesis size (35645 LUT) was too small: with the 8-bit counter the cells above number 127 could never be read, so synthesis removed them. A new 255-cell size with core v2 is being measured. The ladder results for 128 and 255 must be redone with core v2 (HEXN_TAG=_c2 keeps the old folders). |

| CORRECTED SIZE FOR 255 CELLS (23 Oct; yosys synth_xilinx -flatten -abc9 -arch xc7, hex19, core v2, sandbox yosys, NO place and route) | fpga/ypcb/hexn with HEXN_NCELL | NCELL 128: 35946 LUT; NCELL 255: 62847 LUT, 22240 FF, 24 DSP, 2 RAMB18, 3608 CARRY4 (about 21% of 298,600 LUT as recalled from the data sheet, to be checked). Slope about 212 LUT per cell from 128 to 255, the same as 19 to 64 (about 210). This replaces the earlier 255-cell figure (35645 LUT), which was too small because core v1 could never read cells above 127 and synthesis removed them. Placement time and clock at this size are not known yet. |

| SEQUENCE PREDICTION WITH THE MEMORY BLOCK, PYTHON MODEL (23 Oct; Alan asked how the pattern recognition can give inference) | tools/hexn_seq_predict_v1.py (host policy only, same commands as the write rules, device answers compared with the Python copy of the block: 0 of 7980 differ; NOT yet run on a board) | Idea: store each transition as one 18-wire pattern (the 9 wires of the state left, then the 9 wires of the state entered); to predict, cue with the current state on the low 9 wires only; the recalled pattern's high half is the predicted next state; chaining the recalled half as the next cue predicts several steps ahead. Results (mean of 5 seeds, 150 learning steps, 240 test steps, states are random 9-wire patterns differing in >= 3 wires, noise per wire in the views): CYCLE of 4, 6 or 8 states at noise 0.05: always answered, 100 percent right, also 2, 3 and 4 steps ahead. At noise 0.15: 89 percent answered, 94 to 98 percent right when answered (the rest are 'ambiguous' because noise made the learner store a transition twice: S=4 used 6.8 slots for 4 transitions), 87 to 98 percent right 2 to 4 steps ahead. At noise 0.25 (S=6): 71 percent answered, 67 percent right: too noisy for 9-wire states. BRANCH (state 0 goes to 1 with 70 percent, to 2 with 30 percent): the model says 'ambiguous' for 14 to 15 percent of test steps, which is the share of time spent in the branching state, and is 93 to 96 percent right on the rest (a guess counts as right if it is one of the possible successors); multi-step columns are not measured for branches (shown as 0.00). Limits: first order only (the next state depends only on the current one), 8 slots, no eviction, the learner can store duplicates under noise. |

| SEQUENCE PREDICTION, BETTER LEARNER (23 Oct; Python model, mean of 5 seeds; tools/hexn_seq_predict_v2.py, clone of v1 left untouched; device answers vs Python copy of the block: 0 of 29933 differ; not yet on a board) | learner variants | Two changes to the learner: an ODD number of look windows when a state is viewed (with an even number a tied wire becomes 0, which biases the stored pattern and made noise create duplicate slots), and a second slot for a state is stored only after several CONFIRMED conflicts that AGREE with each other (a real branch repeats the same alternative; noise gives a different one each time). Results at noise 0.15: v1 (4 looks, 2 conflicts) cycle of 4 used 6.8 slots for 4 transitions, answered 89 percent, right 98 percent; 7 looks with 3 agreeing conflicts: exactly 4.0 slots, answered 95 percent, right 99 percent. Cycle of 6: answered 89 to 97 percent, right 97 to 99 percent, slots 8.0 to 6.0 (exact); 4 steps ahead 97 to 99 percent. Cycle of 8: answered 89 to 96 percent, right 94 to 98 percent, 4 steps ahead 87 to 98 percent. At noise 0.25 (cycle of 6): answered 71 to 75 percent, right 67 to 83 percent, 4 steps ahead 53 to 82 percent: still weak. Branch: ambiguous 14 to 16 percent (the share of time in the branching state, as it should), right 99 to 100 percent on the rest, 7.0 slots for 7 real transitions. The odd look count was the main gain; the agreement rule helps a little. 4 agreeing conflicts did no better than 3. |

| ODD LOOKS APPLIED TO THE WRITE RULES (23 Oct; Alan asked to apply the odd-man-out tie-breaker; Python model only, NOT yet on a board) | tools/hexn_mem_write_odd_v1.py (5 seeds), tools/hexn_mem_write_v4.py and tools/hexn_tang_write_v4.py (clones, v3 untouched; selftest 3 seeds, board-model answers vs Python rules: 0 of 13204 differ) | An odd number of looks cannot tie, and the block reads a tied counter as 0. Result: 7 looks beats 4 looks for plain v1 (duplicates 0.33 to 0.00, wrong wires per slot 0.67 to 0.38 in the 3-seed selftest; 0.75 to 0.27 in the 5-seed run) and for merge md2 retire (hot-now 0.89 to 0.94, no duplicates, no wrong merges). md3 gets WORSE at 7 looks (hot-now 0.96 to 0.85, never-stored called familiar appears); md5 is bad everywhere. Candidate new policy: 7 looks (or 5) with merge md2 retire. Small seed counts, model only; needs a board run. |

| ODD LOOKS WRITE RULES ON THE KINTEX CARD (23 Oct; 10 seeds; NC=64 build, memory block only; tools/hexn_tang_write_v4.py via hexn_card_run_v1.py) | board answers vs Python rules: 0 of 46028 differ; table equals the Python-only table | RESULT: PASS. 7 looks beats 4 looks: plain v1 hot-now 0.89 to 0.94, wrong wires per slot 0.76 to 0.28, duplicates 0.40 to 0.10; merge md2 retire hot-now 0.83 to 0.95, wrong wires 0.96 to 0.28. CORRECTION of the 3-seed selftest note above: md2 at 7 looks is NOT free of duplicates or wrong merges (duplicates 0.20, merges of two different objects 0.10 per run). md3 at 7 looks: hot-now 0.89 (no gain), wrong merges 0.90, never-stored-called-familiar 0.055 (worst). Plain v1 at 7 looks is about as good as md2 at 7 looks (0.94 vs 0.95 hot-now, same 0.28 wrong wires, fewer wrong merges: none), so the gain comes from the odd number of looks, not from merging. Differences between rows are small against 10-seed noise. |

| HASHED RAM TIER, PYTHON MODEL (23 Oct; Alan: patterns in the card RAM, address = hash of the pattern, bucket compared on read; RAM read time and the 8 Mbit limit to be included; MODEL ONLY, no board, RAM latency ASSUMED 10 and 30 cycles, not measured) | tools/hexn_ram_tier_v1.py (2 seeds, 600 queries each) | Model: T hash tables each addressed by K chosen wires of the W-wire key (so near patterns share an address), buckets of B patterns, closest in the buckets wins if within W/8 wires (min 3). Capacity ASSUMED as 8 Mbit / (4 bits x W wires) = 111,111 entries at W=18, 31,250 at W=64. Findings: (1) an 18-wire key cannot tell many objects apart in RAM: with 20,000 stored the wrong-answer rate is 0.6 to 1.0, so a RAM tier needs wider keys (W=64 gave 0 wrong answers everywhere, misses instead). (2) Recall depends on table size versus objects stored: at W=64, T=3 K=10 B=4 (12,288 entries) holds 1,000 objects at 1.00/1.00/0.98/0.93 right for 0/1/2/3 flipped wires, but only 0.31 to 0.24 with 20,000 stored (table too small); T=3 K=12 B=4 recalls 0.92 to 0.72 with 20,000 but that is 49,152 entries which exceeds the assumed W=64 capacity of 31,250, so it does NOT fit; the sizing probe allowed 4x capacity and these rows must be read against the cap column. (3) More tables help noise tolerance but cost cycles: serial reads cost T x (latency + B-1): 3 tables = 39 cycles at latency 10, 99 at 30, against about 1 cycle for the 8-slot fabric block (13 and 33 if the tables sit in parallel banks). Not yet modelled: counters (only binary patterns), writes/updates in RAM, refresh/replace policy beyond FIFO. |

| HOT BUFFER IN FRONT OF THE HASHED RAM TIER, PYTHON MODEL (23 Oct; Alan: the cell's existing block as the buffer for the RAM side; MODEL ONLY, RAM latency ASSUMED 10/30 cycles, counters and updates not modelled) | tools/hexn_ram_tier_v2.py (clone-style new file, v1 untouched; 3 seeds, 6000 lookups, 5000 objects, W=64, RAM tier 24,576 entries, 2 noisy wires, popular set changes half way) | The buffer's value depends on how skewed the access is. Mean cycles per lookup, serial 3-table RAM at latency 10: no buffer 40.0; 8 slots 38.8 / 35.1 / 23.1 for Zipf 0.8 / 1.0 / 1.3 (hot hit rate 0.03 / 0.13 / 0.43); 32 slots 36.1 / 28.7 / 14.6; 128 slots 31.1 / 22.0 / 9.2 (hit rate 0.79 at Zipf 1.3). At latency 30 serial: 100 without buffer, 97 / 87.5 / 57 with 8 slots. Parallel banks at latency 30: 34 without buffer, 33 / 29.8 / 19.7 with 8 slots. Accuracy unchanged by the buffer (right 0.96 to 0.99, wrong 0.000). So 8 slots alone help only when a few objects dominate; they do not hide the RAM time for flat access. |

| 128-CELL BUILD (CORE V2) ON THE KINTEX CARD (23 Oct; build_hex19_nc128_c2, hex19 shape, 128 cells, core v2 with the 12-bit ci) | build: synth 523 s, pnr 1671 s, frames 160 s; nextpnr max frequency 68.68 and 74.18 MHz on br.clk (PASS at 50), br.drck 700 and 772 MHz; real silicon clock still unmeasured | CARD RESULT: tools/hexn_tang_cells_v1.py via hexn_card_run_v1.py, example_random19_script_v1.txt, --ncell 128 --episodes 40: trained settings 0 of 19 detectors differ from the rules; 240 frames, frames with any cell differing 0; differing cell results 0 of 30720; cell outputs that fired (rules) 15435; CELLS TEST PASS.
