Repeated anomaly (an extra key 4 before a block end, N=3, W=80 events):** promoted when it repeats every 2, 4 or 8 blocks (at block 11, 17, 33), never when it repeats every 16 or 40 blocks: with N sightings the slowest accepted repeat gap is about W/(N-1), so W sets how fast a repeat must be to count as real. **D. Noise (3000 real blocks, N=3):** false entries per run at W=40 / 80 / 320 events: r=0.10: 0 / 0 / 12; r=0.30: 6 / 17 / 76; r=0.60: 27 / 59 / 182. The hardware-cheap TUMBLING window (count plus start time, restarted after W) matches the exact sliding window to within 1 entry in every row, so the cheap form is enough. **E. Candidate capacity (new routine 1 5 3 7 from block 200 in 30% noise, 20 streams):** 4 slots never learn it; 8 slots learn it (block 255, 1.4 noise entries promoted); 16: block 229, 3.9; 32: block 223, 7.0; 64: block 220, 10.8; 128: block 221, 10.9. So a candidate store of 16 to 32 slots is a good size here, and a small store filters noise at the price of learning a new routine a little later. **Errors in my own first runs (fixed, same session):** the known table was first capped at 64 entries, which silently evicted real pairs and gave false alarms, and the true-pair count was taken from runs 1 and 2 alone, which counted legitimate unseen transitions as false entries; both corrected before the figures above. **Limits:** three short real runs; noise and repeated anomalies injected; confidence is the plain count share, nothing learned; one context-length rule; no RTL; no reward. **Next:** RTL for the store (known and candidate tables, the tumbling counter, retrieve and observe commands) next to `hexn_hist_v1`, simulated against this model; then the reward goal read. |
| **Layer 2 store and retrieve, RTL (simulation only; nothing on the card).** `rtl/hexn_store_v1.v`, `rtl/tb_hexn_store_v1.v`, bit-true reference model `tools/hexn_store_v2.py` (clone of the v1 design with slot rules fixed so hardware and model cannot differ), checker `tools/hexn_store_rtl_check_v1.py`, `tests/test_hexn_store_rtl_v1.py` (3 pass, about 3 s; refuses to run without iverilog). **What it is:** KNOWN table (default 128 slots: key = length, context of up to 3 events, next event; seen and contra counts) and CAND table (default 32 slots: key, count, start time), both scanned one slot per clock. Commands: CLR (clear history), OBS (predict, self-check verdict, update), LOAD (host load: add pair or seen+1, no candidates), RD (retrieve only). Outputs: verdict (0 ok, 1 contradiction, 2 novel, 3 start), context length used, best next event, its count, total count at that context. Host sets n_prom (1 to 15) and w_win (events) as inputs, the host-set promotion level. **Check:** iverilog against the model on four command streams, every command's outputs and the final tables slot by slot plus the counters: (1) host load of runs 1 and 2, real run 3 online with a retrieve before each event, 244 commands; (2) cold start by the promotion rule, 180; (3) 30% noise in real blocks with tiny tables (known 16, candidate 8, so constant eviction: 419 known and 13,473 candidate drops) and the 16-bit clock wrapping, 13,039; (4) random commands with N=2 and 4 candidate slots, 1,501. **Result: 0 mismatches in 14,964 commands, and the tables are identical.** Slowest command (one OBS) is 746 clocks at 128/32 (about 28 microseconds at 27 MHz), so serial scanning is far faster than a key press. **Two bugs found by this check and fixed before the figures above:** (a) a function that read the history registers inside a continuous assignment did not re-evaluate when they changed (stale context; replaced by plain wires); (b) the candidate to drop was chosen by raw start time, which orders wrongly after the 16-bit clock wraps; now chosen by age (t minus start), in both RTL and model. **Real run 3 through the model v2 as before:** one flag, event 22, 70 known, 6 candidates. **Size (yosys `synth -noabc`, generic cells only, no LUT or clock figures because ABC does not finish here):** 128/32 gives 21,490 generic cells including about 5,400 flip-flops and 12,100 multiplexers. That is too large for the Tang Nano 20K as flop arrays; the scan already reads one slot per clock, so the tables are the natural fit for block RAM, which is the next RTL step before any card build. **Limits:** simulation only; flop-array tables; seen and contra saturate at 255, candidate count at 15; time is a 16-bit value from the host (a core tick counter in the real design); tie rules are lowest event, lowest slot; no confidence beyond counts; no reward. **Next:** move the tables to block-RAM style storage and re-check against the same model; then connect OBS to the hold/history output of core v6; then the reward goal read. |
| **Layer 2 store, RTL v2 with block-RAM style tables (simulation only; nothing on the card).** `rtl/hexn_store_v2.v`, `rtl/tb_hexn_store_v2.v`; clone of `hexn_store_v1.v` (untouched); same commands, same outputs, same bit-true model `tools/hexn_store_v2.py`. Each table is now one synchronous-read memory (one word per slot: known 31 bits, candidate 35 bits) with one read and one write port; scans run one clock behind the address counter; writes are registered requests with a one-clock gap before the next scan; after reset a sweep clears both tables (busy high until done); the host table read-out is a second synchronous read port. **Check** (`tools/hexn_store_rtl_check_v1.py`, now runs both RTL versions): the same four command streams, 14,964 commands each, **0 mismatches for v2 and v1, tables identical to the model**. Slowest command 757 clocks at 128/32 (v1: 746), about 28 microseconds at 27 MHz. **Size (yosys `synth_gowin`, mapping before the logic-optimisation step, which does not finish in this sandbox, so no LUT or clock figures):** the memories are recognised and mapped to Gowin primitives, 2 block-RAM blocks (SDPX9B, the known table and its read-out copy) plus 36 small RAM16SDP4 (candidate table), and the flip-flop count drops from about 5,400 (v1) to about 600. **Limits:** the candidate table is small enough to land in logic RAM rather than block RAM; the read-out port duplicates the known table's RAM; simulation only; no timing figure. |
| **Core v7: the store connected to the hold output; whole chain checked in simulation (nothing on the card).** `rtl/hexn_core_v7.v` (clone of v6, untouched), runner `tools/hexn_card_run_v6.py`, check `tools/hexn_store_card_v1.py`. New commands, no earlier command or reply changed: 0x18 STORECFG [n wlo whi mode], 0x19 STORECLR, 0x1A STORECMD, 0x1B STORERD (raw table word), 0x1C STORESTAT, 0x1D STORELAST. RUNHOLD feeds the store (mode 1 observe, 2 host load) when the held class is a change or the first sample and is not NONE. **Chain check** (IR codes, exact-match bank, hold length 1 with ignore-unknown, store; routines on 7 key classes A = 0 1 2 5, B = 3 1 2 6, C = 0 4 2 6, D = 3 4 2 5; 10 percent of frames damaged by one flipped bit): 458 frames (248 training by host load, 210 test by observe with N=2 W=80 and two routines carrying an extra key), 189 events fed to the store, 85 store answers checked after each fed event, then the counters and every used slot of both tables read back: **PASS, no difference from the Python chain** (store ended with 113 known, 32 candidates, 5 promoted). **A bug this check caught in my first v7:** the hold's change flag lasts ONE clock, but the reply was built a clock later after the store step, so the reply said 'no change'; fixed by latching it in the clock it is valid. Takes about 8 minutes in simulation. **Limits:** simulation only; one hold per core; the store's time is a count of its own commands; K <= 8 classes; no core size or timing figure. |
| **Reward-driven goal read, abstract model (Alan, 27 Oct 2026; Python only, no RTL).** `tools/hexn_reward_v1.py`, `tests/test_hexn_reward_v1.py` (4 pass). Each stored entry gets a value (average reward of the routines through it); the teacher gives a reward only at a routine's goal (desired goal +1, other -0.5); the goal read takes the possible next key with the highest value, exploring 10 percent. World: the real routines (the goal depends on the first AND second key, two decisions per routine); 40 routines of passive learning, then the system chooses; 30 seeds, 800 routines. **Results:** (A) final share reaching the desired goal 0.953 / 0.955 / 0.955 for context 1 / 2 / 3 with perfect key reading (ceiling 0.95 from the 10 percent exploration; a random chooser gets 0.496), 0.928 / 0.918 / 0.919 with 10 percent misread keys, 0.879 / 0.869 / 0.850 with 20 percent; routines needed to reach 0.9 with 20 percent misreading: 75 / 117 / 214 for context 1 / 2 / 3, so a short context learns fastest when keys are misread (same lesson as the path model); (B) teacher error (reward sign flipped): 0.953 / 0.959 / 0.955 at 0 / 20 / 40 percent, **0.545 at 50 percent (chance) and 0.045 at 60 percent (steered to the wrong goal)**, as the arithmetic says (the order of the two choices flips at q = 0.5); (C) goal change at routine 300 (7 -> 8): a running-mean value never re-steers in time (0.05 correct in routines 300 to 500, 405 routines to reach 0.9, 2 of 30 seeds not at all), a constant step re-steers in 90 routines (step 0.1) or 75 (step 0.3). **Reading:** the reward can sit as one extra value field beside seen and contra; keep it with a constant step (or a count that stops growing), not a pure running average. **Limits:** abstract table not the store RTL; the system knows which keys are possible next; reward only at the end; synthetic world from the real routine design; teacher modelled as a sign flip. **Next (if Alan agrees):** put the value field into the store model (hexn_store_v3 and bit-true check), then RTL. |
| **Layer 2 store with REWARD VALUES: bit-true model, RTL, closed-loop test (simulation only; nothing on the card).** `tools/hexn_store_v3.py` (model), `rtl/hexn_store_v3.v` + `rtl/tb_hexn_store_v3.v` (clone of store v2, untouched), `tools/hexn_store_rtl_check_v2.py`, `tools/hexn_reward_store_v1.py`, `tests/test_hexn_store_v3.py` (4 pass). **What was added:** a signed 8-bit VALUE in every known entry (reward +1 = +64, -0.5 = -32; 0 at creation); a ring queue of the last 32 known slots an OBS used; command REW (4) with the teacher's reward: every queued slot moves toward the reward by a step 2^-s (s host-set), then the queue is emptied; a GOAL READ output (gbest, gval) = the successor with the highest value (ties higher seen, then lower event) at the longest known context of length up to glvl (host-set 1 to 3). **RTL check: 14,088 commands in four cases (real runs with rewards at the goal keys; the reward world; heavy noise with tiny tables and a wrapping clock; random commands with random rewards): 0 mismatches in outputs including gbest and gval, and the tables identical slot by slot including the values.** **A flaw found in my own first draft of the value rule and fixed before any figure above:** a plain arithmetic shift of (reward - value) stalls early when the value should rise (step 2^-3 stops at 57 of 64, step 2^-5 at 33, step 2^-7 never leaves 0) but runs all the way down for a drop; the policy still worked, by accident, because bad entries sank. The rule now rounds UP when the difference is positive and DOWN when negative, so the value reaches the reward exactly both ways (checked for steps 2^-2 to 2^-7). **Closed-loop test on the store model (reward world, 20 seeds, 800 routines, 40 passive then the store chooses with 10 percent exploration, N=2 W=400):** share reaching the desired goal 0.952 / 0.947 / 0.916 for goal-read context 1 / 2 / 3 (ceiling 0.95), 50 / 53 / 55 routines to reach 0.9 (the abstract model of the previous row: 0.953, 51); with 10 percent misread keys 0.887 (57 routines) at context 1 against 0.868 (113) at context 3, with 20 percent 0.810 (115) against 0.798 (243, 2 of 20 seeds not at all); teacher wrong 20 / 40 / 60 percent: 0.954 / 0.896 / 0.177 (inverted at 60); goal change 7 -> 8 at routine 300: routines to reach 0.9 after the change 76 / 86 / 121 / 168 for step 2^-2 / 2^-3 / 2^-5 / 2^-7. **Reading:** the store with values reproduces the abstract result, slightly worse under misread keys (it has to learn the pairs first and misreads create bogus ones) and at 40 percent teacher error (8-bit values are coarser); a short goal-read context is the better default; the step is a trade between steadiness and re-steering speed, 2^-3 a sensible default. **Limits:** simulation only; the used-slot queue can point at a slot overwritten since; reward granularity 1/64; one reward per routine; the system knows which keys are possible next from its own entries; not yet connected to the core. |
| **Front end, first version: System View page (Alan, 27 Oct 2026; viewer only, simulation data).** `docs/hex-n/frontend/hexn_system_v1.html` (built by `tools/hexn_system_page_v1.py` from `hexn_system_v1.src.html` plus a trace), trace made by `tools/hexn_system_trace_v1.py` (`docs/hex-n/sessions/system_trace_run3_v1.json`: runs 1+2 host-loaded, run 3 observed, N=2 in W=80, value step 1/8, goal read context 1, desired goal key 7), `tests/test_hexn_system_trace_v1.py` (2 pass). Run 3 result: 65 presses, 1 flagged, 50 of 60 next keys right at 3-key context, 8 of 16 goal reads were the desired goal. Scope: it displays a trace file, it takes no live data, and nothing comes from a card; data in and live results out remain to be built. |

| **Core v8 RUNS ON THE TANG NANO 20K: card check passes and matches simulation (Alan ran it, 27 Oct 2026, USB serial /dev/ttyUSB1, bitstream `hexn_tang17_core_v8.fs`).** Command: `python3 tools/hexn_store_card_v2.py docs/hex-n/frontend/example_sensors_script_v1.txt --port /dev/ttyUSB1 --train 14 --test 14`. Output: frames 285 (train 141, test 144); events fed 121, answers checked 61, rewards sent 15; store known 71, candidates 31, promoted 1; non-zero values [-23, -14, -8, -1, 4, 16, 28, 37, 40, 44, 48, 49, 52, 53, 62]; `STORE REWARD CHAIN CHECK PASS`. The figures are identical to the simulated run of the same command (core v8 chain, logged above), so layers 1 and 2 (detector bank, hold counter, store with reward values and goal read) now work on the chip as in simulation. Scope: one script at train 14 / test 14; the full-length run, other seeds, and a power-cycle repeat are not yet done; the reward is the rule-based teacher; Layer 3 not built. |
| **Layer 3, first Python model: aligned channels, combined path, weighted possible outcomes (Alan, 27 Oct 2026; Python only, no RTL, nothing on a card).** `tools/hexn_layer3_v1.py`, `tests/test_hexn_layer3_v1.py` (4 pass). Alan's direction: Layer 3 combines the channels into one combined path, keeps deeper memories, and its outcome is the possible next events each with a weight. Design: layer 1 channels report stable-key changes; changes within `align` ticks form one MOMENT with a snapshot of every channel's latest key; path memory holds contexts of the last 1..L snapshots (L=4, deeper than layer 2's 3) seen through channel masks (all channels, then fewer); weights decay with a half-life (200 moments); `predict` returns ranked possible next moments with weights (PPM-C escape rule: a context seen t times with d different followers keeps t/(t+d) of the weight left) plus the unseen remainder; `channel_view(c)` folds these into what channel c will do next. Test world (SYNTHETIC; the recordings of light, pot and thermistor were made one at a time, not together with the remote): routines A=0125 B=3126 C=0426 D=3425, a light state decides which pair is used and toggles every 8-15 routines, a thermistor channel drifts and means nothing. Score = remote key predicted, 1,563 keys. Remote alone, context 3 (the layer 2 reading): top-1 0.757, weight on the truth 0.744. All channels, context 4, full mask only: 0.851 / 0.834. With reduced masks too: 0.877 / 0.872. The best possible in this world is 0.75 for the remote alone and 0.875 with the light, so both readings sit at their ceilings; the reduced masks matter because the meaningless thermistor channel otherwise splits the contexts and slows learning. Scope: shows the mechanism works; says nothing about real coupled data; no reward or goal read at layer 3 yet; sizes, memory layout and RTL not decided. |
| **Layer 3 with reward, goal comparison and a feedback record stream (Alan, 27 Oct 2026: "add in the reward and compare with goal mechanisms, this becomes a new set of data which can be fed back through the teaching side, an overarching feedback mechanism"; Python only, no RTL, nothing on a card).** `tools/hexn_layer3_v2.py` (extends `hexn_layer3_v1.py`, untouched), `tests/test_hexn_layer3_v2.py` (3 pass; with v1, 7 pass), records `docs/hex-n/sessions/layer3_feedback_records_v2.json`. Added: a value and a count on every path-memory entry, moved toward the reward by step 1/8 for every entry used since the last reward; a goal read (`choose`: most specific context first, an option counts after 3 tries, untried options count as 0); a goal comparison (`compare`) that turns outcome versus goal into a RECORD (goal, desired outcome, outcome, the weight layer 3's own path gave the desired outcome beforehand, choices, reward +64 or -32, the same signed bytes as the layer 2 REW command). Feedback: the reward stream goes down into the layer 2 store's own teaching commands, so layer 3 is the teacher of layer 2, and the recorded OBS/RD/REW commands replayed into a fresh store give identical tables (checked, True). Test world (SYNTHETIC): each trial a goal channel requests end 5 or 6 at random, the agent picks key 1 in {0,3} and key 2 in {1,4}, the end follows a fixed rule, a thermistor channel is noise. Share reaching the desired end, trials 600-800, 10 seeds: random 0.495; layer 2 store (remote keys only) taught by layer 3's rewards 0.479 (it cannot see the request, so it stays at chance); layer 3 with the goal channel in its path 0.948 (0.858 in trials 40-100). Layer 3's own weight on the desired end just before it happened: 0.965 mean (1.000 when reached). Scope: synthetic world and rule-based teacher; layer 2 learns nothing useful here because the information it would need lives on another channel, so the feedback shown is that layer 2 CAN be taught from layer 3's records through its normal interface, not that it improves in this world; distilling layer 3's learned choices down into layer 2 or into a host LOAD is not done; no RTL, no sizes, nothing tested on real coupled data. |
| **Design intention: the same reward-and-goal machinery for abstract inputs (Alan, 27 Oct 2026; intention only, nothing built).** Alan's point: Hex-N is a detector system today, but the layers work on numbers only (keys, a goal as a number, a reward as a signed byte). Adding an INTERPRETIVE layer, an encoder that turns any source into stable keys per channel and a decoder that turns the weighted possible outcomes back into something readable, would let the same path memory, goal comparison and feedback loop be used on non-sensor data. Known limits recorded for when this is taken up: the layer 2 store has 3-bit events (8 classes) and 128 known / 32 candidate slots, layer 3 has no hardware design, and the quality of any abstract use is set by how well the encoder turns the source into a small, stable set of keys; nothing has been tested on a non-sensor source. |
| **Layer 3's feedback applied to layer 2: layer 2 now steers to the goal (Alan, 27 Oct 2026: "apply the feedback from layer 3 into layer 2 and see if it improves the scoring overall"; Python model on the store v3 model, no new RTL, nothing on a card).** `tools/hexn_layer3_v3.py` (extends v2, untouched), `tests/test_hexn_layer3_v3.py` (2 pass). Two things come down from layer 3: the reward of its goal comparison (v2), and the goal itself as one OBS event before each trial whose class is the DESIRED END (5 or 6), so layer 2's history holds what is wanted; layer 2's goal read then uses context 2 (STOREVAL g=2) so key 1 sees (previous end, wanted) and key 2 sees (wanted, key 1). Only existing layer 2 commands are used (OBS, REW, RD, STOREVAL). Synthetic world of the v2 entry. Share reaching the desired end, 10 seeds, trials 40-100 / 100-300 / 600-800: random 0.472 / 0.499 / 0.495; layer 2 rewards only 0.497 / 0.509 / 0.479; layer 2 rewards + goal events, context 2: 0.927 / 0.946 / 0.949; context 3: 0.853 / 0.867 / 0.920; layer 3 alone 0.858 / 0.943 / 0.948. So the feedback lifts layer 2 from chance to layer 3's level, and layer 2 learns faster early because its contexts are small and the noise channel is not in them. Scope: this world needs only the goal and the last key, so a short context is enough; layer 3's advantage is for several channels and longer paths, which this test does not stress; the goal event uses one of the 8 event classes (class 5 or 6, kept apart from the end keys by position, a fact of this world); the reward rule is the teacher's; no sizes or RTL for layer 3. |
| **Tic-tac-toe as a test of layer 2 and layer 3 (Alan, 27 Oct 2026; Python models only, nothing on a card).** `tools/hexn_ttt_v1.py` (agent plays X against random and blocker opponents), `tools/hexn_ttt_selfplay_v1.py` (two learners play each other), raw output `docs/hex-n/sessions/ttt_results_v1.txt`. The machine gets no rules or board: only the move stream as numbers (cell 0-8 = key 0-8, 9 = new game) and a reward at the end (win +64, draw +16, loss -32); 10 percent random moves in training; scored by greedy play on a copy, 400 games, 3 seeds. Layer 2 = the store v3 model with goal read context 1/2/3 (run with 10 key classes, WIDER than the chip's 3-bit events: the 9 cells do not fit the chip's store); layer 3 = two channels (X moves, O moves), path up to 5 snapshots. Win / draw / loss after 3,000 training games vs a RANDOM opponent: random X 0.61/0.12/0.27; layer 2 context 1 0.74/0.08/0.18, context 2 0.73/0.11/0.16, context 3 0.70/0.11/0.19; layer 3 0.94/0.03/0.03; perfect player 0.99/0.01/0.00. Vs the BLOCKER (wins or blocks when it can, else random): random X 0.06/0.23/0.71; layer 2 context 1 0.02/0.56/0.41, context 2 0.11/0.71/0.18, context 3 0.15/0.80/0.05; layer 3 0.53/0.37/0.10; perfect 0.89/0.12/0.00. Reading: layer 3's longer path beats layer 2's 3-key context by a wide margin on winning (0.94 vs 0.74 and 0.53 vs 0.15); layer 2 with the longest context learns to avoid losing (draws 0.80, loss 0.05) but rarely wins. The known table of the layer 2 model reached 1,122 entries after 3,000 games against 128 on the chip. SELF-PLAY (3,000 games per pair, one pass, as budgeted): it did NOT reach perfect play. Drawn share in games 2,501-3,000 with exploration: layer 2 0.14, layer 3 0.61; both then greedy: 0.00 for both (greedy play is nearly deterministic, so this is only a few distinct games per seed); trained X vs random 0.72/0.12/0.16 (layer 2), 0.67/0.11/0.23 (layer 3); vs blocker 0.01/0.43/0.55 and 0.10/0.23/0.68; trained O vs random X 0.39/0.11/0.50 and 0.32/0.09/0.59. A first self-play run had two bugs in the O-side measurement (random stream restarted every game; cell 0 treated as no move) and its O figures were discarded; the numbers above are from the fixed run. Scope: three seeds, one pass, model-level, move-history contexts (the same board by another move order is learned separately), the reward is the game's, no layer 3 hardware; self-play needs a different method or much longer training before it is a fair test of the idea. |
| **Store v4 model: row per context, presence bits, wider events ("expansion everywhere", Alan, 27 Oct 2026; Python bit-true model only, no RTL yet, nothing on a card).** `tools/hexn_store_v4.py`, `tests/test_hexn_store_v4.py` (2 pass); `hexn_store_v3.py` untouched and used as the reference. Changes: event field EVB bits (default 4, 16 classes; v3 has 3); the known table is rows, one row per context holding [seen, contra, value] for each possible next event; context lengths 1 and 2 are DIRECT-addressed (never collide, no eviction), length 3 is hashed (XOR fold) into ROWS3 rows with the full context kept as a tag, a different context replaces a row only if that row's total seen is lower than the promotion count (otherwise the promotion is dropped and counted in dropk); PRESENCE bits (one per context, one valid bit per hashed row) are read first so a context with no pattern is never walked, and three clear bits give 'novel' with no row read; the rules (verdicts, back-off, best, goal read, N in W promotion, REW and queue) and the candidate table are unchanged. Check: 18 random command workloads (CLR/LOAD/RD/REW/OBS, 3 settings of N, W and goal read context, 1,500 commands each, v3 with 1,024 slots so it never evicts) give IDENTICAL outputs on every command and the same known and candidate counts. Cost on a tic-tac-toe move stream (1,500 games, 10 key classes, ROWS3 1,024, goal read context 2): 436 known entries, per lookup 3.0 presence reads + 39.1 row words = 42.1 word reads, against a v3 scan of every slot (128 at the chip's size, 1,024 or 2,048 if enlarged); 6 promotions dropped at length 3 out of 442 promoted. Scope: counts are word reads, not clocks; the length-3 hash is a first choice not tuned; the row words are 24 bits each (seen, contra, value), so ROWS3=1,024 with 16 events is about 393 kbit, too big for the Tang's block RAM (about 830 kbit total, 4 tiles used) unless ROWS3 is cut to 256 or fewer or the row is stored sparsely, which is the next design decision; RTL and the model-versus-RTL check are not done. |
| **Store v5 model: address INDEX plus dense entry HEAP (Alan, 27 Oct 2026: "the quick lookup becomes a RAM address location table, there's your tokenisation ... only known values are stored, the new ones are added to the table and the main RAM store"; Python bit-true model only, no RTL yet, nothing on a card).** `tools/hexn_store_v5.py`, `tests/test_hexn_store_v5.py` (2 pass); v3 (rules) and v4 (row per context) untouched. Layout: INDEX of 2^IB slots, each empty or holding a context TOKEN (length and last events, kept in full as the tag) plus the address of its first heap row; lookup = hash, probe up to P consecutive slots, first empty slot ends the probe (nothing is deleted); a token not in the index is not known (the index is the presence table). HEAP of HROWS rows of R entry slots (event, seen, contra, value) with a link to a next row; rows handed out by a free pointer, so only contexts that occur use memory. Rules and candidate table as v3; a full index or heap drops the new known entry (counted in dropk). Hash: three shift-and-XOR mixes then an XOR fold (no multiplier); a plain fold alone clustered (47 dropped entries at 2,048 slots with 227 used), the mix gave none. Check: 15 random workloads (3 settings, 5 seeds, 1,500 commands) give IDENTICAL outputs to store v3 on every command and the same known and candidate counts (v5 with 8,192 index slots, probe 16, 4,096 heap rows, so it never runs out). Measured on the tic-tac-toe move stream (1,500 games, 16 event classes, goal read context 2): 525 known entries in 226 index slots and 260 heap rows; IB 10, R 4, HROWS 512, P 4: 9.2 memory reads per lookup, 0 dropped, 89 kbit of RAM (about 5 block-RAM tiles of 18 kbit) against 393 kbit for the v4 layout and a v3 scan of every slot; IB 11 / HROWS 1,024: 9.0 reads, 181 kbit; IB 9 / HROWS 512: 9.3 reads, 10 dropped, 77 kbit. Scope: reads are counted, not clocks; one workload; the token keeps the full context so there are no false matches; the heap has no deletion or compaction (a full heap stops adding; compaction and saving the table to flash or SD would be side work for the BL616 firmware, not designed or checked); RTL and the model-versus-RTL check are not done. |
| **Store v5 RTL: address index plus dense heap, wide events; bit-true against its model in simulation (27 Oct 2026; nothing on a card, not yet connected to the core).** `rtl/hexn_store_v5.v`, `rtl/tb_hexn_store_v5.v`, `tools/hexn_store_rtl_check_v3.py`, `tests/test_hexn_store_rtl_v3.py` (refuses to pass without iverilog; with the model and v4 tests 6 pass in 18 s). Parameters: event bits EVB (4), index address bits IB (10), heap row bits RB (9), slots per row RSB (2, so 4), probe length P (4), candidate slots CCAP (32); rules, candidate table, reward queue and commands as store v3 (0 CLR, 1 OBS, 2 LOAD, 3 RD, 4 REW); same hash and probe order and row allocation order as the model, so the memories match slot for slot. Every memory read takes 3 clocks (address, wait, use). Check (iverilog against `tools/hexn_store_v5.py`): four cases, every command's outputs, the counters and the whole index, row headers, entry words and candidate table compared after each run: wide_random (2,501 commands, 16 classes), tiny_forced_drops (1,501 commands, index 16 slots, probe 2, 8 heap rows of 2: 366 dropped entries all matched), tictactoe_stream (1,779 commands), small_cand_n3 (2,001 commands, 4 candidate slots): 0 mismatches in all four. Slowest command: 370 clocks (an OBS) in the wide case against about 757 for the v3 store at 128 and 32 slots, with 537 known entries stored instead of at most 128. Bugs found by the check and fixed: a part-select past the end of the probe counter gave an unknown index address (every lookup failed at IB 10); a retrieve before any event (empty history) must answer 'novel' and not scan. Yosys synth_gowin of the module alone (EVB 4, IB 10, RB 9, RSB 2, CCAP 32): 14 block-RAM blocks including the duplicate read ports used only for the dump (about half that for the real design, to be confirmed), 46 small distributed-RAM blocks, about 980 flip-flops; no LUT figure (the sandbox's ABC step does not give one); the real LUT, block-RAM and clock figures need the place-and-route run with the store inside the core. Scope: not connected to core v8 (core v9 and board top v13 are the next step), no card test, no BL616 firmware, no compaction or save to flash. |

Tang Nano 20K, nextpnr: LUT4 12,828/20,736 (62%), DFF 2,856, BSRAM 14/46, 77.2 MHz (target 27), against core v8's 12,715 LUT4 / 4 BSRAM / 70.7 MHz. Bitstream `hexn_tang18_core_v9.fs`; core v8 (`hexn_tang17_core_v8.fs`) stays the working fallback. | **Core v9 (store v5) ran on the Tang Nano 20K and passed (Alan, 27 Oct 2026, 18:25).** Bitstream `hexn_tang18_core_v9.fs`, `python3 tools/hexn_store_card_v3.py docs/hex-n/frontend/example_sensors_script_v1.txt --port /dev/ttyUSB1 --train 14 --test 14`: 285 frames (train 141, test 144), 121 events fed, 61 answers checked, 15 rewards sent, store known 71, candidates 31, promoted 1, 15 distinct non-zero values; every index slot, header, entry and candidate word and every answer matched the bit-true model `hexn_store_v5.py`. | **Store v5 capacity and drop test, simulation (Alan, 27 Oct 2026: "test for the drop values, then move into the layer 3 side"; nothing on the board yet).** `tools/hexn_store_cap_v1.py` host-LOADs random events straight into the store (command 0x1A) on core v9 and checks every answer, the five counters (known, candidates, promoted, dropk, dropc) and, at the end, 200 read-backs plus the index, row header, entry and candidate words against the bit-true model. Simulation, 170 events, every 8th table row compared: 452 known entries, 321 of 512 rows and 299 of 1,024 index slots used, **1 known entry dropped (dropk 1)**; card and model agree on every answer, counter and compared word. STORE CAPACITY CHECK PASS. This crosses the first drop, but the heap-full case (512 rows, model: about event 450, then thousands of drops) is only reachable on the board or in a much longer simulation. Board command (freshly loaded bitstream): `python3 tools/hexn_store_cap_v1.py --port /dev/ttyUSB1 --events 2000`. In observe mode random events promote almost nothing (candidate table of 32 thrashes), which is why the test loads. Layer 3 sizing and hardware plan: `docs/hex-n/LAYER3_HW_v1.md`, `tools/hexn_layer3_size_v1.py`. |
| **Forgetting rule and memory tiers: design intention (Alan, 27 Oct 2026; no model or RTL yet).** Once the memory is full, patterns that fade are not simply deleted, because layer 3 needs its long-range patterns. Proven patterns spill to a slower tier and can be recalled: Tang = BSRAM then the SD card (Alan has a small SD card for it; via the BL616 firmware, deferred until a second Tang); Kintex = no SD slot, so local BRAM (fast), main RAM (slower), JTAG (slowest); the PCIe route is not available on this card. Details and open checks in `docs/hex-n/LAYER3_HW_v1.md`. Next: a Python model of full-memory rules (drop new, drop oldest, drop lowest confidence, drop lowest but protect valuable, spill to a slow tier) measured on the synthetic world. |
| **Layer 3 full-memory rules, Python model (Alan, 27 Oct 2026: forgetting rule and memory tiers; no RTL, nothing on a card).** `tools/hexn_layer3_forget_v1.py` (extends `hexn_layer3_v1.py`, untouched): memory capped at 500 entries, synthetic coupled world of 3,000 routines plus a RARE routine (remote keys 6 0 3 5, none of whose steps occur elsewhere) about once per 100 routines, 93 rare keys scored. Run `python3 tools/hexn_layer3_forget_v1.py 3000 500 100` (21 s). Remote-key weight on the truth, all keys / rare keys (top-1 for rare in brackets): unbounded 0.864 / 0.514 (0.57); drop the new entry (what store v5 does) 0.868 / 0.474 (0.484); drop the oldest 0.852 / 0.312 (0.355); drop the lowest faded weight 0.867 / 0.195 (0.183); protect entries seen 3 or more times 0.866 / 0.220 (0.258); spill the lowest weight to a slow tier 0.864 / 0.514 (0.57), identical to unbounded, at 144,500 slow reads over 11,942 moments (about 12 per moment). Findings: (1) common patterns fit in 500 entries, so all rules score about the same overall; (2) the rare pattern is what forgetting damages, and rules that evict by weight hit it hardest, because a rare pattern always has a low faded weight; (3) refusing new entries keeps the rare pattern better than the evicting rules only because the memory freezes on early traffic, which would not hold if habits changed (not tested); (4) a slow tier loses nothing, but its read count is high for an SD card, so recall needs to be cheaper (cache recalled contexts, bring a whole context back in one read, use a presence bit to skip the slow tier for contexts that were never spilled). Limits: synthetic stationary world, whole-memory victim search (hardware would look at a few nearby entries), slow tier unlimited and free. |
| **Tang SD card is driven by the FPGA directly (Alan, 27 Oct 2026, sent the NESTang SD loader module; read from the file only, nothing built).** The loader's ports are `sd_clk`, `sd_cmd`, `sd_dat0` and `sd_dat1..3` tied to 1 (SD-native 1-bit mode, FPGA as host), around an `sd_reader` module that reads one 512-byte sector at a time from a 24-bit sector number. So the SD tier does not need the BL616 firmware. The file has no write path, so spilling to the card needs a new SD writer; `sd_reader` source and licence not yet checked. Spilled contexts should fit one sector so a recall is one read. Details in `docs/hex-n/LAYER3_HW_v1.md`. |
| **SD reader source read (Alan, 27 Oct 2026, sent `sd_reader` and `sdcmd_ctrl`; read only, nothing built or run).** Single-block reads (CMD17) in 1-bit mode after the standard init (CMD0, 8, 55/41, 2, 3, 7, 16); no write path; data CRC16 and response CRC not checked; SD clock = core clock / 6 or / 8 depending on CLK_DIV (derived, not measured), about 1 to 1.5 ms per 512-byte sector. Licence of the source project not known (page unreachable from this session): check before copying. Writer (CMD24, CRC16, busy wait) and a read-back integrity check are still to be built. Details in `docs/hex-n/LAYER3_HW_v1.md`. |
| **SDRAM controller source read (Alan, 27 Oct 2026, sent nand2mario's `sdram` module; read only, nothing built or run).** Tang Nano 20K embedded SDRAM: 64 Mbit, 32 bits wide, 8 MiB, byte addressed, 5 cycles per access, 4-cycle read latency, up to 66.7 MHz; needs a 180-degree shifted clock from a PLL and a refresh command about every 15 us; writes are byte-wise. Proposed Tang tiers: BSRAM -> SDRAM (spill tier, about 2 million 32-bit words) -> SD card (save/reload, about 1 ms per sector). Licence unknown. Details in `docs/hex-n/LAYER3_HW_v1.md`. |
| **Licence of the Sipeed NESTang SD/SDRAM example is GPL v3 (Alan sent the licence file, 27 Oct 2026; not legal advice).** Copying that code into our tree and conveying the result would pull the combined work under GPL v3; private use on our own boards would not. The SD reader (WangXuan95) and SDRAM controller (nand2mario) have their own authors and licences, unknown. Nothing has been copied into the repository. Decision for Alan: write our own SD and SDRAM controllers from the public specs, or accept GPL v3 for a separate module. Details in `docs/hex-n/LAYER3_HW_v1.md`. |

| **Two board tests built (Alan, 27 Oct 2026, 19:38: "complete both").** (1) SDRAM self-test v2 (`rtl/hexn_tang_sdramtest_v2.v`, build `tools/hexn_tang_sdramtest_build_v2.sh`): scans read delay 1-6 plus a negative control, report over the serial port. (2) SD tier test (`rtl/hexn_tang_sdtest_v1.v` + `.cst`, build `tools/hexn_tang_sdtest_build_v1.sh`): reuses the proven `fpga/verilog/sd_spi_v1.v` unchanged; writes then reads back 8 raw blocks at three places on the card (block 4,096, 50,000,000, 100,000,000, so it also proves block addressing past 4 GB on the 64 GB SDXC card), counts mismatches and times the phases, serial report plus LEDs. Open flow: 1,856 LUT4, bitstream `/tmp/hexn_tang/sdtest1/hexn_tang_sdtest_v1.fs`. Simulation against the behavioural card: passes (small block numbers because the model holds 64 blocks); `tests/test_hexn_sdram_v1.py` now has 4 tests, all pass. **It overwrites 24 raw blocks of the card.** Neither is run on a board yet. Alan's point recorded as a design direction, not yet modelled: with proven long-term patterns held on the card, the FPGA keeps only the short-term reference table in BSRAM. Caveat noted for later: the card read costs about 1 ms, so the chip still needs a small on-chip index or presence bit to know whether a pattern is on the card at all; otherwise every miss costs a card read. |
| **Board results, 27 Oct 2026 (Alan, serial captures; both tests ran on the Tang Nano 20K).** **SD tier test: PASS.** Card ready, all three regions (block 4,096; 50,000,000; 100,000,000) written and read back with 0 mismatches on the 64 GB SDXC card using `sd_spi_v1` unchanged, so block addressing past 4 GB works and no reformatting was needed. Timing from the reported clock counts at 27 MHz (8 blocks each): read about 4.7 to 4.8 ms, i.e. 0.58 to 0.61 ms per 512-byte block (about 0.85 MB/s); write 11.0 to 15.7 ms, i.e. 1.4 to 2.0 ms per block. Occasional dropped characters in the serial text are host-side (repeats of the same line are complete). **SDRAM self-test v2: our controller works on the real embedded SDRAM.** Read-back of 256 words: read delay 1 fails (d0=FFFFFFFF, bus not yet driven), delays 2, 3, 4 and 5 all 0 errors with d0=9E3779B1 (correct), delay 6 fails (d0=BEB7FFB3, not the written word); the negative control (delay 3 compared with the wrong pattern) shows 256 errors with the correct word read, so the test does discriminate. So the clean window is 4 settings wide (2 to 5), against 1 (setting 3) in the simulation. Unexplained: with burst length 1 the part should drive a word for about one clock, so the later clean settings may be the bus holding the last value rather than valid data; the earliest clean setting (2) is the safer evidence of when data is truly valid. Decision pending Alan: keep the default read delay 3 (inside the window with one clean setting each side). Reference seen on the same board: the factory LiteX demo in flash reports the same SDRAM as 8 MiB, 32-bit, 48 MT/s, CL 2, memtest OK. |
| **Three-tier long-term store cost model (Alan, 27 Oct 2026: yes to modelling it first).** `tools/hexn_layer3_tiers_v1.py` (Python only, clones the spill rule of the forgetting model; run `python3 tools/hexn_layer3_tiers_v1.py 3000`, about 1 minute). Tiers: on-chip table, embedded SDRAM (300 ns per probe, measured), SD card (0.6 ms per probe, measured). Synthetic world, 12,312 moments, about 4,800 entries in all. Findings: (1) **Without a presence filter every moment probes both off-chip tiers: about 14,200 us per moment, nearly all of it the card.** (2) **Moving single entries between tiers is poor:** a context's followers end up in different tiers, so almost every lookup touches the card even with a filter (11,000 to 12,800 us per moment at 800/200-entry SDRAM). (3) **Moving a whole context as one unit plus a counting presence filter (65,536 entries, so fewer than 1% false positives) with SDRAM as the off-chip tier: 4.1 us per moment with a 600-entry on-chip table, 1.1 us at 1,500, 0.1 us at 3,000.** The SDRAM (8 MiB, hundreds of thousands of entries) never fills in this world, so the card is not touched. (4) Forcing a tiny SDRAM tier (200 entries) to push cold contexts to the card is a stress test, not a real configuration: card probes then dominate (5,400 us per moment at 600, 160 us at 3,000). Reading: SDRAM is the long-term tier for the moment-to-moment path; the card is for persistence and the cold tail, saved and restored in bulk, not probed every moment. Not measured: prediction accuracy under whole-context movement; the cost of the filter itself on chip (65,536 counters is 64 K x a few bits, roughly 8 to 16 block RAMs); a real SDRAM probe is a hash lookup (one 8-clock operation per context assumed, followers packed in one word group); synthetic world only. |
| **Accuracy of the tiered long-term store, steady and changing worlds (Alan, 27 Oct 2026: "check the accuracy of the new system").** `tools/hexn_layer3_tiers_acc_v1.py` (run `python3 tools/hexn_layer3_tiers_acc_v1.py 3000 300`, about 1 minute). On-chip capacity 300 entries against about 4,500 (steady) or 7,300 (changing) entries in all. Top-1 hit rate for the next value of channel 0. **Steady world:** unbounded 0.876, tiered (whole contexts to off-chip memory) **0.876, identical**, drop_low 0.877, drop_new (store v5 today) 0.713. **Changing world (habits A-D, then new habits F-I, then A-D again):** unbounded 0.872 / 0.873 / 0.872 in the three phases; **tiered 0.872 / 0.873 / 0.872, identical, including phase 3 (the old habits recalled from off-chip after the memory had been filled with other habits)**; drop_low 0.871 / 0.765 / 0.760 (forgets); **drop_new 0.713 / 0.280 / 0.708 (freezes on the first habits and fails to learn the new ones; this confirms the earlier worry about freezing)**. The tiered rule loses nothing by construction and the measurement agrees. Limits: synthetic world; unlimited off-chip tier; no timing here (see the tiers cost row). |
| **Three-tier long-term store model (Alan, 27 Oct 2026, "yes": model the store spanning SDRAM and SD with a presence bit before any hardware).** `tools/hexn_layer3_tiers_v1.py` (run `python3 tools/hexn_layer3_tiers_v1.py 3000 2048 4000`, about 40 s), `tests/test_hexn_layer3_tiers_v1.py` (1 pass). Tiers: block RAM (fast, a cap), SDRAM (cap), SD card (unlimited); lowest faded weight moves down one tier, used entries come back up. Costs from the board figures: SDRAM probe 300 ns, SD read 0.6 ms, SD write 2 ms. **Results (synthetic world, 3,000 routines, 12,312 moments):** (a) accuracy is the same as the unbounded store (top-1 0.873 against 0.873, rare routine 0.314 against 0.314): spilling down the tiers loses nothing. (b) With a small fast tier (600 entries; the working set is about 4,800) the card is hit constantly: consulting every tier at every lookup costs about 10 ms per moment even with a perfect presence bit (63% of lookups find their key on the card), and without any presence bit about 15 ms. The presence bit then saves only about 35% because most keys really are on the card. (c) A "skip" rule, where a context that already has followers in the fast tier is answered from there with no slow lookup, cuts lookups from 23.6 to 1.6 per moment, with top-1 0.872 and the rare routine 0.294 (from 0.314): a small accuracy cost. Time per moment is then 3.4 ms, most of it card WRITES (16,881 sector writes, entries churning down and up), so batching writes is the next saving. (d) With the Tang's real fast tier (2,048 entries) and 4,000 SDRAM entries, skip mode needs a card read on 0.01 per moment (118 reads in 12,312 moments) and 1,062 writes; presence bits then matter: no bit 6,530 reads, perfect bit 118, 4,096-bit bitmap 194, 16,384-bit 134, 65,536-bit 118 (a few kilobytes of block RAM). Time per moment 0.18 ms with a presence bit against 0.49 ms without. **Not measured:** that all followers of a context fit one sector, write batching, real sensor data, a combined design on the board; the synthetic world's working set is small, so these counts show the mechanism, not the figures a real run will give. Recommendation (not yet decided by Alan): fast tier answers first, SDRAM next, card only for rare patterns behind a hashed presence bitmap of 16,384 to 65,536 bits (2 to 8 KB). |
| **DECISION (Alan, 28 Oct 2026, 04:11): three-level memory for Layer 3.** The long-term patterns go on the card (Tang: SD); the faster table is built directly into the design (block RAM); the next level is the RAM (Tang: the embedded SDRAM; Kintex: card RAM). This is the arrangement modelled in `tools/hexn_layer3_tiers_v1.py` (row above). Still open and not decided: the presence-bitmap size (model suggests 16,384 to 65,536 bits), write batching to the card, and the "skip" rule (answer from the fast table when a context has followers there), which costs a little accuracy on rare patterns. Next step proposed: put the SDRAM tier behind store v5 in simulation. |
| **Creation-time memory settings (Alan, 28 Oct 2026, 13:36 to 13:39).** Bitmap size, write batching and the shortcut are settings chosen when the system is created, some typed in and some calculated, the way the UniCell assembler (`tools/project_assemble_v1.py`) takes options plus a MAN file. Built: `tools/hexn_config_v1.py` + `tests/test_hexn_config_v1.py` (5 pass). It reads the card's MAN file (tiers: `sd_card`, `sdram_embedded`; block RAM `kbits_datasheet`) and the user's choices (fast table entries, entry bytes, lookback, patterns on the card, bitmap factor, shortcut on or off; any derived value can be overridden) and writes `hexn_config.json` (each value with where it came from) and `hexn_config.vh` (Verilog localparams, not yet read by any RTL). Derived: contexts on card = patterns / 2.4 (from the model world: 4,828 entries over 2,033 contexts); bitmap bits = the larger of 4,096 and the next power of two above factor x contexts; entries per sector = 512 / entry bytes; batch sectors = larger of 2 and bitmap bits / 16,384. It checks block RAM against 80% of the card minus the 14 blocks core v9 already uses, and refuses (exit 1) when the settings do not fit. Tang defaults (4,000 patterns on the card): bitmap 65,536 bits, 4 batch sectors, 5 more blocks, fits (22 available). **Corrections to what I told Alan: the bitmap depends on how many contexts sit on the card, not on the pattern bit width; the batch buffer depends on the sector size and entry size, with the number of sectors waiting a separate setting.** Heuristics, not measured: bitmap factor 32, entry size 8 bytes, the batch sector rule, 4,000 patterns. The Kintex MAN file has no block RAM size (`total: null`) and no RAM-tier capacity, so a Kintex run needs `--bram-kbits` and `--ddr-mb` for now; adding both to a new MAN version is open. The default of 20,000 patterns on the card produced a 524,288-bit bitmap that did not fit the Tang, which shows the check works. |

Facts: the design has **two Xilinx DDR3 memory controllers (MIG 7-series), channel 0 at address 0x1_0000_0000 and channel 1 at 0x1_8000_0000, each 2,147,483,648 bytes (2 GiB), ECC ON, 72-bit data (64 + 8 ECC), 15 row bits, 3 bank bits, 1 chip select, 4:1 clock ratio, user-interface clock 133.33 MHz, internal AXI width 512 bits.** It also has a parallel NOR flash controller (AXI EMC), a UART-lite, an I2C temperature sensor (LM73), a PCIe DMA block and a MicroBlaze CPU; the C application tests every memory range (32-, 16- and 8-bit tests) and skips the flash. The zip contains a built bitstream (`top_wrapper.bit`, 18.7 MB) that passed through Vivado; running it needs Xilinx tools and a Xilinx-compatible JTAG cable, which we have not tried. **Uncertain:** a 133.33 MHz user clock at 4:1 means a 533 MHz memory clock (DDR3-1066), not the DDR3-1600 the chips are rated for; the hardware file also lists CAS latency 11, which does not fit 1066, so those two figures are probably generic fields and I would not rely on either until the memory part's settings are read from the MIG project itself (not found in this zip). Consequence for Hex-N: the Kintex RAM tier would run on Xilinx's own controller only inside Vivado; for our open flow a controller still has to be written or borrowed, and it must also calibrate the memory (the vendor block does that itself), which is the hard part. Licence of these files: Xilinx-generated sources carry "SPDX MIT" headers (seen in `memorytest.c`); the rest not reviewed. |

| **Tang RAM tier built in simulation (28 Oct 2026, Alan: "build the tang side").** New files: `rtl/hexn_tier_v1.v` (fast table in block RAM, presence bitmap in block RAM, our SDRAM controller behind them), `rtl/tb_hexn_tier_v1.v`, `tools/hexn_tier_ref_v1.py` (bit-true Python copy), `tests/test_hexn_tier_v1.py` (3 tests, pass). Sizes are parameters (FB, BMB, RAMB, P) that map to the creation-time settings (`HEXN_FAST_ENTRIES`, `HEXN_BITMAP_BITS`, `HEXN_RAM_ENTRIES`); the glue that reads `hexn_config.vh` directly is NOT done yet. **Rules as built:** a put goes to the fast slot if it is empty or holds the same key, otherwise to the SDRAM (bitmap bit set, linear probe of up to P slots, tag word + value word); a get tries the fast slot, then the bitmap (bit clear = answer "not known" with no SDRAM access), then the SDRAM probe. A resident key is never evicted by a newcomer (no promotion yet). **Checked:** against the rule-checking SDRAM model with 0 rule violations, every stored key read back with its latest value (including 1 in 7 updated), never-seen keys never reported found, and all 11 counters (fast/SDRAM puts, fast hits, bitmap skips, SDRAM probes, false alarms, word reads/writes, overflows) EQUAL the Python reference exactly in three settings. **Measured in the test (600 keys, 256 fast slots, 1,024 SDRAM slots, 4,096-bit bitmap):** 424 of 600 puts spilled to the SDRAM, 281 of 301 never-seen lookups were answered by the bitmap alone, 20 were false alarms; with a 16-bit bitmap the bitmap saves far fewer reads (test asserts it). **Honest limits:** (1) with a small SDRAM region and P=4 some puts are dropped (counted as `overflow`, 2 of 600 at RAMB=10/P=8) so a real build must size the SDRAM region and P to keep that at zero or add a spill path; (2) the whole SDRAM is cleared by a sweep after reset (2 words per slot, about 8 clocks each: roughly 0.7 s at 1,048,576 slots, 27 MHz; not measured on the board); (3) synthesis only (yosys synth_gowin, FB 11, BMB 16, RAMB 20, P 8): about 1,100 LUT-class cells, 11 block RAM blocks (4 + 7), and the three hash functions use 6 hardware multipliers (MULT36X36) which may cost more DSP blocks than expected: place-and-route and timing at 27 MHz NOT yet run, nothing built for the board; (4) timing figures in simulation use the model's idealised SDRAM clock. |
| **Tang RAM tier, native-18 version (28 Oct 2026, Alan: the Tang is native 18 bit, scale to use it).** New files (v1 untouched): `rtl/hexn_tier_v2.v`, `rtl/tb_hexn_tier_v2.v`, `tools/hexn_tier_ref_v2.py`, `tests/test_hexn_tier_v2.py` (5 tests pass, counters bit-equal to the Python copy). **Changes:** key width KW (default 18) and value width VW (default 18) are parameters; ONE reversible hash (a KW-bit multiply then an xor-shift) replaces three 32-bit hashes; because the hash is reversible, the fast slot is its low FB bits and only the other KW-FB bits are stored as the tag, so a fast entry is 1 + (KW-FB) + VW = 26 bits at 18/18/11 (v1: 57); the SDRAM start slot is the top RAMB bits of the hash, the bitmap bit is low-slice XOR top-slice; with KW = 18 and RAMB = 20 the SDRAM is simply direct-indexed by the key (the key space, 262,144 keys, is smaller than the slot count). The old widths (24/32) still work and still equal the reference (test). **Measured by synthesis only** (yosys synth_gowin; FB 11, BMB 16, RAMB 20, P 8; no place-and-route yet): block RAM 11 blocks -> 7 (fast table 7 -> 3), multipliers six 36x36 -> one 18x18, LUT4 about 700 -> 600 and carry cells (ALU) 533 -> 472. **What this means:** at the same block-RAM budget the fast table can hold about twice the slots; the price is that the key must fit KW bits (18 gives 2^18 distinct keys) and the value VW bits, so what goes into key and value (context tag + event, and the entry word) is still to be decided against the layer-3 needs. A multiplier-free hash (xor-shift only) is possible and untested. SDRAM entries still use two 32-bit words (hash word + value word); packing key and value into one word is not done. Not on a board. |
| **Tang RAM tier board test built (28 Oct 2026, Alan: run place and route so he can load it on the card).** New files: `rtl/hexn_tang_tiertest_v1.v/.cst` (board top: PLL, the native-18 tier, a self-checking sequence, serial report of 16 values), `rtl/tb_hexn_tang_tiertest_v1.v`, `tests/test_hexn_tiertest_v1.py` (2 tests pass: report clean and counters equal the Python reference; with a deliberately tiny SDRAM region puts are dropped and show only as 'known key not found', never as wrong values), `tools/hexn_tang_tiertest_build_v1.sh`, `tools/hexn_tang_tiertest_check_v1.py` (reads a serial capture and compares with the reference). **Place and route, open flow, GW2AR-LV18QN88C8/I7:** routed, timing met (103 MHz achievable against the 27 MHz target); 2,211 LUT4 (10.7%), 7 of 46 block RAM, 3 18x18 multipliers and 1 36x36 (the 36x36 is the test's own key arithmetic, not the tier), 1 PLL. **Board settings:** key 18 bits, value 18 bits, 2,048 fast slots, 65,536-bit bitmap, SDRAM region 2^18 slots (direct-indexed, no collisions), 6,000 keys, 3,000 never-seen keys. **Expected serial result (from the Python reference):** fast_put 2248, sd_put 4610, overflow 0, fast_hit 1965, skip 2957, sd_get 4078, fp 43, sd_rd 12723, sd_wr 9220, found 6000, and R0 = 0; the clear sweep (R4) should be about 0.17 s (roughly 4.7 million clocks, an estimate not a measurement). **Not yet run on the board; this is the first run.** The simulation uses an ideal SDRAM clock; the board needs the read-delay setting 3 (the clean window found on the board earlier was 2 to 5). Bitstream: `/tmp/hexn_tang/tiertest1/hexn_tang_tiertest_v1.fs` (not in the repository). |
| **Tang RAM tier BOARD RESULT: PASS (Alan, 28 Oct 2026, 15:13, first run of `hexn_tang_tiertest_v1`).** Serial capture stored verbatim: `docs/hex-n/sources/tang_tiertest_v1_board_capture_28oct.txt` (repeated reports; the tail has a few garbled characters from the capture being stopped mid-line, ignored; one clean report checked). **Result:** errors 0 (no wrong value, no known key lost, no never-seen key found); all 10 tier counters EQUAL the Python reference exactly (fast_put 2248, sd_put 4610, overflow 0, fast_hit 1965, skip 2957, sd_get 4078, fp 43, sd_rd 12723, sd_wr 9220, found 6000), checked with `tools/hexn_tang_tiertest_check_v1.py`. **Measured:** the SDRAM clear sweep took 4,928,566 clocks = 0.183 s at 27 MHz (my estimate was about 4.7 million); the whole test (15,857 operations: 6,000 puts, 858 updates, 6,000 known gets, 3,000 never-seen gets; keys and values by arithmetic) took 281,234 clocks = 10.4 ms, about 17.7 clocks (0.66 microseconds) per operation on average. **What this shows:** the three levels (fast table in block RAM, presence bitmap, embedded SDRAM through our own controller) work together on the real board with read delay 3, and the bitmap kept 2,957 of 3,000 never-seen lookups away from the SDRAM. **What it does not show:** nothing about accuracy of learning (keys and values are arithmetic, not real patterns); the SDRAM region used was the direct-indexed 2^18 slots, so probing past the first slot was never exercised on the board (it is in simulation); no writes batched, no SD card level behind it yet; refresh/long-idle behaviour over minutes not tested here (the reports ran repeatedly for tens of seconds only). |
| **SD card level integrated (simulation only), 28 Oct 2026 (Alan: "start the integration of the sd card side").** New files: `tools/hexn_tier_ref_v3.py` (bit-true Python model; its header states the rules), `rtl/hexn_tier_v3.v` (v2 is untouched and still the board-tested one), `rtl/tb_hexn_tier_v3.v`, `tests/test_hexn_tier_v3.py` (3 tests pass, about 70 s). **Rules chosen here (hardware-simple, NOT the weight-based rules of `hexn_layer3_tiers_v1.py`, which search whole tiers):** every key lives in exactly one level; FAST (block RAM, direct-mapped by the hash) -> SDRAM (direct-mapped victim level) -> CARD (512-byte sectors of 64 entries, sector chosen by the hash, any free entry). A new or promoted key takes its fast slot and the resident moves down; the SDRAM occupant moves down to its card sector; a GET that finds the key below PROMOTES it; a PUT removes any lower copy first. A 4-bit presence counter per key bucket (saturating at 15, then sticky) says whether the card can hold the key; a zero answers 'not known' without a card read. The card is reached through ONE write-back sector buffer (a changed sector is written only when another sector is wanted, or on FLUSH) and a 'sector ever written' bit per sector (a never-written sector reads as empty without touching the card). The card's sector overflow DROPS the entry and counts it (`sd_full`). **Checked:** the Python model equals a plain dictionary over 6,000 random operations when no sector overflows; the RTL (with the real `sd_spi_v1` against the behavioural card, and the SDRAM against its rule-checking model) returns every value correctly and ALL 17 counters equal the Python model exactly, in 1,500-operation runs and in two smaller settings including one with a deliberately tiny card region where sectors overflow (counted, never wrong values). **Measured by counting in the 1,500-operation run (tiny fast table of 32 slots, so most traffic goes down; NOT a realistic hot-set workload):** 1,151 card reads and 1,124 card writes for 1,500 operations; the one-sector buffer was hit only 242 times, so **random keys get almost no batching from a single buffer**; at the board figures (0.6 ms read, 2 ms write) that run would need about 3 s of card time. **Synthesis only** (FB 11, BMB 14, RAMB 18, SB 16): about 2,100 LUT cells, 11 block-RAM blocks plus the sector buffer, one 18x18 multiplier; place-and-route NOT run. **Honest limits:** no board run; the sector 'written' bits sit in block RAM so SB is limited to about 16 (32 MB of card) in this version; promotion/demotion costs several SDRAM and card operations per access (not yet timed on a board); no batching beyond the one buffer: the next step is a write queue or log-style appends grouped by sector (needs a design decision on where the key-to-sector index lives); the card region is overwritten (spare card needed); persistence across power-off is not yet used (the SDRAM and fast levels start empty after reset, the card keeps its sectors but the counters and 'written' bits are cleared, so old card contents are NOT re-found: a restore step is not built). |
| **Batching the card writes: model result and decision (28 Oct 2026, Alan: "batching first").** New files: `tools/hexn_batch_model_v1.py` (three card-side strategies on one synthetic workload: 100,000 keys, 200,000 operations, Zipf popularity, 30 percent puts, popular set drifting, fast table 256 slots and SDRAM 1,024 slots scaled down so the card is used; card read 0.6 ms, write 2.0 ms; compares strategies only, does not predict the real workload) and `tools/hexn_tier_ref_v4.py` (bit-true model of the chosen design). **Result:** S0 one write-back sector buffer (the v3 rule) 84,414 reads + 60,436 writes = 171.5 s card time (0.86 ms per operation); S1 write queue grouped by sector, 1,024 entries 128 s, 8,192 entries 53 s; **S2 log with exact index 18,868 reads + 638 writes = 12.6 s (0.06 ms per operation), about 14 times faster than S0.** All checked against a plain dictionary first. **Design chosen (v4):** the card level becomes a LOG: demoted entries are appended to an open 64-entry sector in block RAM and written as one whole block when full (no read first); an exact INDEX in RAM, one word per key hash (sector id mod L and entry number), replaces the presence counters and the sector scan (no false alarms; 2^18 words = 1 MiB of the SDRAM for 18-bit keys); the card is a circular log of L = 2^LB blocks; space is reclaimed by compaction (read the oldest block, re-append the entries whose index word still points at them); a one-block read buffer remembers the last block read. If the live entries would exceed CAP = (L - 4 - 2) x 64 a demotion is dropped and counted (log_full), so L must be sized for the live data: with the test workload (21,234 live card entries, about 332 blocks) L = 2,048 blocks (1 MB) and 512 blocks gave compaction cost of 0 and 229 block reads and 13.0 s total, L = 256 blocks dropped 6,618 entries and cost 17.5 s. **Not built yet in RTL** (the reference `hexn_tier_ref_v4.py` exists and equals a dictionary in three sizes with compaction active, 40,000 operations each). **Costs and limits to keep in mind:** reads dominate (about 18,500 random block reads at 0.6 ms: the floor for random lookups on a card); the index sits in RAM so it is lost at power-off (a restore would rescan the log: not built); a flush writes the partly filled open sector in place; the SD card's own write wear is not modelled (a circular log spreads writes evenly over L blocks, which helps). |
| **Log-structured card level built in simulation (tier v4), 28 Oct 2026.** New files: `rtl/hexn_tier_v4.v`, `rtl/tb_hexn_tier_v4.v`, `tests/test_hexn_tier_v4.py` (3 tests pass, about 60 s); v2 and v3 untouched. Implements exactly `tools/hexn_tier_ref_v4.py`: open 64-entry sector buffer, whole-block writes, exact index in the SDRAM (word per key hash), one-block read buffer, compaction, drop-and-count when the log is too small. **Checked:** with the real `sd_spi_v1` against the behavioural card and the SDRAM against its rule model, every returned value is correct and ALL 19 counters equal the Python model exactly in three settings, including a log of 8 blocks where compaction runs (11 block reads, 214 entries moved) and 47 entries are dropped and counted. **Measured by counting, same 1,500-operation sequence as the v3 run (tiny fast table, so most traffic goes down):** card blocks read 325 and written 13 (v3: 1,151 read and 1,124 written), so card time falls from about 3 s to about 0.2 s for that run. **Synthesis only** (FB 11, RAMB 18, LB 12): about 1,800 LUT cells, 3 block-RAM blocks for the fast table plus the two small sector buffers, one 18x18 multiplier; place-and-route NOT run; no board test top yet. **Limits:** the index is in the SDRAM so it is lost at power-off; there is no block header or sequence number on the card yet, so after a power cycle the log cannot be re-found (a restore needs the block format extended: see the card-size discussion); card start-up clears 2^RAMB tag words and 2^KW index words (about 0.27 s at KW 18, RAMB 18, simulated start-up not measured on the board). |
| **Card format proposal: models, datasets, save/restore, larger keys, bitstreams (28 Oct 2026, Alan 16:33 and after).** Alan: the key space must be larger; long runs lead to the index being cached onto the card (that is where save comes in); models and datasets must be kept apart, which leads to a directory structure; asked whether bitstreams can be loaded from the SD card so the bitstream and its associated data are files. New document `docs/hex-n/CARD_FORMAT_v1.md` (PROPOSAL, nothing built; open questions listed in it). **Prior art found and applied:** the UniCell native filesystem notes (`archeology/full-cell/docs/core/NATIVE_FS.md`, June 2026) rule out a directory tree and use self-describing blocks with the index rebuilt from the block headers; the proposal keeps that and uses a one-level catalogue of volumes (model + dataset), log blocks that carry volume id and sequence number, two alternating index checkpoint slots, and a files region holding the config, the bitstream and captures. **Found while thinking it through:** (1) a promotion leaves the old log entry on the card, so a replay after a crash would resurrect stale values; promotions must append a tombstone (not in v4); (2) the log block needs a header, so 62 entries per block instead of 64. **Bitstream from the card, answered from the Gowin guide UG206 (fetched 28 Oct 2026):** the GW2AR-18 QN88 package supports only JTAG and MSPI flash boot (slave modes SSPI/SERIAL/CPU are marked unsupported for it); RECONFIG_N reloads from the flash, not from the card; so the Tang cannot boot from the SD card directly. A loader design that copies a file from the card into the board flash is a possible indirect route but is NOT verified. Alan's answers to the two questions (structure, key width) were: structure unanswered (he asked about bitstreams instead), key width no preference. |
| **Front end / boot shell idea (Alan, 28 Oct 2026, 16:41) and feasibility check.** Alan: on boot a front end loads; it copies a bitstream from the SD card into the flash space and loads it with its data; moves files between the card and the host both ways; creates new bitstream folders on the card; after a task a simple reboot returns to the front end. New document `docs/hex-n/FRONT_END_v1.md` (PROPOSAL; nothing built). **Checked this session:** board flash is 64 Mbit (8 MB) so about 8 of our 907 KB bitstreams fit; flash pins 59 to 62 can be released to user logic by the open flow (`gowin_pack --mspi_as_gpio`); RECONFIG_N is package pin 9 per the chip database, but whether it is reachable on the board is not in our board files; the chip has no multi-boot, always boots from the start of the flash. **Key consequence:** copying a task bitstream to the start of the flash replaces the front end, so a plain reboot would load the task again; recommended solution: link a standard front-end block into every build so every reset starts in front-end mode. First build step proposed: a read-only flash probe plus a guarded erase/program/verify test in the upper half of the flash, with the controller refusing writes below 4 MB. | 28 Oct 2026 | **Card format and log v5, Python reference built and tested (`tools/hexn_tier_ref_v5.py`, `tests/test_hexn_tier_v5.py`, 7 tests, about 100 s; v4 untouched; no RTL yet).** Implements `CARD_FORMAT_v1.md` as real bytes: 512-byte log block = 16-byte header (volume id, 40-bit sequence, count, version 1, magic 0x4858, CRC16-CCITT) + 62 entries of 8 bytes; live and tombstone entries; block position = (sequence - 1) mod L. **Tombstone** appended on every promotion from the card (GET or PUT); compaction drops tombstones; `save()` drains the fast and SDRAM levels into the log and flushes, and returns how many entries did not fit; `restore()` rebuilds the index from the card alone by scanning all L block headers then replaying valid blocks oldest first (reads = L + valid blocks). **Measured by the tests:** one flipped bit anywhere in a block is caught; dictionary behaviour holds through compaction (tombstones written and dropped); after save, power loss and restore every key comes back with its latest value; after a power loss at arbitrary points (four runs) the restored state equals exactly the index at the last durable block write; a torn block write (four runs) is caught as one bad block and the state falls back to the previous durable point; an over-full save reports the loss and never returns wrong values. **Mutation check:** with tombstones switched off the crash test fails (stale values come back), so it does test them; the clean-save test still passes without them, as expected (no delete operation exists, and a clean save writes fresh newest entries). **Findings:** LOW must be at least 3 (enforced) so a torn write can only destroy entries that exist elsewhere; after a crash a key that was demoted to the card comes back with its last durable value, which can be older than the value it had in RAM; keys only in RAM at the crash are absent; a power loss during a rewrite of an already-flushed partial block loses that block's content (not earlier blocks), so the torn test avoids flush. **Not built:** the RTL for v5; the index checkpoint (restore by scan costs L + valid reads, 2.5 to 5 s at L = 4,096); volume catalogue; hashed index for wider keys. |

### Kintex memory model: sizing check (29 Oct 2026, after Alan's confirmation)
Alan confirmed the three levels (LUT table, block RAM, DDR3 bucket lines of 8) and that the Tang work is the same design on a different target. `tools/hexn_tier_ref_v3_kintex_check_v1.py` runs the existing Tier3 reference with SPS=8 (fast = LUT, mid = BRAM, bottom = DDR3 lines). Measured, random keys: 100,000 keys (KW 24, FB 10, RAMB 14, SB 17) and 1,000,000 keys (KW 26, SB 20): 0 wrong values, 0 entries dropped (sd_full 0), 200,000 gets all found. Python model only, no RTL yet. Observation: with random keys the small top levels rarely hit (fast_hit 2,043 of 200,000 at 100k keys), so most traffic goes to DDR3; the levels pay off only for repeated/hot keys. Next: hexn_tier_v7 (BRAM mid level, line-wide DDR port) checked against this reference.

A DDR3 answer that does not come within 4096 clocks sets the sticky `ddr_err` (not exercised by a test yet).

Not placed or routed, not run on the card.

### Kintex tier: JTAG command set (29 Oct 2026, 13:00-14:00)
Alan: "continue with the jtag command set". The existing Hex-N JTAG link already carries a reliable byte stream (160-bit frames, CRC16, resend), so the command set is a byte protocol on top of it, not a new link.
New files: `rtl/hexn_tier_v8.v` (clone of v7, v7 untouched, plus 3 read-only debug ports: level-0 slot, level-1 slot, line-written bit), `rtl/hexn_tier7_cmd_v1.v` (byte command layer, contains the tier; shares the Wishbone port with its own line read while the tier is idle), `tools/hexn_tier7_host_v1.py` (builders, parsers, `Tier7Dev`, `dump_all()` which flushes, reads both upper levels and every written DDR3 line and saves JSON), testbenches `rtl/tb_hexn_tier7_cmd_v1.v` and `rtl/tb_hexn_tier7_jtag_v1.v`, tests `tests/test_hexn_tier7_cmd_v1.py`, `tests/test_hexn_tier7_jtag_v1.py`.
Commands (all little-endian; command list in the header of the cmd file): 0x40 PUT, 0x41 GET, 0x42 FLUSH, 0x43 COUNTERS (17 x 4 bytes), 0x44 INFO (sizes, so the host knows what it is talking to), 0x45 LEVEL (one entry of level 0 or 1), 0x46 LINE (flush, then one DDR3 line: 8 entries). Unknown opcode answers 0xEE. Rule: the host reads a whole answer before sending the next command (Tier7Dev does).
Measured (simulation): byte-level test 2 + 1 tests pass: replies of PUT/GET, counters, every entry of both upper levels and every DDR3 line equal `Tier3` (SPS 8), also with a slow DDR3 and full lines. Whole chain (JtagLink with CRC/resend -> hexn_jtag_bridge_v1 -> cmd layer -> tier -> DDR3 stand-in): 150 steps clean and 60 steps with injected bit errors (30 per mille) both give the model's answers and a dump_all equal to the model's contents (the 2 chain tests take 327 s).
Found while testing: a GET that misses returned the stale rdata register; the layer now returns 0 for a miss (fixed in the new file before it was ever committed).
NOT done: restore from a dump (writing a saved store back; it needs the presence counters and written bits rebuilt, so it is a tier feature, not just a command), the top level that joins this to the UberDDR3 build and the BSCANE2 chain choice on the OptiPlex (the DDR3 memtest uses USER1; this layer's bridge must use another chain, e.g. HEXN_CHAIN=2 in OpenOCDExec), a run on the card, an LUT count (synthesis running; not yet read).
