### Layer 4 test world: Connect Four (29 Oct 2026, Alan: "expand the game to connect4, larger grid, gravity, so it needs a degree of planning, strategy")
Decision (Alan): the first test world for layer 4, the strategist, is Connect Four (7 x 6, a counter drops to the lowest free place). Intention only for layer 4; nothing of layer 4 itself is built or modelled.
New: `tools/hexn_connect4_v1.py` (the world: board, gravity, 69 winning lines, players: random, blocker, alpha-beta lookahead(depth) as a stand-in strategist), `tests/test_hexn_connect4_v1.py` (4 pass). The machine's view stays as in tic-tac-toe: a stream of column numbers 0..6 plus 7 = new game, i.e. 8 classes = the 3-bit events of the layer 2 store (tic-tac-toe needed 10).
Measured (20 games as X and 20 as O each, seed 0, so 40 per row; small samples): blocker vs random 37 wins, 1 draw, 2 losses (mean 20.1 moves); lookahead(2) vs random 40-0-0; lookahead(2) vs blocker 37-0-3; lookahead(4) vs blocker 40-0-0; lookahead(4) vs lookahead(2) 21 wins, 2 draws, 17 losses (mean 32.5 moves). Reading: one-move tactics (win/block) already beat random; looking 4 plies ahead beats the blocker every time; deeper looking over shallow gives only a modest edge in these 40 games (not significant). Games run about 18 to 33 moves, so layer 3's path memory of 5 snapshots sees only the last few moves.
Still open for layer 4 (asked 14:37, not yet answered): goal and state as keys or something bigger; steps invented or only chosen from steps already seen to work; behaviour when a step is not reached; how much of layer 3 is already tested.

### Layer 4 design intentions, answers from Alan (29 Oct 2026, 14:45; INTENTIONS ONLY, layer 4 not built or modelled)
Alan's answers to the four open questions, in his words in summary:
- The GOAL is set at the start; there are "win" conditions. Layers 2 and 3 are the existing machinery; the question is whether they can predict or shape moves far enough ahead to reach the goal.
- Layer 4 matters where there is more than one possibility and the number of possibilities grows exponentially. Layer 4 PLANS the possible choices, then, using the existing levels, builds a PATH to a possible point of success (the goal). It sets a STEP target; layer 3 does most of the work of reaching that step; then layer 4 plans the next target.
- GETTING STUCK: because layer 4 has planned the possibilities, it can PRUNE the step tree at each point and move on with the next level of goal steps.
- Order: layer 3 must first be tested to see what it can do, before layer 4 is planned. If layer 4 then shows a better improvement, it becomes the next layer up.
- Test world: Connect Four (previous row).
Reading (mine, to be confirmed): steps are targets layer 4 chooses in a tree it builds itself (not only steps already seen to work); a step counts as reached when layer 3 reports a state matching it; a step that cannot be reached is pruned and the tree continues from another branch.

### Connect Four baseline: layers 2 and 3 alone (29 Oct 2026, ~15:00; Python, no RTL)
`tools/hexn_connect4_l23_v1.py` (run: `python3 tools/hexn_connect4_l23_v1.py 2 1000 100`), clone of the tic-tac-toe harness; layer 2 = hexn_store_v3, layer 3 = hexn_layer3_v2, both unchanged. X plays first, learns with 10 % random moves, then greedy play of 100 games on a copy; mean of 2 seeds [win / draw / loss] after 250 / 500 / 1000 training games.
vs RANDOM: random X 0.52 / 0 / 0.48; layer 2 context 1: 0.64, 0.74, 0.61 wins; context 2: 0.62, 0.64, 0.58; context 3: 0.66, 0.68, 0.70; layer 3 (path 5): 0.74, 0.82, 0.82 wins (loss 0.17 at 1000).
vs BLOCKER (wins if it can, else blocks, else random): random X 0.05 wins; layer 2 all contexts about 0.03 to 0.11 wins, no clear learning; layer 3: 0.08, 0.14, 0.32 wins (loss 0.69 at 1000), still rising at 1000 games. lookahead(2) and lookahead(4) win 40 of 40 against both.
Reading, with limits: layer 3 clearly learns something in this world (random opponent 0.52 to 0.82, blocker 0.05 to 0.32) and layer 2 alone barely does; layer 3 is far below even a 2-move lookahead. 2 seeds and 100 test games each, so differences of about 0.1 are within noise (random X scored 0.65 in a 40-game run and 0.52 here); the layer 3 trend against the blocker is the one result that looks larger than noise. Not tuned; only 1,000 training games (layer 3 still improving); contexts are move histories, not boards.
Next (not started): longer layer 3 training to see where it levels off; then the first layer 4 model in Python (goal tree of steps, layer 3 as the step executor, pruning), measured against this baseline.

### Layer 4 idea logged and first half tested: layer 3 as the predictor, branch depth by confidence (29 Oct 2026, 15:28 to 15:50; Python only)
Alan (15:24, 15:28): the look-ahead tree is a map of possibilities; the hard part is predicting the most obvious path, where the system is going, from limited information (the board now and the positions it could move to). Layer 3 can build a predictive model from the moves played and, when it meets a position, predict ahead; layer 4 then varies the depth of each branch by the predicted branch confidence, so the loss of data is small on the short paths but they remain relevant if chosen. (INTENTION; layer 4 not built.)
Test of the first half: `tools/hexn_connect4_predict_v1.py` (layer 3 = hexn_layer3_v1 `channel_view`, unchanged; X plays randomly; prequential, each prediction made before the move is learned; run `python3 tools/hexn_connect4_predict_v1.py 3000 1000 2`). Top-1 / top-3 hit rate of layer 3's predicted reply of O, mean of 2 seeds, ~10,000 predictions per window per seed, chance about 0.15:
- RANDOM opponent: 0.15 / 0.43 in all three windows (= chance; nothing to learn, as expected). Calibration flat at 0.14 to 0.16 for every confidence level.
- BLOCKER opponent: 0.16, 0.17, 0.17 top-1 and 0.45, 0.46, 0.47 top-3: barely above chance. Layer 3 does NOT pick up the blocker's win/block rule from move histories (the rule depends on threats on the board, which a move history does not hold unless the same history repeats).
- lookahead(2) opponent: top-1 0.45, 0.51, 0.54 and top-3 0.78, 0.83, 0.84, still rising. Calibration (last window): top weight 0.5 to 0.75 gives 0.42 hit rate (5,434 predictions), top weight above 0.75 gives 0.80 (5,557 predictions); lower weights 0.29 to 0.34.
Reading: layer 3 predicts an opponent whose replies depend on the history it has seen (the deterministic search player), and its confidence number is informative there (high weight, high hit rate). It cannot predict a rule that depends on the board; for such rules (win now, block now) layer 4, which sees the board, has to supply the forced replies itself and use layer 3 only for the remaining choices. Limits: opponent set is three players, X random, 2 seeds; contexts are histories, not boards.

### Layer 4 to layer 3: how the step goal is passed down, and when it is re-evaluated (29 Oct 2026, 15:46 to 15:48; INTENTION, nothing of this built)
Alan asked how the goal is passed back to layer 3. Finding: in the first layer 4 model (`tools/hexn_connect4_layer4_v1.py`) it is NOT passed down at all: layer 4 picks the column itself and layer 3 only supplies predicted replies of the opponent; this is a gap against Alan's design (layer 4 sets a step target and layer 3 does most of the work of reaching it).
Proposed mechanism (Alan: "yes that works"), built from what already exists in `tools/hexn_layer3_v3.py`: (1) layer 4 sends layer 3 the current STEP TARGET as an event on a goal channel, so the target becomes part of layer 3's path memory and its choices are conditioned on it; (2) when the step is reached or fails, layer 4 compares the outcome with the target and sends layer 3 a reward (the compare-and-reward rule of hexn_layer3_v2); (3) layer 3 chooses the moves toward the target; layer 4 watches and decides when a step is done, failed or must change. A step has to be a small key: a short vocabulary of named targets (examples only, not decided: make an open three, block the opponent's three, take the centre column, win); the vocabulary decides how much layer 4 must know about the game and is OPEN.
Re-evaluation rule (Alan): the plan is re-evaluated after each move by the opponent; IF the state of play has not changed in a way that matters, the goal does not need to change. So: after each opponent move layer 4 checks (a) is the current step reached, (b) is it still valid and reachable on the new board; if yes, the same target stays (no new goal event, no replanning cost); if not, it replans: rebuild and prune the tree from the new board and send the next target. To be measured when built: how often the target is kept, and the cost in tree nodes compared with replanning at every move.

### Layer 4 plan reuse (Alan, 29 Oct 2026, 15:50; INTENTION, not built)
The plan is kept between moves: layer 4 already holds the look-ahead tree. If the opponent does what was predicted, layer 4 keeps the subtree under the reply actually played, removes (prunes) the branches not chosen, and extends the surviving branches at the far end instead of rebuilding. (Each full turn, own move plus reply, adds two plies per surviving branch.) A reply that was not expanded (below the confidence cutoff) has no subtree: search fresh from there. Notes (mine): the first model (tools/hexn_connect4_layer4_v1.py) rebuilds the tree at every move (about 6,000 positions per move against the random opponent, measured) and does no reuse; the reuse rate should follow layer 3's prediction accuracy; values at the old far end are estimates and must be replaced by deeper ones and backed up the tree; layer 3's stored probabilities go stale as it learns (refresh at the frontier). Possible hardware reading (undecided): the tree is keys and values (position key to value and confidence), the kind of data the Kintex memory tier stores. To measure when built: positions looked at per move with and without reuse, and the share of replies that were already in the tree.

### Connect Four baseline, longer training (29 Oct 2026, ~16:00; Python, no RTL)
`python3 tools/hexn_connect4_l23_v1.py 2 4000 100`: same method as the 1,000-game row, 2 seeds, 100 test games, [win / draw / loss] after 1,000 / 2,000 / 4,000 training games.
vs RANDOM: random X 0.52; layer 2 context 1: 0.61, 0.73, 0.70; context 2: 0.58, 0.66, 0.66; context 3: 0.70, 0.77, 0.64; layer 3: 0.82, 0.83, 0.77 wins (loss 0.17, 0.17, 0.23).
vs BLOCKER: random X 0.05; layer 2 context 1: 0.03, 0.01, 0.04; context 2: 0.10, 0.29, 0.08; context 3: 0.07, 0.10, 0.14; layer 3: 0.32, 0.36, 0.23 wins (loss 0.69, 0.64, 0.76). lookahead(2) and (4): 40 of 40 wins against both.
Reading: layer 3 does not keep climbing; it levels off at roughly 0.8 against random and 0.2 to 0.35 against the blocker, and the later points are as likely noise as a real fall (2 seeds, 100 games: differences of 0.1 are within noise). So the earlier rising trend (0.08, 0.14, 0.32 at 250, 500, 1,000 games) stops at about the 1,000-game level. This is the baseline layer 4 has to beat. Not tuned; contexts are histories, not boards.
Partial first layer 4 run (tools/hexn_connect4_layer4_v1.py 100 1, random opponent only so far, 100 games): forced replies only 0.90 then 0.98 wins; the three tree variants 1.00 and 1.00. Positions per move: uniform opponent model about 5,976; with layer 3 prediction about 11,400; without the tactics prior about 10,023. The random opponent does not separate the variants; the other two opponents are still running.

### Layer 4 as a second teacher of layer 3; never closing the possibilities (Alan, 29 Oct 2026, 16:03; INTENTIONS, not built)
(1) As play continues the tree slides down and unused paths are removed; this can also help layer 3, as another level of reinforcement: layer 4 knows which predicted branch turned out to be the real one and whether the current step was reached, which layer 3 alone does not see, and can send that back (reward and confidence). Alan expects a small effect on the prediction. Risk noted (mine, to be measured): layer 4 chooses the player's own moves, so layer 3 would learn from games shaped by layer 4's choices and may become over-confident about situations layer 4 steers into; compare prediction accuracy with and without the feedback.
(2) Layer 4 still takes care of a sudden random move and NEVER fully closes a possibility, unless it leads to an actual win (or the game ends). Gap in the first model (tools/hexn_connect4_layer4_v1.py): its probability cutoff theta drops unlikely replies completely, so a surprise reply is not looked at. Planned change in the step-target version: every legal reply gets at least a shallow check (about two plies) regardless of its predicted probability.

So the card agreed with the model through a very large number of full-line events, which is a strong check of that path, BUT the store was nowhere near full: **the stepped keys of the card script cluster in the tier's hash**: `hashk` is a multiplication by an odd constant plus one xor-shift, and keys of the form (i+1) x 0x2545F491 mod 2^24 reach only 5,191 of the 16,384 top-14-bit values and 10,630 of the 131,072 top-17-bit values (checked in Python for 800,000 keys), while 800,000 random 24-bit keys reach 16,384 and 130,762. **Consequences:** (1) the 'about a fifth of capacity' reading in the 09:45 soak row was wrong for that key set: that run (200,000 stepped keys) also used only a small part of the store; its pass still stands for what it did. (2) The hash is weak for structured keys (arithmetic progressions, which real data can contain); a stronger mixing function is an open design item, which would change the RTL and the model together (new versions, not an edit). (3) `tools/hexn_tier7_card_v3.py --mixkeys` (clone of v2, v2 untouched) spreads the keys with a mixing function; the model replay of that stream (2,000,000 steps, 800,000 keys) gives: level 1 16,359 of 16,384 full, DDR3 lines written 128,255 of 131,072, sd_full only 3,232, found 453,244, notfound 745,479, sd_blk_rd 2,507,629, sd_blk_wr 1,899,297, put_new 800,170. | 30 Oct 2026 10:25 | IDEA ONLY (nothing built, nothing measured): A NEURAL HELPER BESIDE LAYERS 3 AND 4 (Alan's idea, after he put the question 'so this fits a predictive language model, not a neural model'; layers 1 to 3 as built are exact-history counting and prediction, layer 4 is the planner) | **Why:** exact-history memory cannot treat two similar situations as alike, so a position it has never seen starts from nothing. Draughts positions rarely repeat exactly, so this matters more there than in Connect Four. **Form (Alan's decision):** not a strict layer 5 on top. A helper that sits NEXT TO layers 3 and 4, LISTENS to both, and puts SUGGESTIONS/MOVES BACK TO LAYER 3 (the same route layer 4 already uses: a goal event on its own channel, layer 3 chooses, the end-of-game reward reinforces). **Possible jobs, in my order of value (Claude's ranking, not decided):** (1) position judge for layer 4, so the tree can stop early; (2) key maker for layer 3, so similar positions share an entry (the key-representation insight); (3) fallback predictor where layer 3 has no entry. **Form of the part (sketch):** small integer multiply-and-add network, a few thousand weights, on the multiplier blocks; learns from self-play data the exact store and layer 4 already produce (layer 4 teaching layer 3 is the pattern), trained on the host first, then loaded. **Order of work (Alan's decision):** get layer 4 working on silicon first, with whatever tweaking that needs; the helper comes after. First experiment when its turn comes: job 1 as a small Python test, to see whether layer 4 can search shallower without losing strength. |

| 30 Oct 2026 10:45 | IDEA ONLY (nothing built, nothing measured): TWO LAYER 3s — A LIVE ONE AND A PLANNING COPY FOR LAYER 4 (Alan's idea, while the requirements for layer 4 on silicon were being discussed) | **Alan's proposal:** use layer 3 twice. One instance handles the current inputs and outputs (the live one). A second instance is the predictive model for layer 4: it uses what the first one knows and is FED FROM the live one. **Claude's reading, to be tested:** (a) gains: layer 4's many lookups (about 800 to 24,000 positions per move in the Python model, run of 30 Oct, 20 games, 1 seed, rough) do not compete with the live layer 3; the copy can be read-only; it could run ahead on spare cycles between moves. (b) The second instance could also be fed IMAGINED events (its own predictions) to roll a line forward, which is what the tree walk does by lookup now. (c) Costs: double memory, or one store with two read ports giving the same separation without a copy; the copy goes stale between syncs. (d) Ways to feed the copy, not chosen: write-through of every update; periodic snapshot (needs the restore-from-dump step, not yet built); shared store with a second read port. (e) In the Python model the planner already reads layer 3 by history key without changing it, so the 'pollution' reason for a copy is not present there; the gain is in hardware access. **Untested question, cheap in Python:** does a planner using a copy frozen at the start of the game (or refreshed every N moves) play as well as one using the live layer 3? **Decision:** none yet. |

It does NOT show that a judge cannot help; it shows this first model does not help in Connect Four, where the hand score is already a decent leaf. (3) Part of the gap at depth 4 is the kind of search: the plain player assumes the opponent's best reply (minimax), which is the right model against a minimax opponent, whereas an expectation over a flat or weakly learned spread is not; so this is NOT a clean test of the memory alone. **Conclusion for now: as built, Hex-N does not beat a plain search in draughts; the learned layer 3 adds nothing measurable at this stage.** **Not tested, so not closed:** teaching from layer 4 and self-play (they helped in Connect Four against some opponents), the neural judge (it helped in Connect Four), a board-based key instead of move histories (the similarity step), a stronger opponent mix, bigger samples, and the cost in hardware. Cause (mine, plausible, not isolated): the -1 on every other class starves a class the cell has stopped recognising. A counters-only variant (threshold frozen) was no better. **A LOCAL rule works:** only the winner's counters move, lane on +1 and lane off -1 (saturating +-31), no other detector and no threshold change. With the bit wrong 0 / 10 / 20 / 30 / 40 percent: A 0.784 / 0.782 / 0.779 / 0.773 / 0.771; B 0.681 / 0.647 / 0.637 / 0.650 / 0.631. **So what is supported:** a local online nudge of the winning detector (cheap, one detector, no cross-detector traffic) lets a detector start from very little training and follow slow drift (0.64 to 0.76 or 0.78; 0.41 to 0.61), and costs nothing when the patterns are steady. What is NOT supported: that the feedback input itself is needed; it helps by about 0.02 to 0.03 only when it is nearly perfect. **Not tested / limits:** the model is optimistic by up to about 0.08 at 8 classes (see the 22:10 row); random patterns only; a rise in noise is not helped; bigger drift, merged or new classes, and the interaction with layer 2/3 are untested; the hardware cost of keeping the counters in the cell is not measured (the training counters are 6 bits per lane, held in one memory by the shared trainer, and whether its commands allow single-sample updates after FINISH has NOT been checked); the few bits of state / leaky store idea is untested. |

(2) The CLAMP AT ZERO of the total does lose information: making the total signed (V2 against V1) adds +0.015 to +0.04 in every condition (0.881 to 0.910; 0.747 to 0.780; 0.510 to 0.552), because a detector whose total is negative or zero is read as zero and cannot be ranked. (3) With a signed total the counted detector is AHEAD of the nearest template at small training sets (2 per class 0.780 against 0.752; 4 per class 0.889 against 0.879; noisy 4 per class 0.552 against 0.541) and BEHIND by 0.01 to 0.02 at 16 per class (0.910 against 0.930; 0.928 against 0.945; 0.894 against 0.904; 0.599 against 0.620), where the plain template weights (V6/V7) win. The counted weights use what separates a class from the others, which helps with few samples; with many samples the simple majority bit is better. **Cost of the change (not measured):** a signed total needs one more bit in the accumulator and a signed compare, and the margin has to be reported below the threshold as well (a new detector version; det_v3 already exists, so v4 or later), plus a controller that picks the largest signed margin instead of counting bursts. **Limits:** random patterns; the detector model is up to about 0.08 optimistic at 8 classes (22:10 row); the tie rule is lowest-index for every arm; no RTL, no synthesis of the signed version. |

No wider accumulator is needed than today's AW 7 (plus the sign). **Size, same tool for both (native yosys 0.69, `synth_gowin` and `synth_xilinx -family xc7`; this tool's Gowin counts are larger than the recorded 0.33-era figures for the same detector, e.g. v3 at 12 wires 263 LUT + 29 ALU against the recorded 150 + 35 for v2, so only the v3/v4 difference is meaningful; the Xilinx counts match the recorded ones):** detector v3 -> v4 at 12 / 18 / 20 wires, Gowin LUT+ALU 292 / 354 / 336 -> 305 / 364 / 348 (+3 to +4 percent), flip-flops 13 / 14 / 14 -> 17 / 17 / 17; Xilinx 87 / 112 / 131 -> 84 / 111 / 129 (about equal). Real recordings (all events | goal events): order-1 0.56 | 0.39; order-2 0.63 | 0.39; order-3 0.80 | 1.00; store N=3 0.77 | 1.00; N=2 0.79 | 1.00; N=1 0.80 | 1.00. **Findings (information lost):** (1) **The store with the promotion filter off and room enough IS an order-3 back-off n-gram (same numbers); layer 2 as built is BEHIND that simple predictor by 0.065 to 0.09 on ORDER2, 0.08 to 0.14 on NOISE and 0.03 on the real recordings**, because a pair is not used until it has been seen three times. The filter did NOT help prediction under insertion noise in these streams (junk pairs have low counts and never become the best follower); it may still save memory (not measured). N=2 recovers about half of the gap, N=1 all of it. (2) **A full table cannot learn a changed world:** after the switch at event 400, N=1 with KCAP 128 collapses (0.30 in events 400-500, 0.34 in 500-700) because every new entry has 'seen' 1 and is the lowest-'seen' entry, so it is dropped at once; the same store with KCAP 1024 reaches 0.65 | 0.83. **Evicting the LEAST RECENTLY USED entry instead fixes it at KCAP 128: 0.675 | 0.864 (better than the big table), with ORDER2 unchanged (0.86 | 0.89).** With the promotion filter on, new entries start at 'seen' = N so they are less exposed (0.53 | 0.75), and LRU changes nothing there. (3) Forgetting (weaken old followers on a surprise), or choosing the best follower by seen minus contra, add at most 0.01; no important loss there; the contradicted count is not needed for the prediction itself. | 31 Oct 2026 04:10 | STORE V6: LRU EVICTION ON THE STORE V5 DESIGN, BUILT AS A CLONE AND SIZED (Alan, 31 Oct 02:07: "size that first"; `rtl/hexn_store_v6.v`, `rtl/tb_hexn_store_v6.v`, `tools/hexn_store_v6_check_v1.py`, `tests/test_hexn_store_v6.py` (2 pass, needs iverilog); store v5 untouched; simulation + yosys counts only, NOT placed, NOT on a card) | **Finding first:** the model's LRU fix cannot be a stamp added to store v5. v5 never frees a heap row (`hp` only counts up) and when the heap is full a new entry is DROPPED (v5 header; confirmed in the code), so v5 stops learning altogether once full. A real LRU therefore needs: a stamp per context (put in the INDEX word, refreshed on every lookup hit: 8 bits, units of 16 events, wraps at 4,096 events), a dead (tombstone) bit so probe chains survive a removal, a free list of heap rows (rows chain through their header, head in `fl_head`, popped with a 3-clock read), and an eviction FSM: scan the index for the oldest live context other than the current one (3 clocks per slot), clear its entry words, hand its rows to the free list, mark its slot dead, retry the insert. **Grain differs from the model:** v6 evicts a whole CONTEXT (all its followers); the model evicted single (context, next) pairs. **Checks (simulation, iverilog):** (1) heap never full, 400 events: v6 answers identical to v5 on every command (stamp, dead bit, FSM invisible). (2) small heap (16 rows x 4 slots, 128 index slots, 6 symbols, function changes at event 1200), next-event accuracy windows 600-1200 \| 1200-1400 \| 1400-2400: **v5 .557 \| .055 \| .098 (counters: 5,230 entries dropped); v6 .570 \| .585 \| .594** (2,796 evictions, 360 drops). **Size (yosys 0.69, default settings of core v9: 4 event bits, 1,024 index slots, 512 rows x 4, probe 4, 32 candidates), logic = LUT1-4 + ALU, FFs separate:** Gowin v5 1,147 LUT + 526 ALU = 1,673, 977 FF; **v6 1,472 LUT + 572 ALU = 2,044 (+371, +22%), 1,101 FF (+124)**; Xilinx xc7 v5 about 1,000 LUT-class cells, v6 about 1,300 (LUT counts 801 against 1,145 incl. LUT5/6; +43%). Memory: index word grows from 24 to 34 bits (+10,240 bits on 1,024 slots, +42% of that RAM); heap, row headers and candidates unchanged; yosys reports the same 14 DPX9B and 46 RAM16SDP4 for both, so the real BSRAM count needs place-and-route (not run). Against core v9's recorded 12,828 LUT4 (62%) the logic adds about 1.8 points of the Tang's 20,736 slots IF yosys and place-and-route counts agree (they have differed before). **Time:** longest command 1,416 clocks (small test, 128 index slots) against 271 for v5; the scan costs 3 clocks per index slot, so at 1,024 slots one eviction is about 3,200 clocks and a command that evicts at all three context levels about 9,500 clocks (about 0.35 ms at 27 MHz) - scaled from the measurement, not run. Only happens while the heap is full. **Known gaps:** `n_known` is not decremented on eviction (it overstates); reward-queue pointers into an evicted row are not cleared (a later reward could land on a recycled slot); probe exhaustion with no free or dead slot still drops; candidate table untouched; eviction scan could be made cheaper (stamp-ordered list or a small CAM) - not tried; the 8-bit stamp wrap is not stressed; no comparison yet with the model's per-pair LRU at the same capacity; not tested with the board commands or the core. |

Snapshot-ID table.** `SnapTable`: an index of 2^IB slots hashes a (masked) snapshot to a slot with the same mix and probe as the store; the slot holds the full key, a small ID and a stamp; IDs are handed out in order; when IDs run out or the probe window is full, `refuse` gives no ID (that context is skipped) or `lru` reuses the ID of the least recently used snapshot (old contexts alias the new snapshot until they age out). `Layer3Id` runs the layer 3 model with the context elements taken as IDs. **Results** (L=6, default masks (3 per snapshot), 6 seeds, remote-key top-1): demand is SMALL in these worlds: distinct masked snapshots 46 (world 3), 70 (steady), 80 (rule switch), distinct moments 12, 41, 44. World 3: no table .834; 256, 128 or 64 IDs (refuse or lru) .834; 32 IDs lru .649; 16 IDs .514. Steady: .874; 128 IDs .874; 64 refuse .876 (180 refusals) and 64 lru .874; 32 lru .773; 16 lru .503. Rule switch (before \| just after \| long after): .855 .871 .868; 128 IDs identical; 64 refuse .842 .867 .867 (856 refusals); 64 lru .854 .868 .865; 32 lru .772 .776 .752; 16 lru .548 .499 .474. **Reading:** (1) with a 7-bit ID (128 IDs) nothing is lost in these worlds, and with 6 bits LRU loses at most 0.003; refuse at 64 loses up to 0.013; below 64 it falls quickly. (2) So a snapshot ID of 7 bits, an index of 512 slots (9 bits) and probe 4 are enough HERE; **real sensors will give far more distinct snapshots** (seven or eight classes on three sensors with three masks can reach hundreds), so the ID width must be a build setting like LMAX, not a fixed value. (3) The follower side (moments) needs the same kind of table; it is only counted here (12 to 46 distinct). (4) Store v7's tag would hold `lookback` IDs of 7 bits instead of 4-bit events: at LMAX 8 that is 56 bits plus the length, so the index word grows again; the cost is not sized. **Limits:** synthetic worlds with 3 sensors and 4-bit keys; aliasing after an ID reuse is tolerated, not cleaned; masks multiply the demand by 3 and are fixed here; no RTL for the ID table; speed of the extra lookup per moment not measured (one table lookup per mask per moment). |

(4) World C shows the limit of one cue: with an unknown sun factor a single height cannot separate neighbouring animals; no amount of learning helps; a second cue (the ratio of two readings, the time of day) is needed. (5) The learning rate matters: .15 was too slow, 1.5 matched nb. **Design consequences:** the sensor profile = the goal table as prior weights + a learning rate; the neural for sensors is K parallel copies of the 8-weight unit (one per animal) or one time-shared copy; the prior should be a bounded score (floored at -8 here), which suits whole-number weights. | 1 Nov 2026 01:10 | FIRST BOARD RUN OF THE SCORER (Alan, 18:32) AND A BRIDGE LIMIT FOUND. MEASURED (1 MHz, batches of 64 words x 20): table load (193 words) 6,871 us and 'play done'; no timeouts; write+start 2,354 us, wait 85 us (results already in on the first poll), read 2,135 us, total 4,574 us = 71.5 us per word (arithmetic had said about 45 us at 1.5 MHz; 71.5 us at 1 MHz is the same link rate scaled). WRONG: 292 of 1,280 results (22.8%). CAUSE FOUND IN SIMULATION, NOT YET RE-RUN ON THE BOARD: the proven `spi_bridge_v1.v` counts the bytes of a transaction in 8 bits and stops at 255, so a 64-word WR_WORDS (259 bytes) loses its 64th word, and a 64-word RD_WORDS returns a damaged last word. Reproduced with the simulated ESP32: 64-word write fails, 63-word write passes; 64-word read fails, 63-word read passes. It fits the board result: three of the table's 192 weights were in the 64th slot of a 64-word chunk (words 63, 127, 191), and 1 - (11/12)^3 = 22.97% of random samples touch at least one of 3 specific table cells (292 of 1,280 = 22.8%), plus a stale 64th sample each batch. FIX (software only, no bitstream change, proven RTL untouched): at most 63 words per transaction; applied to `hexn_scorer_test.ino` and `hexn_link_speed.ino`; the unit test now uses 63-word transactions. ALSO FOUND: the ESP32 'judge it itself' timer printed 0.01 us a word because the compiler removed the unused loop; made the sum volatile. EARLIER ROWS AFFECTED: the 00:35 link-speed 'words/s' figures were computed for 64 words per transaction, of which 63 were real data (about 1.6% lower than printed for the batch rows); the clock-limited conclusion stands; the 00:35 rows are not edited. NOT CHANGED, TO DECIDE: the main repo's `unicell_unit_bridge.ino` and the web sketch link layer also use 64-word chunks and so share the limit; the bridge could instead get a 16-bit counter as a new version (spi_bridge_v2). |

Table load 6,845 us. Tang route per batch: write+start 2,320 us, wait 85 us, read 2,101 us, total 4,506 us = 71.5 us per word. Where the Tang can still win: direct sensor feeding with no SPI in the path, a much larger judge, or an ESP32 busy with other work; none of these is measured yet. |

| 1 Nov 2026 02:35 | MORSE-KEY DEMO BUILT (Alan, 19:12: 'go for press length and space length, i think you may see where this is going (- - - ,,, - - -)'). READING TAKEN: a Morse key; his pattern is dashes, dots, dashes, which is O S O (letter gaps between the groups), not SOS (dots, dashes, dots); I said so in the reply and the test checks the pattern as typed. A1 = key (sidetone on the buzzer), A2 = clear. The 1 ms counter measures press length and space length; each is a log2 layer-1 bin in its own sensor slot of ONE scorer table (slot 0 press: class 1 = dash from 256 ms; slot 1 space: class 1 = letter gap from 256 ms; slot 2 space: class 1 = word gap from 1024 ms; other slots carry the neutral bin 15 with weight 0). A decoder collects up to 5 dots/dashes and emits a character at a letter gap; the display shows the last four characters on seven segments (approximate for M, W, X, K, V). New: `fpga/verilog/hexn_morse_top_v1.v`, `hexn_morse_gen_v1.v` (generated by `tools/hexn_morse_gen_v1.py`: table ROM, decoder, glyphs), `hw262_shield_v2.v` (clone of v1 taking raw segment bytes; v1 untouched), `tools/hexn_morse_build_v1.sh`, `tests/hexn_morse/`, `tests/vm/test_hexn_morse_v1.py`, `docs/hex-n/DEMO_MORSE_v1.md`. CHECKED IN SIMULATION (fast clock, unit 160 ms): S, O, word-gap space, 1, C, a 6-symbol pattern giving 'not a character', E, clear, O S O, buzzer low while the key is down. BUILT: `fpga/build/hexn_morse_demo_v1/unit_top.fs`, 1,113 LUT4 (5%), 175 MHz capable. LIMITS: the bins are powers of two so the thresholds are 256 and 1024 ms (a unit of about 150 to 200 ms works; a faster key needs another table or the adaptive version, not built); the word gap (1024 ms) sits only one bin above the letter gap's top (512 to 1023 ms is letter gap). NOT RUN ON A BOARD. |

| 1 Nov 2026 08:40 | DIAGNOSTIC FOUND THE LINK FAULT; LINK REWRITTEN AS VERSION 2, NOT YET RE-RUN ON THE BOARD (Alan, 21:38, diagnostic output pasted). MEASURED on the board: stage 1 link ok; register write/read ok; 1 word and 10 words written and read back ok; at 100 words the gateway gave NO reply (empty line after the 3 s timeout) and the diagnostic stopped. READING OF THE CAUSE (from the pattern, not confirmed): the ESP32 serial receive buffer is 256 bytes by default and the sketch read one character per pass, too slow at 921,600 baud, so lines of about 256 characters or more lost bytes: a lost newline gives no reply; a lost character inside a line gives a silently different word, which would explain the 996 wrong results of the 21:36 run (that run's 200-word lines). FIX: gateway v2 (8 KB receive buffer, bulk read, 460,800 baud, checksum ` #HHHH` on commands and OK replies, `ERR checksum` on a mismatch) and `SerialLink` that verifies, clears and resends up to 4 times (`link.retries`). Test with a flaky-line model (every 7th command damaged, every 11th loses its newline, every 9th reply damaged): a 150-row two-judge run gives exactly the reference words; the test fails with the reply check off; 8 tests pass. Alan must upload the new gateway sketch and re-run `hexn_front_diag_v1.py`. If stages 2 to 5 pass, the front-end run is repeated. If a stage still fails, that stage names the next suspect (the earlier ones listed at 08:10: 200-word SPI writes, the multi-run split, gateway parsing). |

For scale, v1 shared (add, sub, >=) was 101 slots, so adding accumulate and the 3-way compare cost about 56 slots. Real cells for context (same flow): accumulator_cell_v4s 84 / 105 / 91, branch_cell_v4s 209 / 32 / 111, together with the adder and compare cells 332 LUT4 + 200 ALU. THESE ARE NOT COMPARABLE with the block: the block has no accumulator pulse_mode or threshold logic (abs and a threshold compare, a large part of its 105 ALU), no branch routing, no held registers, no emit-source mux; it covers only the arithmetic core. So the claim is limited to: for the SAME set of arithmetic functions, one shared adder is about a quarter smaller than separate units behind a mux, and the cost of the select is visible (about 17 LUT4 more than the separate version). Whether a block that also holds the threshold, routing and held-register parts stays smaller than the separate cells is NOT shown. Only the pulse qualification differs. COMBINED TEST (simulation): producer with random gaps -> config_gate_v1 (WIDTH 64, SETTLE 1, DRAIN_TIMEOUT 60) -> arith_block_v3 (shared) -> sub_to_flex_v1 -> consumer with random 0 to 3 clock delay; 1,500 random words, about 440 random reconfiguration requests across all five modes and random steps (432 config loads seen by the block including the first; the rest arrived while the gate was busy and were rejected, as designed). RESULT: 1,500 admitted, 1,500 results, every one equal to the model, order kept. CONTROL, same stream and same block with the existing flex_to_sub_v1 and the configuration driven straight into the block at random clocks: it FAILS, and worse than expected: a word dropped on a config clock leaves the one-word bridge busy for good, so only 11 of 1,500 words were ever admitted (10 results). PHASE 2 (found while writing it, now a stated limit): in accumulate mode a word with neither pulse produces NO result; the bridge then stays busy, the next word is not admitted (admitted=1 of 2), and a following config request times out with cfg_err[0] set (the gate's timeout rule works, but the stall itself is not cleared). So a region behind the one-word bridge must produce exactly one result per word; the accumulator cell does not (it answers only on a pulse). Fixes to consider, none built: make the block answer every word, or let the gate/bridge clear busy on a stated latency instead of waiting for a result. NOT DONE: carrier region behind the gate and its settle time, the real divider cell, flex W18/W32, place and route, anything on a board. |

MEASURED, simulation, THROUGHPUT: producer -> bridge (plain flex_to_sub_v1, or the gate when idle) -> adder_cell_v4s -> sub_to_flex_v1 -> consumer that takes a result as soon as it sees it = 5 clocks per word (200 words, 999 clocks), identical with and without the gate. The adder cell alone takes a word every clock, so this chain runs at ONE FIFTH of the cell rate, not one half. The 5 is the one-word-in-flight rule: admit, cell, held result, consumer ack, then busy clears. It depends on the consumer's speed (here 1 clock) and on how the bridge is built; a faster ack path or two words in flight would change it, not tried. MEASURED, synthesis only (yosys 0.69 -nowidelut, one run, 64-bit data, 32-bit config): gate v2 = 40 LUT4 + 55 ALU + 128 flops (95 slots; gate v1 was 76, so the watchdog costs about 19); block v4 shared = 124 LUT4 + 33 ALU + 77 flops (157 slots; v3 was 158), naive 117 + 104 + 77 (221), so sharing is 29% smaller. FINDING WHILE WRITING THE POLICY: gate v2's watchdog could not tell a stuck CELL from a stuck CONSUMER (a result waiting to be taken); v3 adds the input res_valid, and a result that is present but not taken is only the level `blocked`. A mutant that drops this (as v2) loses data in the stall test, so it was a real flaw, not a cosmetic one. MEASURED, simulation (iverilog), chain: producer -> 3 x [gate_v3 -> arith_block_v4 -> sub_to_flex] -> consumer; stage 1 adds, stage 2 subtracts 100, stage 3 compares >= 7; each stage has a model that computes the expected result of every admitted word from the config active at admission. SCEN 1 one transient fault in stage 2 (kill of its result path, cleared when its region is reset): recovered, resets 1, lost 1, neighbours untouched, every result at every stage equals the model (299 of 300 reached the end). SCEN 2 two faults 100 words apart with MAX_RETRY 1: both recovered (retries forgiven), 298 of 300 through. SCEN 3 persistent fault, MAX_RETRY 1: stage 2 goes dead after 1 reset (lost 2), stage 1 and 3 never reset, upstream held (stage 1 admitted 63 of 300), downstream quiet, no wrong result. SCEN 4 final consumer stalled 400 clocks (4x the watchdog): blocked rose, NO fault, NO reset, all 300 words arrive correct. SCEN 5 clean run with 72 random reconfigurations of stage 2 in the stream: all correct; chain throughput 6 clocks per word (one unit alone was 5). MUTATION CHECKS (each must fail, each does): watchdog ignoring a present result fails SCEN 4; no config replay fails SCEN 1; retries never forgiven fails SCEN 2; lost word not counted fails SCEN 1. MEASURED, synthesis only (yosys 0.69 -nowidelut, one run, WIDTH 64, CFG_W 32): gate v3 = 75 LUT4 + 88 ALU + 163 flops (163 slots; v2 95, v1 76). One unit (gate v3 + shared block v4) is about 320 slots plus its output bridge (not measured); the many 8-bit and 16-bit counters (watchdog, retries, good words, resets, lost, state timer) are the cost and could be cut. Parts alone (combinational): nano logic as a topology decode 397 LUT4; 18-bit shifter shared 119, two separate shifters 171, left-only 77. Real cells for scale only (not the same function): nano_cell_v4s 287 LUT4 + 76 flops; shift_cell_v4s (32-bit, with lane cut) 518 LUT4 + 30 ALU; shift_stage_v4s with a FIXED amount 3 LUT4. Reading: sharing is 24 to 27% smaller across the whole block; the nano logic is the biggest single part and is much cheaper as a truth table (about 160 slots saved). CAUTION ON OLDER FIGURES: ledger #899 (quoted in the scoping note) gives 3,261 LUT4 for shift_cell with a run-time amount against 26 fixed; with this tool and flow shift_cell_v4s measures 518 + 30 and the fixed-amount stage 3, so the old absolute numbers do NOT reproduce here (older tool and flow); the select is still the large part of a run-time shifter, but about 6 times smaller in absolute terms than the old note says. ALAN'S NOTE 05:26 ('the adder and nano are 2 arrival designs'; READING: they wait for two operands that arrive separately): the block receives both operands TOGETHER in one 64-bit word, so the pairing (join) happens upstream of the gate; the real nano cell holds one operand in a register and fires on the second arrival, which this block does not do. A true two-arrival block would need a join stage in front (hold the first operand, fire on the second); not built, not measured. NOT DONE: gate random-stream or chain with v5, flex W18 build of the block (the shift is 18-bit but the rest is 32-bit), place and route, anything on a board. |

JOIN STAGE (join_v1): the two-arrival front stage. Operands A and B arrive separately on flex hand-offs; it holds the first and fires one {A,B} hand-off when the second has arrived, ready to feed config_gate_v3. STICKY_B=0 pair mode (k-th A with k-th B, both cleared when taken); STICKY_B=1 the nano behaviour (B is held like load_hold and pairs with every later A until a new B replaces it; a new B is accepted only while no pair is waiting). MEASURED, simulation: pair mode with random arrival order, A-first and B-first batches of 300 pairs each, a lone operand never fires and fires on the second arrival; sticky B with a controlled sequence; join -> gate_v3 -> block v5 (nano XOR, sticky B) equals the REAL nano_cell_v4s fed load_hold and flow in the same order; a new B offered under a waiting pair does not change it. Three deliberate breaks (fires on one operand, held B cleared after use, B overwritten under a waiting pair) each fail. MEASURED, synthesis: join_v1 = 4 LUT4 + 66 flops (the two operand registers), the same in both modes. 2. TRUTH TABLE IN THE NANO CELL: nano_cell_v4s_tt is a clone with the same ports; the per-bit decode of the 10-bit topology is replaced by a 4-bit table decoded once. MEASURED, simulation: identical to the ORIGINAL nano_cell_v4s on every clock for all 1,024 topology words (13,314 clocks, random hold/flow/load_hold/valid); a wrong table entry is caught. MEASURED, synthesis (yosys 0.69 -nowidelut, one run): nano_cell_v4s 287 LUT4 + 76 flops; nano_cell_v4s_tt 131 LUT4 + 76 flops (54% fewer LUT4). The flex nano (nano_cell_v4sa) was NOT converted. 3. MASK: mask_cell_v4s works in eight 4-bit nibble lanes and zeroes the lanes named in an 8-bit mask (mask_en 0 passes the data). That is data AND a constant pattern, i.e. the nano AND with the HELD operand coming from the config instead of from an input. arith_block_v6 adds that one option (cfg_data[26]: in mode 7 the held operand is the 8 keep-flags in cfg_data[15:8] expanded to 32 bits; mask_en=1 -> keep = ~nibble_mask, mask_en=0 -> keep = FF). MEASURED, simulation: equals the REAL mask_cell_v4s for all 256 nibble masks x mask_en 0/1 on random data (512 configs, 2,148 checks); the v5 test file passes unchanged on v6 (nano and the rest kept); inverted keep flags are caught. MEASURED, synthesis: block v6 (shared, truth-table nano) 401 LUT4 + 33 ALU + 88 flops = 434 slots against 403 for v5, so the mask costs about 31 LUT4 + 1 flop on top of the nano logic; the stand-alone mask_cell_v4s is 35 LUT4 + 43 flops. So the mask is absorbed at about the cost of a mask cell, but it shares the nano logic and the one block. Other cells looked at, not converted: the invert addon is the nano NOT; latch (state with set/clear/toggle) and compare (arithmetic) do not reduce to this table. 4. SHIFT (Alan 05:40): agreed and consistent with the measurements: a shift with the amount fixed at design time is wiring only (shift_stage_v4s with a fixed amount 3 LUT4), while the block pays for choosing the amount at run time (18-bit shifter 77 LUT4 left only, 119 both directions with one shifter, 171 with two). A barrel shifter is stages of fixed wiring plus a 2-way choice per stage; the choice is the whole cost. A limited range of amounts (his earlier idea: a small range per cell, chain for larger totals) would cut it; not built or measured here. LIMITS: join stage tested only with the flex hand-off and 32-bit operands; one word in flight through the gate still limits throughput to 5 to 6 clocks per word; block v5/v6 not re-run through the gate random stream or the chain (the join + gate + block run in SCEN 3 is a short check, 48 words); no flex W18 build; no place and route; nothing on a board. |

| 1 Nov 2026 12:40 | WEIGHT SAVE AND LOAD PORT FOR THE NEURAL JUDGE, ROUND TRIP THROUGH THE LOADABLE ITEM (Alan, 12:17: 'the hexn weights, how they are stored and how they can be saved and reloaded'; 12:19: 'yes start there first' = wire the neural judge's weights out so learned weights can be saved as an item). WHAT THE FILES SAY (read, not run): the neural judge (rtl/hexn_neural_v1.v) holds 7 weights + bias, 16 bits each, in registers (no block RAM), learns in hardware, has a write port (w_wr/w_addr/w_data) and an output bus w_flat that only the testbench used; the batch scorer (fpga/verilog/hexn_scorer_v1/v2) holds two 128 x 8-bit tables in block RAM, loaded by LOAD words in the stream, with NO readback (it is trained on the PC, the host keeps the copy), weights survive reset/mode change (only the bias clears), CLEAR zeroes them; the history stores and the 8,000-weight position judge are not weights of this kind (store LOAD exists, judge is an idea). So weights are loaded at run time, NOT baked into the logic: a new set needs no synthesis. NEW FILES (earlier ones untouched): rtl/hexn_weight_port_v1.v, rtl/tb_hexn_weight_port_v1.v, tests/test_hexn_weight_port_v1.py (5 tests; with the existing neural and item tests 9 pass). DESIGN: SAVE = one-clock pulse rd_start copies w_flat into a snapshot in ONE clock, then streams 8 words (out_valid, addr 0..7 with 7 = bias, data, out_last); LOAD = one in_valid pulse per word becomes one w_wr pulse to the judge; both are taken only while the caller's `idle` is high and no stream is running, otherwise the request is ignored and err_busy goes high (sticky until reset) - a learn step updates the weights one by one, so a copy or write in the middle would mix old and new. MEASURED, simulation: load known words and read them back through the port; after 200 learn steps the snapshot equals the live weights and a SECOND judge restored from the saved words gives the same y and the same weights for 60 more mixed commands; a save and a load requested while a learn step is in flight are refused and flagged; FULL ROUND TRIP: a judge learns 300 steps from blank (two seeds), SAVE streams 8 words, Python packs a weights item (HXI1, 1 output x 7 inputs, Q14, bias in the i32 slot), validates it, unpacks it, writes the words, a FRESH judge is loaded from them and over 40 further commands gives output y and all 8 weights identical to the original (text files compared); one flipped bit in the item is refused ('checksum') before anything is loaded; three mutations (no busy guard on load, wrong load address, rotated snapshot) are each caught. MEASURED, synthesis (yosys 0.69 synth_gowin -nowidelut, one run): port alone 143 LUTs + 4 ALU + 177 flops (128 of the flops are the snapshot). LIMITS: the port is not in any top-level design yet; the neural judge itself is in no top (it is simulation only); the host link (esp32-bl616-link-protocol) has no word for items or for save/load, so how the 8 words reach the host or the card is not designed; the item format covers one output (the multi-output case in whole numbers is not run); the batch scorer still has no readback; nothing on a board; no place and route. |

So the 8-bit limit is on the scorer's weight field only; the 16-bit neural weights have no path in the real stream. CORRECTION TO MY 12:21 REPLY: I said stream top nibbles 14 and 15 are unused; nibble 14 is the CONTROL word in hexn_raw_engine_v3 (clear and judge control); only nibble 15 is reserved. The wire format for the halves is therefore NOT fixed here. NEW FILES (earlier ones untouched): rtl/hexn_weight_split_v1.v, rtl/tb_hexn_weight_split_v1.v, tests/test_hexn_weight_split_v1.py (4 tests; with the port tests 9 pass). DESIGN: SAVE side collects the port's 8 streamed words in a buffer, then sends 16 bytes (low byte then high byte of each address; address 7 is the bias) with ready/valid, a last flag on the final byte; LOAD side takes byte pulses in EITHER half order and passes a weight to the port only when BOTH halves are in, never half a weight; if a byte for another address arrives while a pair is incomplete, the incomplete halves are dropped and err_pair goes high (sticky until reset); a second save while sending is ignored and err_over goes high. MEASURED, simulation: 16 bytes in order with random stalls and the bytes equal the live weight halves; a fresh judge reassembled from them equals the original in three orders (in order with gaps, high half first with no gaps, random order and gaps); an incomplete pair is dropped and flagged (the half weight was not written, the complete pair after it was); a lone half is never written; a second save during sending is flagged and still exactly 16 bytes; the byte order equals the little-endian payload of the loadable item (7 weights as i16 + the low 16 bits of the bias) and a judge loaded from the ITEM's bytes ends with the same weights as the judge that was saved (two seeds); four mutations (halves swapped, incomplete pair not flagged, write on one half, wrong half flag) are each caught. MEASURED, synthesis (yosys 0.69 synth_gowin -nowidelut, one run): splitter alone 55 LUTs + 5 ALU + 66 flops + 4 RAM16SDP4 (distributed RAM; the 8 x 16-bit buffer). LIMITS: the bytes are plain pulses, not yet carried in a stream word or an ESP packet; a pair must be completed before the next address starts (interleaved addresses are treated as an error); the bias travels as 16 bits (the judge's bias is 16 bits; the item stores 32); the splitter and port are in no top-level design; nothing on a board; no place and route. |

RESTORE validates the whole record BEFORE any write (checksum, geometry, class order, each body word's class and address), then replays it as ordinary LOAD words; a refused record is skipped whole and a status word reports it (restore status words ARE counted in nres). MEASURED, simulation: the main test (7 phases: dump, restore round trip, and refusals) passes; five mutants (checksum unchecked, array size unchecked, class order unchecked, refused record not skipped, class 1 dumped from the wrong table) are all caught. BUG FOUND AND FIXED: the validation loop indexed with j[9:0] on a 9-bit register, giving x on bit 9, so every comparison was x and EVERY record was accepted (phases 3 and 4 failed with status 0); fixed with {1'b0, j}. MEASURED, place and route (not on a board): LUT4 6268/20736, 121.6 MHz, against v7 5755 (+513) and v6 5380 (+888 in total for both stages); DFF 2619, ALU 1432, BSRAM 10/46. | 1 Nov 2026 15:10 | POWER CYCLE REPORTED, AND A DAMAGED-RECORD TEST ADDED (Alan, 15:00: 'yes i did power off between, and had to press the card's reset to get it to work as well'). THE POWER-CYCLE CASE IS NOW SHOWN: Alan powered the board off between the save and the restore, and the restore then matched the flash copy (14:59 result, ledger 15:00). NOTE, NOT INVESTIGATED: the unit needed the card's reset button pressed after power-up before it would work. Two possible readings, neither checked: the bitstream was in flash and the unit has no power-on reset of its own that the ESP32 link relies on, or the bitstream had to be reloaded. Kinds 1 to 6 = the existing spaces, 7 = PROPOSED pattern bank: a small RAM on the card holding each block's config word(s); applying a slot sends the word through config_gate_v3; save is a plain RAM read (cells cannot be read back). BUILT: tools/hexn_record_v1.py and tests/vm/test_hexn_record_v1.py (3 tests pass): round trip for all kinds, each refusal code with only that fault present, and no single flipped bit in any of 3 record kinds is accepted. | 1 Nov 2026 | 20:40 | SECOND CORRECTION (to the 20:20 row, which was wrong; the 20:10 reading was right). Board run 3 (Alan): capply 0 0 -> 01010004; capply 2 5 -> 00000000 (clean); cprog 4 1 (unit 0 from the bypass slot) -> 01010004. Reading config_gate_v3.v: fault is STICKY (set on a fault event, cleared only by rst), and resets / lost are CUMULATIVE counters (cleared only by rst). So the status word is the unit's history since its last reset, not the result of this apply. The identical value 01010004 on every read of unit 0 (resets stays 1, never 2) means ONE event, not one per apply; units 1-3 never had one. The one event fits the first crun after power-up that timed out with a word stuck inside unit 0 (a fault event needs a word in flight with no result for WATCHDOG clocks). That is a reading that fits every board line so far, NOT yet proven; a test that could show it wrong: reset the unit (reload the bitstream or power-cycle), then cfill, cprog 0 must give four status words 00000000 and ctest must pass with no note; and running crun BEFORE programming must bring the flag back. Sketch: cprog / capply now print the note only when a unit's status CHANGES (sticky counts since its last reset), so an old event is not repeated. Compiles against stubs; not run on the board. |

| 1 Nov 2026 | 20:30 | BOARD RUN 4 after a full power-off and a reload of v13. (a) PREDICTION CONFIRMED: cfill then cprog 0 on the freshly reset unit gave status words 00000000 x4 and no note; the earlier 01010004 on unit 0 was a sticky history flag from the first timed-out crun (fault / lost / resets are cleared only by the unit's reset), as read from config_gate_v3.v at 20:40's row. (b) Two earlier tries in the same session (20:15, 20:17) returned status words DEADBEEF x4, i.e. the command was IGNORED (nothing written). Reading, NOT proven: hexn_unit_v2 starts a command when the command register (cfg18) CHANGES (cmd != cmd_q). The register keeps its value while the ESP32 restarts (opening the Serial Monitor restarts the ESP32) and the sketch's counter ucnt starts again at 0, so the first cprog after a restart is the SAME word as the last cprog before it and is not seen as new. The third try worked after other commands in between had moved ucnt on. Fit: banner printed at 20:17 (restart), identical word. Not seen in simulation because the testbench counter never restarts. Sketch fix: write 0 to cfg18 before every command (op 0 does nothing), so every command is a change. Compiles against stubs; NOT run on the board. The same weakness exists for any host that repeats a command word; the front end must do the same or count on from the register. |

READING: sharing the table by wires does NOT save area: each slave still needs its own 128:1 selection, about 109 sites, against about 54 to 69 for the grid cell with its own RAM16SDP4 table; what sharing saves is CONFIGURATION: one 128-bit change updates six cells, and a save holds one table per six cells. It does remove the 648-small-memory limit, and with about 109 sites per cell the limit becomes look-up tables: about 190 cells for the whole chip (ESTIMATE from 20,736 / 109, no place and route, nothing else on the chip), against 81 for the RAM-table grid. A cheaper form, not built: keep a RAM16 table in every slave but write all six together from the centre (area as the grid, one configuration port per cluster). |

On the 3-colouring one work core comes with two routing cores = 43 + 2 x 30 = 103 LUT + 93 flip-flops, against 119 LUT + 80 flip-flops for one mixed cell per work core: about 13 percent fewer look-up tables per work core, not a big saving; the gain is the structure (a pure routing layer, registered buffers between work cores, routing cores that can be limited to cross and corner paths and so be smaller) and that the routing density can be set separately from the work density. | 2 Nov 09:10 | Work/routing grid MODEL and PLANNER (tools/hexn_wrgrid_v1.py; Python only, no RTL). Square grid, work cores at (odd,odd), routing cores everywhere else, work core faces fixed (inputs W and N, output E), routing core 4 faces with one registered select per output (a cross is two lines on different faces; a stream may branch, nothing else shares a wire). Planner follows the netplace_v1 idea (one simple line per connection, joined one at a time, no sharing) but is NEW code, not a reuse of netplace/tightplace (those place on UniCell's Grid class with its own cell types; I read their headers only, not their code). It places each op on the nearest free work core, finds exact-length paths by a layered search, and makes both operands arrive on the same clock by lengthening the early one. Simulator runs the PLACED grid bit by bit (LSB first, 32-bit words) and was compared with plain Python arithmetic on 4 to 6 random input sets per task; a deliberately broken op gives a wrong answer, so the check is not vacuous. My own set of 12 small example tasks (the original 12-step list was not found in the repo, so these are mine: scale and offset, abs difference, clamp, bit-field extract, threshold, sum of four, 12-bin index, bit mix, multiply-accumulate-style, midpoint, range test, 8-term sum): all 12 pass. Planner-found sizes (not true minima; it is a greedy search with a pad limit): 5x5 for six tasks, 7x7 for four, 9x9 for sum4 and the 8-term sum (13x13 also works for all). Findings: (1) ALIGNMENT is the real cost. A buffered op (compare, min, max) puts its result a whole word (32 clocks) later, so the other operand needs about 32 routing hops of detour: clamp used 30 of its 48 route hops as padding, the 8-term sum 76 of 172. Routing cells are the delay line, so long waits cost routing cells, or a work core in word-delay mode, which the serial cell does not have yet. (2) PARITY: every routing hop and every serial op keeps the clock parity, detours add 2, so two operands that differ by an odd number of clocks cannot be aligned by routing; the planner inserts a 1-clock delay work core (copy). Shift right by an odd count is the usual cause. (3) FAN-OUT: one value used 8 times needs a branching line; a first version without it could not place the 8-term sum. (4) Ops marked buffered (shift right, compare, min, max) need a whole-word store and are NOT in the 119-LUT serial cell; the model treats them as available, so costs for them are open. (5) Work cores are used sparsely (2 to 8 of the work cores in each grid); wires are the limit, not work cores. Not done: hex 6-face version, real hardware, word-per-clock streaming of many words, cost of the alignment delays in LUTs. |

Alan: the original UniCell RAM cell routes like any other cell and has three modes: pass through, hold until requested, repeat what is stored. Modelled here only as HOLD: a stored delay of any length on the last wire into a work core (RAM depth = delay in bits), used when the clock gap between two operands is odd or 6 or more; small even gaps still use a routing detour. The pass-through is the normal hop; repeat and request-driven release are NOT modelled (the release time is fixed by the planner). Same 12 example tasks, all pass the bit-by-bit check against plain arithmetic. Route hops (v1 to v2): clamp 48 to 20, 8-term sum 172 to 81, 12-bin index 28 to 23; alignment padding hops 30, 76 and 8 to 0 in those three; the 8-term sum fits 7x7 instead of 9x9; the 1-clock delay work core is no longer needed (an odd gap is just a hold). RAM needed: clamp 1 hold of 38 bits, 12-bin index 13 bits, 8-term sum 7 holds, 122 bits in all. Results come at the same clocks or slightly later (clamp 75 to 81, bin12 52 to 56) because a hold waits exactly the gap. Not done: a real RAM cell in RTL, its LUT cost, and the repeat mode (would suit a constant such as a threshold that is used again and again without re-sending it). |
| 2 Nov 09:40 | RAM-ROUTING CELL cost probe (sub/verilog/hexn_ramroute_probe_v1.v; synthesis counts only, yosys 0.69, -nowidelut; NOT function-tested). Alan 07:35: replace each routing/buffer cell 1 to 1 with a RAM cell. Probe = the 6-face routing core plus one memory and the modes pass / hold (stored delay) / repeat (record n bits, replay). Measured: memory 16 bits = 74 LUT + 16 ALU + 50 FF + 1 RAM16SDP4; 64 bits = 90 LUT + 24 ALU + 58 FF + 4 RAM16SDP4; 256 bits = 124 LUT + 32 ALU + 66 FF + 16 RAM16SDP4. The plain routing core is 30 LUT + 24 FF. Counting a RAM16SDP4 as about 3 LUT sites (the earlier placement finding, not re-measured here), slots (LUT+ALU) become about 93, 126 and 204: a RAM-routing cell is about 3 to 4 times a plain routing core at 64 bits. ESTIMATE, not measured: with two routing cells per work cell (43 LUT work core) a 1-to-1 RAM layout is about 43 + 2 x 126 = 295 slots per work cell against 103 for plain routing, so about 70 work cells on the Tang's 20,736 slots (about 200 plain); and 4 RAM16SDP4 each limits 64-bit cells to about 160 of the 648 blocks. Most of the cost beyond the 30 is the read/write pointer arithmetic (ALU), which a shift-register delay or a cheaper counter could cut; not tried. The model result (0 to 7 holds per task, 122 bits at most) says a 1-to-1 layout is far more memory than those 12 tasks use, so a small memory (16 bits, or a few cells with more) is the likely fit; a mix with plain routing cells remains the cheaper option. |

So the trigger version is about 40 to 45 percent MORE logic than the timed one (about +38 slots), not less: it still needs a bit counter, and adds the start-flag capture and two more face selects. (My earlier guess that on-demand would be cheaper was wrong.) The word-start flag is assumed to travel with the data (the UniCell cell has a start flag; the work-core probe has a word-start input ws); the model does not route the data's own start flag. ROUTING (tools/hexn_wrgrid_v3.py, v2 untouched; trigger line planned and length-checked, release NOT simulated; data still uses the timed mechanism): the trigger is a one-bit pulse from the late operand's source to the RAM cell; release is pulse + 1 or 2 clocks, which fixes the length the line must have. Over the 12 example tasks (7 holds in the 8-term sum, 1 each in clamp, field extract, 12-bin index, multiply-accumulate and range test): triggers routed OK in 5 cases (2 to 6 wires each, short because the late source is close to the hold), FAILED to find an exact-length line in 6 (range test 1 of 1, 8-term sum 5 of 7; reason not investigated, probably the exact length the line must have is shorter than the distance or has the wrong parity), where the timed release would simply be used. Reading: for a fixed-timing design the timed release is cheaper in logic and in wires and always plannable; the trigger earns its cost only where the timing is not known in advance (branching, waiting on a result, input from the controller); a trigger line is also one more routed wire that must itself arrive on the right clock. Not done: simulating the trigger release, routing the data start flag, repeat started by trigger. |

The flag is a one-bit pulse on its own line to the RAM cell's second input; the cell's word-start select picks that face. A first try, flag leaving the data's own source on the same clock with a line of exactly the same length, FAILED to plan the clamp and the 8-term sum: a work core has ONE output so the flag cannot come out of it, and a pad cell on the west edge has only two routing neighbours, which the data and its fan-out use up. What works: the controller knows every clock (the run is deterministic), so the flag is a pulse put into an EDGE cell (a 'flag pad') at the planned clock and routed by a shortest line to the RAM cell; the planner picks the nearest free edge cell, works out the clock to inject at (arrival clock minus line length, must be 1 or later) and lengthens the line by 2 or 4 if that is too early. Cost: one extra pad and a line per RAM. Results, 12 example tasks, all bit-by-bit correct with the flag line driving the RTL-checked cell: RAMs 0 or 1 per task (clamp and range test 1 each, the 8-term sum 7; the others found detours instead this time), flag wires 4 (clamp), 3 (range test), 14 (8-term sum, 14 of 94 route hops added on top); grids 5x5 to 9x9; clamp now ends at clock 75, 8-term sum at 49. Long-wait chain (waits 38, 76, 114): 4 RAMs, result at clock 149, 13x13. Check that the flag timing matters: injecting the flag 3 clocks late breaks 4 of 6 clamp runs and 1 of 6 range-test runs (a flag ONE clock late loses only bit 0 of the held word, the rest still lands on time, so it is hard to see in the result; that is why I used 3). Assumptions that remain: the controller can put a pulse into an edge cell at an exact clock, one flag pad per RAM, the pad count and edge cells are not limited in the model, and the flag does not pass through any cell that is also a RAM (a second RAM in series gets its own flag). A different design, not modelled: carry the start flag as a second bit on every link (no flag lines or pads, but every routing face is two bits wide, which roughly doubles the routing core's switch). |
| 2 Nov 14:10 | TRIGGER-DRIVEN RAM CELL (Alan 08:57 and 09:02: a routing cell copies data on to the RAM cell, the RAM cell only sees data on its trigger line, that starts the release, and it resets itself when empty until the next pulse; UniCell uses a specific 'high' value sent under specific circumstances, for example a comparator cell; a latched trigger that resets at the end is the easier way). sub/verilog/hexn_ramroute_v6.v, tests/hexn_ramroute_tb_v2.v, tests/vm/test_hexn_ramroute_v6.py (iverilog; passes). Two inputs matter: data face and pulse face (rising edge). ONE pulse line carries both meanings by state: empty cell, pulse = write the 32 bits that start with this clock; word present, pulse = release (read from address 0, 32 bits); when the read ends the cell is empty again (HOLD) or keeps the word for replay on every later pulse (REPEAT). A release pulse during the write is allowed (the read stays behind the write: early stream-out). No timing counter, no delay register, no subtractor, so no 96-clock cap; the release time comes from the pulse. Tested: five words through the same cell one after another with release 40, 5, 20, 31 and 33 clocks after the start (late, early, and the reset between words), and a repeat with two replays; bit 0 is on the face 3 clocks after the release pulse's clock (2 register stages after the edge, same as v5). 6 mutants (release during write blocked, cell never reset, read starts at 1, write ends one early, replay mode resets the word, write starts at 1) all caught. Synthesis count (yosys 0.69, -nowidelut): 77 LUT + 10 ALU = 87 slots, 50 FF, 2 RAM16SDP4 (about 93 counting each block as 3 sites), against 108 / 49 for the timed v5; plain routing core 30 LUT + 24 FF. | 2 Nov 14:50 | JOIN CELL, serial (Alan 09:11: two RAM cells next to each other, each raises a flag when full, the flags go through an AND and back to the release trigger; at the end both signal empty, same again, and the cells are ready for the next round). Prior art found first: the parallel join stage of 1 Nov 06:50 (sub/verilog/join_v1.v, project note claude/join-stage-and-truth-table.md: holds the first operand and fires when the second has arrived, same firing rule); this is that rule on bit streams. sub/verilog/hexn_join_cell_v1.v (two one-word FIFO cells, hexn_ramfifo_v7, plus a serial adder), tests/hexn_join_cell_tb_v1.v, tests/vm/test_hexn_join_cell_v1.py (iverilog; 2 tests pass). Each FIFO raises 'have' when its word has STARTED to arrive (not when full: this fires as early as possible and the read stays behind the write); the AND of the two is the release for both; when a read ends the cell is empty and 'have' drops, so the AND drops by itself, and no second AND for 'empty' is needed because both words are 32 clocks long and finish together (different word lengths would need it). Tested: 12 rounds in a row through the SAME cell with operand B starting 0, 1, 7, 20, 31, 33 or 45 clocks after A and 1, 7, 31, 33, 45 clocks BEFORE A: every sum right; the first result bit comes out 4 clocks after the LATER operand's start every time (fixed latency, whatever the order); an OR in place of the AND fails all 12. Synthesis (yosys 0.69, -nowidelut): 45 LUT + 20 ALU = 65 slots, 35 FF, 4 RAM16SDP4, for both FIFOs and the adder (no routing faces in this test cell; compare v6 87 slots for one routing + FIFO cell). Consequences for the grid: this replaces the whole alignment machinery of the planner (detours, odd-gap delay cores, exact-length lines, flag pads for the release): the cell waits for its own partner. It costs two FIFO cells per two-input work core (about 2 x 40 slots plus the AND) instead of 0 or 1 RAM per task, and it assumes every operand arrives with a write-start pulse on its own line (the producers' 'high'), which is still the open question; a one-input work core needs no join. The AND's two inputs are the cells' flags: in the grid they sit next to each other round the corner cell between the work core's two input cells (the AND can live in that corner cell or in the work core); the flag wires are not routed in any model yet. Not tested: words of different length, a second word arriving during a read, fan-out (one value feeding two cores needs a copy into each core's FIFO), loops (a join in a feedback loop can deadlock if the loop starts empty), place and route. |

If the start bit did NOT double the routing (links stay 30 slots), v7 drops by about 660 slots per task and the small tasks come closer but stay above (diff_abs about 1005 against 729) while the 8-term sum wins (about 1970 against 2816). Reading: the join removes all alignment arithmetic and makes the timing data-driven (a clear win for long chains, loops and unpredictable arrivals) but pays two FIFO cells per two-input core; for short, fixed-timing tasks the aligned plan with a few RAM cells is cheaper. Not done: a mix (join only where alignment is hard), fan-out held in a FIFO per consumer (the model branches the link instead), loops. |

| 2 Nov 16:10 | MIXED MODEL (Alan 09:19: the mix is best; cores with only one data input add another level). tools/hexn_wrgrid_v8.py (v7 untouched), tests/vm/test_hexn_wrgrid_v8.py (2 tests pass). Three kinds of core, chosen per core by the planner from STATIC TIMING (every stream's start clock is known: pads at clock 2, +1 per routing cell, and each core's delay measured by stepping the core itself: serial join +4 after the later start, aligned or one-input +1, shift right by k +k+1, compare / min / max join +35 after the later start): (D) DIRECT, the one-input cores (copy, shift left, shift right): no FIFO, no join, they read the stream off the link; (A) ALIGNED, two-input serial cores (add, sub, and, or, xor) whose operands can be made to arrive together by a SHORT EVEN detour (gap 0, or even and at most 8 clocks): no FIFO; (J) JOIN, everything else (odd gap, longer gap, and compare / min / max always, since the FIFOs are their word store): two FIFO cells and the AND. Results: all 12 example tasks pass the bit-by-bit check on 9x9 (modes per task, in node order: scale-offset DA, abs difference AAJ, clamp JJ, bit-field extract DA, threshold J, 4-input sum AAA, 12-bin index ADJ, bit mix JJA, multiply-accumulate DAJ, midpoint AD, range test JJA, 8-term sum AAAJJJJJ); all three kinds occur; forcing every serial core to join also stays right. Parity matters again: the planner puts inputs on odd rows first so that equal-start operands differ by an even number of clocks and can be aligned by a detour; an odd gap cannot, and there is no 1-clock delay core in this model, so it joins. ESTIMATE (same parts as the 15:30 row: work core 43, routing core 30 or 60 when links carry a start bit and any join exists, join FIFO about 36), 12 tasks: aligned-only v6 about 11,000 slots, join-only v7 about 18,000, mixed v8 about 14,000 (gap limit 4: 14,300; 8: 14,000; 16: 13,800). The mix sits between the two and does NOT beat the aligned plan in total on these small tasks; it wins on none of the small ones; the 8-term sum comes out at 2,864 (v6 2,816, v7 3,020). Two reasons: this mixed model has no RAM hold or 1-clock delay core for the aligned side, so more cores fall to the join than in v6, and a single join in a grid costs every routing cell the 60-slot link when the start bit rides on every link. With the start bit free (links stay 30) the join side is cheaper and the mix would move toward the aligned total. What the mix does give that the aligned plan does not: no controller-timed flag pads, no counter cap, and data-driven timing wherever a join sits. Not done: choosing the cheapest of detour / RAM hold / join per core by cost instead of by gap; a delay core; loops and fan-out into FIFOs. |
