| 2 Nov 20:00 | THE CATALOGUE PLANNER, FIRST VERSION (Alan 09:46: "start the planner, design so it can include those at the same time"). Files: tools/hexn_rtl_probe_v1.py (runs the branch / merge / priority / sequencer RTL in iverilog on a clock-by-clock stimulus), tools/hexn_twins_v1.py (word-level Python twins of those four cores), tools/hexn_wrgrid_v9.py (the planner), tests/vm/test_hexn_twins_v1.py (4 tests), tests/vm/test_hexn_wrgrid_v9.py (5 tests), all pass. DESIGN: v8 had the shape of the cores built in (inputs W and N, one output E). v9 takes the shape from a CATALOGUE: per core kind the input faces, the output faces, the timing class and the cost. Kinds in it: the eleven v8 ops, merge (2 in, 1 out), branch (2 in, 2 out: out1 on the E face, out2 on the S face), priority (3 inputs W N S, 1 out), sequencer (1 in, 1 out), latch (3 in, planned and routed only), mul (listed, refused: not built). A new core kind is a catalogue entry plus a twin; the placing and routing code does not change. TIMING CLASSES: 'static' (output start a fixed number of ticks after the inputs start) and 'dynamic' (merge arbitrate and priority: the order the words leave depends on who arrives first). RULE: a consumer whose two operands must meet (an aligned add / sub / and / or / xor) may only be aligned when BOTH of its inputs have static timing; a dynamic input forces a join (checked: in the task branch_merge_add the add after the arbitrating merge is a join, in or_join the add with a known timing stays aligned). WHAT WAS MEASURED ON THE RTL (probe, then fitted and then checked): merge and priority: a word that finds its cell free leaves 4 ticks after it starts; waiting words leave 34 ticks apart; a cell takes a new word 30 ticks after its word left; branch: result starts at the later start + 38, cells free 30 after; sequencer: result at advance + 2, next advance taken 31 after; join-OR: later start + 4. THE TWINS were first wrong in two places that the RTL comparison caught (the next word was set up while the last bit of the one before was still leaving, which overwrote it; and a word arriving on the very tick the cell frees was dropped). After the fixes each twin equals the RTL tick for tick and word for word on random stimuli (60 merge, 60 priority, 50 branch, 40 sequencer runs, words kept at least 32 ticks apart on one link, as on a real link). The twins are laws fitted to what I measured, not the RTL itself: a corner the random runs did not reach could still differ. THE GRID TASKS (7, on 9 x 9 and 11 x 11): branch_route, merge_two, or_join (add + shl + join-OR merge), seq_add, prio3, branch_merge_add, seq_branch_prio (sequencer feeding a branch feeding a priority): all place, route and simulate to the word-level reference; where the hardware does not fix the order of the words (arbitrate, priority) the reference is the set of words, where it does (sequenced, branch, join-OR) the exact words. A deliberately wrong branch route is caught. COSTS (labelled): measured slot counts for the new cores (branch 154, merge 82, priority 419 full, sequencer 141, latch 3), the v8 estimates for the rest: the seven tasks come to 860 to 1850 slots. needs() lists which kinds a task uses; this is the hook for choosing the bitstream variant. NOT DONE (and each is a real gap): the planner still chooses the mode by a fixed rule, not by cost (detour / RAM hold / join / delay core); latch is not simulated; the stream and multi-word support is only as far as the new cores need it (inputs may be lists of words, loops and deadlock are not checked: a join-OR merge waits for a word a branch may never send); a 4-input priority needs the hex layout; the RAM-cell bands are not modelled; no place and route; mul is not built. |
| 2 Nov 21:00 | BASE DESIGNS (BITSTREAMS), FITTING A DESIGN ONTO THEM, SUGGESTING A NEW ONE, AND THE PROGRAM (Alan 12:46: "for a minimum number of actual bitstreams you would have to supply a set of them, see if the user's design fits onto them, and if not suggest a new base design, a new bitstream, and the programming to place their design onto it"). Files: tools/hexn_wrgrid_v10.py (v9 cloned, kept untouched; adds plan10 with fixed cell types and simulate9 from a grid built from a program), tools/hexn_bases_v1.py, tests/vm/test_hexn_bases_v1.py (4 tests pass; v9 tests still pass). MODEL: a BASE DESIGN is one bitstream = a grid whose work cells have a fixed TYPE (UN unary, AL aligned two-input serial with no FIFO, JN join core, BR branch, MG merge, PR priority, SQ sequencer, LT latch) and whose routing cells are all alike; spare cells are cheap AL cores; the types are spread over the grid by a fixed rule. Loading a bitstream (seconds, state lost) fixes the types; loading a PROGRAM (fast) sets what stays programmable: every routing cell's choice of input for each output, each core's parameters (shift amount, merge mode, branch routes, sequencer values, priority ranks), the pads. FITS means: the planner can place and route the design using only cells of a suitable type (an AL cell only when the operands can be aligned, a JN cell always joins, compare ops need JN) AND the simulation of it equals the word-level reference. PLACE: the cheapest library base that fits, with its program; if none fits, the smallest change to a library base (types swapped, cells or grid added, and the library's other members must still fit), else a new base; or REFUSED with the reason. THE PROGRAM is a text record (cells, routing, pads, and an estimate of its size in bits, about 490 to 560 bits for the small designs tried: 12 bits per routing cell is my own encoding guess), and run_program replays a design from that text alone on the base (the checks do this): so the program is sufficient. LIBRARY (fewest bitstreams): start with one tailored base per design and merge two bases whenever one base with the larger count of each type still fits every design of both and stays inside the slot budget. On my 19 example designs (12 old, 7 new): budget 2000 slots: 16 bitstreams; 3000: 8; 4000: 4 (13 designs on one 7x7 base of 3133 slots, 4 on one of 3470, chain8 alone on a 9x9 of 5078 because it needs 5 join cores, seq_branch_prio alone); 6000: 3; with a budget of about 10,700 or more one 13x13 base of 10,642 slots hosts all 19 (this last figure is from an earlier run of the same code before I made the type order deterministic). The merge is GREEDY: a different merge order gave 2 instead of 3 at 6000 in the earlier run, so these counts are upper bounds on the true minimum, not proven minimums. The default budget of 20,000 slots is the Tang Nano 20K as I recall it (about 20,000 look-up tables): check against the board; and the costs are the v9 mix of measured and estimated numbers, no place and route, so a base that 'fits the budget' here may not place on the chip. THREE DESIGNS TRIED AS A NEW USER on the 4-bitstream library: min(x+y, z) fits an existing base; two branches sharing an input and a merge needs two BR cells: the planner extends the priority base (+1 BR, +1 MG, -2 AL, 3718 slots) and the program replays correctly; a priority feeding a priority is REFUSED because the design is wrong even on an unlimited grid: the second priority's input cell is still full when the next word from the first arrives, so a word is lost (this is the same open point as the RAM cell and merge: a word arriving at a full one-word cell is lost; it shows that chained arbitrating cores need either a queue or back-pressure); and a branch feeding another branch is REFUSED because the second branch waits for a word the first may never send (conditional streams: the checker cannot verify these, and the hardware could wait forever). NOT DONE: the merge search is greedy; the base layout is a fixed spreading rule, not searched; the type set is the eight above (no 'general' cell); no board load time or reload time figures; conditional streams and back-pressure need a decision from Alan. |
| 2 Nov 22:00 | DEAD BRANCHES ARE NORMAL (Alan 14:35: in a flat, unrolled design a branch that feeds a branch is only used occasionally, the second even less). This corrects my 21:00 row, which REFUSED 'a branch feeding a branch' as a possible deadlock. In a flat design a core whose input never comes is simply idle this run; that is the design, not a fault. What does matter, and what the checker now handles: (1) ABSENT STREAMS: the reference (ref9) propagates absence (a two-input core fires only when both words came; a branch with both words sends on the routed output only; join-OR needs both; arbitrate and priority fire for whatever comes); (2) NEVER ALIGN A POSSIBLY-ABSENT OPERAND: the planner marks every stream that may be absent (all branch outputs and anything computed from one) and gives a core with such an operand a JOIN, never an aligned core. Reason (design reasoning, NOT shown by a failing run: I could not build a case where the changed planner chose an aligned core here): an aligned core has no FIFO and no wait, so with one operand missing it would still compute from the one that came; a join core simply never fires. Checked: the rule is in tests/vm/test_hexn_bases_v2.py (the add after a branch output is a join and fits a base with a join core and not one with only aligned cores); (3) STRANDED WORDS: a cell can be left holding a word for a partner that never came (a join core or branch whose other word was never sent). stranded_words() runs the design and lists such cells; place() reports them (a chain of three branches plus two adds leaves words in b2, b3 and one add in most random runs). Such a cell must be CLEARED before the next run, or the stale word pairs with the wrong partner in the next run. THIS NEEDS A CLEAR/RESET INPUT ON THE CELLS (cfgload already does it for the sequencer and priority cores' state, but NOT for their one-word stores, as the priority test found): not built, decision for Alan. | 2 Nov 17:20 | PATTERN ID NUMBERING SCHEME (Alan 16:34: 'propose one, that's fine'; my proposal, taken as accepted unless he changes it). **Type** (16 bits, 4 hex digits) = [15:12] KIND, [11:8] GRID SIZE CODE, [7:0] SEQUENCE within that kind and size. KIND: 0 reserved/test, 1 math, 2 logic, 3 mixed, 4 two RAM layers, 5 RAM bands, F user-created (made by the pattern designer). Size code = the grid side (7, 9, 11 as hex digit 7, 9, B). **Version** (16 bits) starts at 0001 and goes up whenever the generated RTL of that pattern changes in any way that could change behaviour; a new version is a new bitstream, never a silent edit. 0000 0000 = no pattern. Example: math, 9 by 9, first one, first version = 0x19000001... written as type 1900 version 0001. **Why it is a check, not a key:** the library key stays (base, pattern name); the id word only lets the loader confirm what the card holds. **Open:** the sequence numbers are handed out by whichever library registers the pattern, so two separate libraries could give different numbers to different patterns; fixed by one registry if that becomes a problem. Nothing built: `pattern_designer` does not assign ids yet. |

| 2 Nov 21:10 | LOAD FLOW DECIDED BY ALAN (17:59) AND BUILT AS A PLAN: PATTERN DESIGNS ARE REFERENCE FILES; THE PROGRAMMER CHECKS THE CARD, PULLS OR BUILDS THE BITSTREAM, THEN PROGRAM, THEN WEIGHTS. Alan: the pattern creates the design and the programming sits on top; the pattern designs need to be reference files, set in the bitstream; when the programmer reads an ICM it (A) checks the bitstream on the target card, (B) if it is not the right one pulls it, and if it is not available creates it, or at least tells the user it has to be in place before the unit can load; then loads the ICM details, then the weights. **Built:** `plan_load(unit, card, card_pattern_id, library, can_build)` in tools/hexn_unit_file_v1.py (the earlier `load_plan` kept as it was). Outcomes, each tested: already on the card (read from register 11 of spi_bridge_v3) -> go straight on; wrong or none -> pull the bitstream for THIS card from the library; none in the library and a reference file exists and building is allowed -> 'build the bitstream from reference X with the assembler', then load it; otherwise the plan is EMPTY, `problems` says it must be in place first and `needs` names the missing bitstream, and nothing is loaded. The library is per card (a bitstream is per card), each entry holds the pattern id, the reference file name, the bitstream file for that card or none, and the judges it holds. Always refused: a damaged part, a unit naming no base or pattern, an unknown pattern or card, weights for a judge the bitstream does not hold, a unit built for another pattern id. Then wipe, program, weights, ready. 11 tests pass in test_hexn_unit_file_v1.py. **Not built:** the pattern REFERENCE file format as the designer's output with the id in it (hexn_pattern_designer_v1.pattern_file is the stand-in, not yet carrying the id), the assembler step that 'builds', pulling from any real store (the library is a dictionary), and any contact with a board. |

| 3 Nov 16:15 | THE GENERAL WIPE (Alan, 09:50: 'then the general wipe command'). **What it is:** a `clear` input on EVERY cell of a generated pattern; the wipe is `clear` held high. While it is high every state register goes to its idle value: routed registers (words in flight), the cores' counters, carries and state machines, the join / branch / merge FIFO 'have' flags, and (for RAM hold cells) the delay line, which is cleared one address per clock, so **hold `clear` for at least 130 clocks** (the tests use 140). The configuration chain is NOT touched (the program load replaces it). **New RTL (all clones, the v1 / v2 files untouched):** `hexn_unary_core_v2`, `hexn_tile_v3`, `hexn_rcore_flag_v3`, `hexn_aligned_core_v2`, `hexn_tile_al_v2`, `hexn_join_core_v3` (+ `hexn_ramfifo_v9`), `hexn_tile_jn_v2`, `hexn_branch_core_v2`, `hexn_tile_br_v2`, `hexn_merge_core_v2`, `hexn_tile_mg_v2`; `hexn_ramhold_v1` already had it. Generator `tools/hexn_pattern_gen_v7.py`, verify `hexn_pattern_verify_v13` (adds `sim_session`, a multi-phase run: words / idle / clear). Note: the unary core's old `clear` (v1) only zeroed the accumulator; in v2 it is the wipe and also resets the word counter, shift history and outputs. **The hazard it answers, shown in the RTL (`tests/vm/test_hexn_wipe_v1.py`, 6 pass):** a word STRANDED in a cell (one operand arrived, the partner never came). A join (lt), a merge in join-OR mode and a branch each get x alone, then a second run with a new pair of words. WITHOUT the wipe the stale word pairs with the new partner and the answer is different (the control, asserted to differ); WITH the wipe the answer equals the planner's reference for the new words. Also: a wipe before a clean run leaves the output and the start tick unchanged; two wipes in a row are fine; on the lead2 RAM pattern a wipe after all-ones words leaves the hold working with identical timing. **Cost (measured, place and route, wipe-capable RTL):** none worth the name - the mixed pattern J2U2+BRMG_7 yosys 1,981 slots against 2,050 before, placed 2,403 against 2,479 (-6.0% from the estimate), Fmax 246 against 256 MHz estimated; lead2 2,648 / 3,850 against 2,709 / 3,911, 221 against 234 MHz. The reset folds into the flip-flops' own synchronous reset. Records `docs/hex-n/data/pnr/*.wipe.pnr.json` (extra key `generator`). Honest scope: only the cells above; unary 'acc' state and the sequencer / priority / latch (no tiles yet) are not covered; the wipe command is a signal in the RTL - the front-end / programmer command that drives it for 130 clocks is not built; the bench holds `clear` from the testbench, no board run. |
| 3 Nov 16:30 | NOTE: SECOND OPINION ON LOADING FROM THE ESP32 (Alan, 09:50, from Gemini, 'yes i know it can be flaky'; NOTHING built or checked on hardware; follows the 11:15 and 11:25 rows). **The proposal:** the ESP32 is the one SPI master; with the FPGA held in reset (RECONFIG_N low) it writes the bitstream into the Tang's external flash over the flash pins (MSPI), releases reset so the FPGA boots from the flash, and then the FPGA reuses the same MSPI pins as an SPI slave towards the ESP32 for program and data (the Gowin 'dual purpose pin' setting changed to regular I/O). **What I can say:** (1) the pattern - ESP32 programs the flash, then talks to the running design on shared pins - is a sound idea in general and has been done on other boards; (2) one checkable fact from our own toolchain: `yowasp-nextpnr-himbaechel-gowin --vopt help` offers only `sspi_as_gpio` and `i2c_as_gpio`; there is NO option to use the MSPI pins as ordinary I/O in the open-source flow, so step 3 of the proposal would need Gowin's own tool, or a different pin choice; (3) the existing ESP32-to-Tang SPI link (IO5/18/19/23 to Tang pins 73-77) is on ordinary user pins, not the flash pins; (4) every pin name and the claim that RECONFIG_N low 'tri-states the flash' are UNVERIFIED - I have not seen the Nano 20K schematic, and which of MSPI / SSPI / JTAG pins reach a header, and whether the BL616 also drives the flash, are the questions that decide it. **Two other routes to compare when we get to it:** (a) load the SRAM over the configuration port directly (JTAG or the slave-SPI port, which is what the `sspi_as_gpio` option refers to) - no flash writes, no flash wear (flash is rated for a limited number of erases), but the bitstream is lost at power-off; (b) the BL616 firmware route (11:25 row). Decision for later; first job is the Nano 20K schematic and the Gowin configuration guide. |
